Spectral and evolution analysis of composite elastic plates with high contrast
Marin Bužančić, Kirill Cherednichenko, Igor Velčić, Josip Žubrinić

Abstract
We analyse the behaviour of thin composite plates whose material properties vary periodically in-plane and possess a high degree of contrast between the individual components. Starting from the equations of three-dimensional linear elasticity that describe soft inclusions embedded in a relatively stiff thin-plate matrix, we derive the corresponding asymptotically equivalent two-dimensional plate equations. Our approach is based on recent results concerning decomposition of deformations with bounded scaled symmetrised gradients. Using an operator-theoretic approach, we calculate the limit resolvent and analyse the associated limit spectrum and effective evolution equations. We obtain our results under various asymptotic relations between the size of the soft inclusions (equivalently, the period) and the plate thickness as well as under various scaling combinations between the contrast, spectrum, and time. In particular, we demonstrate significant qualitative differences between the asymptotic models obtained in different regimes.



1
Introduction

Derivation of limit models for thin structures in linear and non-linear elasticity is a well-established topic (for example, for the approach via formal asymptotics, see
[
18
,
19
]
and references therein).
As part of recent related activity, there appeared a number of works that derive models of (highly) heterogeneous thin structures by simultaneous homogenisation and dimension reduction,
see
[
6
,
23
,
26
,
27
,
37
]
; for the older work see also
[
8
]
. The present paper aims at making a further contribution to this body of work, by addressing the derivation of effective models for thin plates with high-contrast inclusions in the context of spectral and evolution analysis (see also
[
33
,
34
]
). Simultaneously with the above activity in relation to the analysis of thin structures, the past two decades have seen a growing interest to the analysis of materials with high-contrast inclusions (for early papers on this subject, see
[
39
,
5
,
38
]
) that exhibit frequency-dependent material properties (equivalently, time-nonlocal evolution), which is representative of what one may refer to as “metamaterial” behaviour
[
7
]
. Furthermore, as was recently discussed in
[
16
]
, high contrast in material parameters corresponds to regimes of length-scale interactions, when parts of the medium exhibit resonant response to an external field. As a result, such composites possess macroscopic, or ”effective”, material properties not commonly found in nature, such as
the time non-locality mentioned above (leading to ”memory” effects) or negative refraction, which motivates their use in the context of electromagnetic or acoustic wave propagation for the development of novel devices with cloaking and superlensing properties. Due to the dependence of the effective parameters on frequency, the wave propagation spectrum of these materials has a characteristic band-gap structure (i.e., waves of some frequencies do not propagate through the material, see also
[
3
,
36
]
).

There have been several works dealing with high-contrast inclusions in the context of elasticity: spectral analysis on bounded domains is given in
[
3
]
, in the whole space in
[
40
]
, see also
[
36
]
for treating partial degeneracy (when “directional localisation” takes place), for different models of high-contrast plates (where the starting equations are two-dimensional equations for an “infinitely thin” elastic plate), see
[
33
,
34
]
. In subsequent developments,
[
20
]
deals with high-contrast inclusions with partial degeneracy, when only one of several material constants (namely, the shear modulus) is relatively small,
[
15
]
discusses the limit spectrum of planar elastic frameworks made of rods and filled with a soft material, and
[
11
]
derives an effective model for the case of high-contrast inclusions in the stiff matrix in the context of non-linear elasticity, under an assumption of small loads. In the more recent push towards a quantitative description of metamaterials, elliptic differential equations with high contrast have been analysed in the sense of approximating the associated resolvent with respect to the operator norm (see
[
14
,
16
]
). In the related papers, using the Gelfand transform as a starting point, a new operator family was constructed that approximates the resolvent of the original one and that cannot be obtained directly from the standard limit operator inferred from the earlier qualitative analysis. However, these results are by now obtained only for the whole-space setting and for the particular case of the diffusion operator. In relation to quantifying the resolvent behaviour with respect to the operator norm, we should also mention
[
25
]
, where the dimension reduction for a class of differential operators is carried out in the abstract setting (on a finite domain) and
[
17
]
, where thin infinite elastic plates in moderate contrast are analysed.

In terms of understanding the structure of two-scale limits of partial differential operators with high contrast, we refer to
[
24
]
, where an approach to spectral analysis and its consequences for materials with high-contrast inclusions (including partial degeneracies) on bounded domains is presented, via two-scale convergence. While addressing the description of the limit spectrum only partially,
[
24
]
provided a general framework for the analysis of the limit resolvent, on which new results concerning elasticity and other physically relevant setups could subsequently build. Finally, the subject of homogenisation of stochastic high-contrast media, which naturally follows the analysis of periodic setups, was recently initiated in
[
12
]
and further developed in
[
13
]
.

The main goal of the present paper is to study the behaviour of high-contrast finite plates from the qualitative point of view by dimension reduction from three-dimensional linearised elasticity (including an analysis of the limit spectrum and hyperbolic time evolution), identifying all possible asymptotic regimes, while putting the derivation of the corresponding limit models on the operator-theoretic footing and emphasising the peculiarities of individual scalings. In a future work we intend to carry out a quantitative analysis (in the sense of operator-norm resolvent approximation, in the whole-space setting) of the asymptotic regimes discussed here, using the techniques of
[
16
]
. This will, in most cases, guarantee the convergence of spectra to the spectrum of the corresponding limit operator derived in the present paper. However, for the regime when the period of material oscillations is much smaller than the thickness of the body, spectral pollution is shown to occur for the limit operator
suggested by the present work (i.e., the limiting spectrum has points outside the spectrum of the limit operator). This will make quantitative (i.e., operator-norm based) approaches ever more indispensable for sharp multiscale analysis.

In what follows, we assume that all elastic moduli of the soft component are of the same order (unlike in
[
36
,
20
]
). While we do not apply any additional scaling to either elastic moduli or the mass densities, we do discuss models obtained on different time scales in the context of hyperbolic evolution. Note that this kind of time scaling is indeed sometimes interpreted as a scaling of the mass density (see
[
19
,
31
]
).

Dimension reduction in elasticity always requires a special treatment, due to the degeneracy of the problem as a consequence of the fact that the constant in the Korn’s inequality blows up as the domain thickness goes to zero. From the point of view of spectral analysis, the operator of the associated problem on a rescaled domain of finite thickness has spectrum of order
h^{2},
where
h
is the thickness of the original thin body, with the eigenfunctions describing out-of-plane (“bending”) deformations. This can be physically interpreted as follows: bending deformation can be finite but with small energy, while the magnitude of the (in-plane) stretching deformations is scaled as the magnitude of their energy. Thus for finite plates there are two orders of spectrum: the spectrum of order
h^{2}
and the spectrum of order one. (On an infinite plate there is no natural way to scale the spectrum, see
[
17
]
.) As a result, in the evolution analysis one scales time (or mass density) accordingly, in order to see the evolution of the bending deformation. As a consequence of small, slow in time in-plane forces, in-plane motion is partially quasistatic (see Remark
3.45
below).

Furthermore, we show that in order to see in-plane motion with inertia one has to allow in-plane forces acting in the real (i.e., unscaled) time — to the best of our knowledge, such models have not been discussed in the literature, even in the simplest case of a homogeneous plate, see Remark
3.51
. In a model of this type, when we admit an out-of-plane force (of the same order of magnitude as the in-plane force components), in the limit we obtain a degenerate equation for the out-of-plane motion, since there is no elastic resistance to it (see Remark
3.50
).

The behaviour of plates with high-contrast inclusions also has its peculiarities. As demonstrated in the present work, the effect of the inclusions is not seen on the long timescale under the standard scaling of the corresponding elastic coefficients (i.e., the case of coefficients of order
\varepsilon^{2}
, where
\varepsilon
is the dimensionless characteristic cell size), see Remark
3.45
. In order to obtain standard effects of high-contrast in the long-time evolution, one needs to scale the coefficients differently, see Section
3.4.3
, Section
3.4.4
, Remark
3.54
and Remark
3.56
.

Adopting the operator-theoretic perspective, we start by deriving the limit resolvent in different scaling regimes. To that end, we combine suitable decompositions of deformations that have bounded symmetrised gradients with some special properties of two-scale convergence (see Appendix and the references therein). The limit resolvent is always degenerate (this is caused by the different scaling of (out-of-plane) bending and (in-plane) stretching deformations, as explained above). Moreover, as is usual in derivation of asymptotic models for thin structures, one needs to take care of possible different scalings of the applied loads . These are not necessary for the analysis of the limit spectrum (see Section
3.3
), but are essential if we want to analyse evolution equations with appropriate loads (see Section
3.4
). Here we obtain different models depending on the effective parameter
\delta\in[0,\infty]
, which is the limit ratio between the thickness of the domain
h
and the period
{\varepsilon}
(equivalently, between
h
and the size of the inclusions), where
\varepsilon
tends to zero simultaneously with
h,
as well as on two characteristic orders of spectrum
h^{2}
and one (equivalently, time-scales of order
1/h^{2}
and one). In order to obtain high-contrast effects for “small” spectrum (i.e., on the long time scale in evolution), we also treat a non-standard scaling of the coefficients of high-contrast inclusions. We emphasize that such scaling is different for the case
\delta=0
(small inclusions behave like small plates) and the case
\delta>0
(for the former the the scaling is
\varepsilon^{4},
while for the latter it is
\varepsilon^{2}h^{2}
).

In order to derive the limit spectrum, we employ elements of the approach of
[
39
,
38
]
. Surprisingly, in the regime
\delta=\infty
the limit spectrum does not coincide with the spectrum of the limit operator, which necessitates additional analysis (see Section
3.3.5
and Remark
4.3
). This, however, is not specific for elasticity and would also happen if one carried out simultaneous high-contrast homogenisation and dimension reduction for the diffusion equation.

Suitably adapting the approach of
[
30
]
to dimension reduction in linear elasticity (see Appendix for details), we use our results on resolvent convergence to derive appropriate limit evolution equations. To infer weak convergence of solutions from the weak convergence of initial conditions and loads, we use the fact that the resolvent is the Laplace transform of the evolution operator, while for deriving strong convergence of solutions for all times
t
(from the strong convergence of initial conditions and loads), one needs to show the strong convergence of exponential functions on the basis of the strong convergence of resolvents. Both these implications are analysed in
[
30
]
in an abstract form, which guides our study in the specific context of dimension reduction.

Note that, due to the presence of the discrete spectrum of order
h^{2},
an order one scaling of the operator leads to the limit spectrum covering the whole positive real line (see
[
10
]
). For this reason, in the case when the coefficients on the soft inclusions are of order
\varepsilon^{2}
(where
\varepsilon,
as before, represents the dimensionless cell size) we separate the subspaces of bending and stretching deformations.
This can be done only under additional symmetry assumptions on the elasticity tensor (see Section
3.3.3
). The structure of the limit spectrum for the operator acting in the space of stretching deformations is similar to that of the limit spectrum in high-contrast three-dimensional elasticity (see
[
3
,
40
]
), where the “Zhikov function” describing the limit frequency dispersion is matrix-valued (see Remark
3.34
) in the situations when
\delta<\infty
. For the case of coefficients of order
\varepsilon^{4}
and
\varepsilon^{2}h^{2}
, which correspond to
\delta=0
and
\delta\in(0,\infty)
respectively, the structure of the limit spectrum of order
h^{2}
is again similar to that of standard high-contrast materials (see Section
3.3.4
). However, the Zhikov function is scalar in these cases. The spectral analysis of the case
\delta=\infty
, as already mentioned, requires special treatment (and uses some of ideas presented in
[
2
]
).

In what follows, we first present the results (effective tensors, limit resolvent, limit spectrum, limit evolution equations in different regimes, see Sections
2
,
3
) and then provide the proofs of all statements, see Section
4
. In the Appendix we give some useful claims about decomposition of displacements with bounded scaled symmetric gradients, two-scale convergence, extension operators and operator theoretical approach to high-contrast.

2
Notation and setup

Let
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain and consider the open interval
I=(-1/2,1/2)\subset\mathbb{R}.
Given a small positive number
h>0
, we define a three-dimensional plate

\Omega^{h}:=\omega\times(hI),

whose boundary consists of the lateral surface
\Gamma^{h}:=\partial\omega\times(hI)
and the transverse boundary
\omega\times\partial(hI)
.
We assume that the part of the boundary of
\Omega^{h}
on which the Dirichlet (zero-displacement) boundary condition is set has the form
\Gamma_{\rm D}^{h}:=\gamma_{\rm D}\times(hI)\subset\Gamma^{h},

where
\gamma_{\rm D}\subset\omega
has positive (one-dimensional) measure.

For a vector
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},
we denote by
a_{j},

j=1,\dots,k,
its components, so

\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k}).
Similarly, the entries of a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k},
are referred to as
A_{ij},

i,j=1,\dots,k.
We denote by
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=:(\hat{x},x_{3})
the standard Euclidean coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{3}.
(Note that we reserve the boldface for vectors and matrices representing elastic displacements and their gradients and regular type for coordinate vectors in the corresponding reference domains.) The unit basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
are denoted by
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,k
. Furthermore, for
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we denote by
\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
, the matrix whose
ij
-entry is
a_{i}b_{j}:

\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}=\{a_{i}b_{j}\}_{ij=1}^{k}.

For
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
, by
\boldsymbol{A}^{\top}
we denote its transpose and for the case
k=l
we denote by
\sym\boldsymbol{A}=(\boldsymbol{A}+{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\top})/2
the “symmetrisation” of
\boldsymbol{A}.

For an operator
{\mathcal{A}}
(or a bilinear form
a
) the domain of
{\mathcal{A}}
(respectively
a
) is denoted by
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})
(respectively
{\mathcal{D}}(a)
).

Throughout the paper, we use the notation
\varepsilon_{h}
interchangeably with
\varepsilon,
to emphasize the fact that
\varepsilon
goes to zero simultaneously with
h.

Furthermore, when indicating a function space
X
in the notation for a norm
\|\cdot\|_{X},
we omit the physical domain on which functions in
X
are defined whenever it is clear from the context. For example, we often write
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}},

\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}
instead of
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{k})},

\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{k})},

k=2,3.

Finally, we use the label
C
for all constants present in estimates for functions in various sets. In such cases
C
can be shown to admit some positive value independent of the function being estimated.

2.1
Differential operators of linear elasticity

Consider the reference cell
Y:=[0,1)^{2}.
Let
Y_{0}\subset Y
be an open set with Lipschitz boundary (unless otherwise stated) such that its closure is a subset of the interior of
Y,
and set
Y_{1}=Y\setminus Y_{0}
. We denote by
\chi_{Y_{0}}
the characteristic function of
Y_{0}
and by
\chi_{Y_{1}}
the characteristic function of
Y_{1}
. For any subset of
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we denote by
\chi_{A}
the characteristic function of the set
A
.
The domain
\Omega^{h}
is then divided into two subdomains
\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{0}
and
\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1}
:

\displaystyle\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{0}:=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y+z)\subset\omega}\bigl\{\varepsilon_{h}(Y_{0}+z)\times hI\bigr\},\quad\qquad\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1}:=\Omega^{h}\backslash\Omega_{0}^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}.

Furthermore, we denote

\Omega^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{0}:=\Omega^{1,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{0},\qquad\Omega^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{1}:=\Omega^{1,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1}.

By
\rho^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}
we denote function representing the mass density of the medium. We then define

\rho^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=\rho_{0}\left({\hat{x}}/{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{0}^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}}+\rho_{1}\left({\hat{x}}/{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{1}^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}},\qquad x\in\Omega^{h},

where
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
are periodic positive bounded functions, defined on
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
respectively and extended via periodicity. Namely, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}<\rho_{0}(y)<c_{2}\quad\forall y\in Y_{0},\quad\qquad c_{1}<\rho_{1}(y)<c_{2}\quad\forall y\in Y_{1}.

We also denote
\rho:=\rho_{0}\chi_{Y_{0}}+\rho_{1}\chi_{Y_{1}}
,
\rho^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\rho^{1,{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
We make use of the variational space with zero Dirichlet boundary conditions, defined as:

H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}^{h}}(\Omega^{h},\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):\boldsymbol{v}=0\ \mbox{on}\ \Gamma^{h}_{\rm D}\right\}.

The elastic properties of periodically heterogeneous material are stored in the elasticity tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}
, which is assumed to be of the form:

\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{C}_{1}(y),&y\in Y_{1},\\[3.99994pt]
\mu_{h}^{2}\,\mathbb{C}_{0}(y),&y\in Y_{0}.\end{array}\right.

(1)

where
\mu_{h}
is a parameter that goes to zero simultaneously with
h,\varepsilon_{h}
. The tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}
is then extended to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
via
Y
-periodicity.
The tensors
\mathbb{C}_{0}
and
\mathbb{C}_{1}
are assumed to be uniformly positive definite on symmetric matrices, namely there exists

\nu>0
such that

\nu|\xi|^{2}\leq\mathbb{C}_{0,1}(y)\xi:\xi\leq\nu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\qquad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\ \xi^{\top}=\xi.

(2)

It is well known that for a hyperelastic material the following symmetries hold, which we assume henceforth:

\mathbb{C}_{\alpha,ijkl}=\mathbb{C}_{\alpha,jikl}=\mathbb{C}_{\alpha,klij},\quad i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3\},\qquad\alpha\in\{0,1\}.

The focus of our analysis is the differential operator of linear elasticity
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}^{h}
corresponding to the differential expression

-\bigl(\rho^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)^{-1}\div\left(\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}({\hat{x}}/{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\sym\nabla\right),

It is defined as an unbounded operator in
L^{2}(\Omega^{h},\mathbb{R}^{3})
(where the inner product is weighted
1
1

1

(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\int_{\Omega^{h}}\rho^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{v}
.
by the mass density function
\rho^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}
) with domain

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\varepsilon_{h}})\subset H_{\Gamma_{\rm D}^{h}}^{1}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}),

via the bilinear form

a^{h}_{\varepsilon_{h}}(\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}):=\int\limits_{\Omega^{h}}\mathbb{C}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{U}(x):\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{V}(x)\,dx,\qquad\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{\varepsilon_{h}}^{h})=H^{1}_{\Gamma^{h}_{\rm D}}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})=\mathcal{D}\bigl((\mathcal{A}^{h}_{\varepsilon_{h}})^{1/2}\bigr),

with zero Dirichlet boundary condition on the part
\Gamma_{\rm D}^{h}
of the boundary, which corresponds to the partially clamped case. For a given pair
(h,\varepsilon_{h})
we denote by
\boldsymbol{U}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
any “deformation field” on
\Omega^{h},
i.e., the solution to the integral identity

a^{h}_{\varepsilon_{h}}({\boldsymbol{U}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\boldsymbol{V})=\int_{\Omega^{h}}{\boldsymbol{F}}(x)\cdot{\boldsymbol{V}}(x)dx\qquad\forall{\boldsymbol{V}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma^{h}_{\rm D}}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}),

for some
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

We assume that the following limits for the ratio of the period
{\varepsilon_{h}}
and the thickness
h
exist:

\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=:\delta\in[0,\infty],\qquad\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\frac{h}{{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}}=:\kappa\in[0,\infty]

(3)

and will discuss different asymptotic regimes in terms of the values of
\delta,

\kappa.

The asymptotic regime
\mu_{h}=O(1)
corresponds to the standard case of moderate-contrast (i.e., uniformly elliptic) homogenisation. However, in the present paper we are interested in the “critical” case
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
, which corresponds to high contrast in material coefficients. In addition to this, due to the the dimension reduction in elasticity, higher orders of contrast will also be of interest, namely
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
for
\delta>0
and
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
for
\delta=0,
see the table in Section
2.3
.

Parts of the following assumption will be used occasionally to showcase special situations.

Assumption 2.1
.

(1)

The elasticity tensor is planar symmetric:

\mathbb{C}_{\alpha,ijk3}=0,\mathbb{C}_{\alpha,i333}=0,\quad i,j,k\in\{1,2\},\qquad\alpha\in\{0,1\}.

(2)

The inclusion set
Y_{0}
has a “centre point”
y^{0}=(y^{0}_{1},y^{0}_{2})\in Y_{0}
, such that
Y_{0}
is symmetric with respect to the lines
y_{1}=y^{0}_{1}
,
y_{2}=y^{0}_{2}
. We also assume that the elasticity tensor
y\mapsto\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)
and density
y\mapsto\rho_{0}(y)
are invariant under the corresponding symmetry transformations.

(3)

The inclusion set
Y_{0}
is invariant under the rotations with respect to the angle
\pi/2
around the point
(y^{0}_{1},y^{0}_{2})
. Additionally, assume that the following material symmetries hold:

\mathbb{C}_{0,11ij}=\mathbb{C}_{0,22ij},\quad\mathbb{C}_{0,12kk}=0,\quad i,j,k\in\{1,2,3\},

and that the function
y\mapsto\rho_{0}(y)
is symmetric with respect to the rotation through
\pi/2
around the point
(y^{0}_{1},y^{0}_{2})
.

We define the following subspaces of
L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})
:

\displaystyle L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{V}=(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\in L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3});\quad V_{1},V_{2}\mbox{ are odd w.r.t. }x_{3},\quad V_{3}\mbox{ is even w.r.t. }x_{3}\right\},

\displaystyle L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}):=\left\{\boldsymbol{V}=(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})\in L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3});\quad V_{1},V_{2}\mbox{ are even w.r.t. }x_{3},\quad V_{3}\mbox{ is odd w.r.t. }x_{3}\right\}.

Similarly, we define
L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
L^{2,\rm bend}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
L^{2,\rm memb}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

Remark 2.2
.

Part (1) of Assumption
2.1
is needed to infer that the spaces
L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
L^{2,\rm memb}

(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})
are invariant for the operator
\mathcal{A}^{h}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
Part (2) of the same assumption will additionally be used when we want to infer that the values of the Zhikov function
\beta,
see (
25
), are diagonal matrices, and part (3) will be used in combination with parts (1) and (2) when we want to infer that the
(1,1)
and
(2,2)
entries of the Zhikov function are equal.
Although we do not assume the dependence on the
x_{3}
variable, our analysis can be easily extended to this case (at the expense of obtaining more complex limit equations in some cases). In the case of planar symmetries, a natural assumption would be that the elasticity tensor is even in the
x_{3}
variable.

In order to work in a fixed domain
\Omega:=\Omega^{1}
,
\Gamma:=\Gamma^{1},

\Gamma_{\rm D}:=\Gamma_{\rm D}^{1}
, we apply the change of variables

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}):=(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},h^{-1}x^{h}_{3}),\quad(x_{1}^{h},x_{2}^{h},x^{h}_{3})\in\Omega^{h},

and define
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x):={\boldsymbol{U}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x^{h})
. In the new variables, we will be dealing with a scaled symmetrized gradient and scaled divergence, given by

\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{U}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x^{h})=\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x),\qquad\div\boldsymbol{U}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x^{h})=\tr\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=:\div_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x),

where for a given function
{\boldsymbol{u}}
we use the notation
\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}:=\left(\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}|\,h^{-1}\partial_{x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}\right)
for the gradient scaled “transversally”, and
\tr
denotes the trace of a matrix.
Thus, we are dealing with an operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
(where the inner product is defined with the weight function
\rho^{\varepsilon_{h}}
) whose differential expression and domain are given by

-\bigl(\rho^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)^{-1}\div_{h}\left(\mathbb{C}^{\varepsilon_{h}}({\hat{x}}/{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\sym\nabla_{h}\right),\qquad\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}})\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

respectively. The operator
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
is defined by the form

a_{\varepsilon_{h}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}):=\int\limits_{\Omega}\mathbb{C}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\,dx,\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{D}(a_{\varepsilon_{h}})=H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})=\mathcal{D}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}^{1/2}\bigr).

As in Remark
2.2
, under Assumption
2.1
(1) the spaces
L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
are invariant for the operator
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
We will also say that the operator
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
represents the bilinear form
a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
(a symmetric bilinear form defines a self-adjoint densely defined unbounded operator, see, e.g.,
[
35
]
).
In connection with
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
we define the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
as the restriction of
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
onto the space
L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Additionally, we define the self-adjoint operators
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
in
L^{2}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
whose differential expression and domain are given by

-\rho_{0}^{-1}\div_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\left(\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\right),\qquad\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}})\subset H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),

as the operators represented by the respective bilinear forms

\mathring{a}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{I\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathcal{D}}(\mathring{a}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\bigl(H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\bigr)^{2},

where
H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{k})
stands for the subspace of
H^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{k})
consisting of functions with zero trace on
I\times\partial Y_{0}
.
Finally, we define
\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
as the operator corresponding to the same differential expression as
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
but acting in the space
L^{2,\rm memb}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),
hence representing an appropriate bilinear form

\mathring{\tilde{a}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\cap\left(L^{2,\rm memb}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

2.2
Additional notation

The inner product of
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
(x,y):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}
. For a function
f\in L^{1}(A)
(and similarly for
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{1}(A;\mathbb{R}^{3})
), we denote by

\fint_{A}f:=\frac{1}{|A|}\int_{A}f,

(4)

its mean over
A.
We will also use the shorthand notation

\overline{f}:=\int_{I}f(x_{3})dx_{3},\qquad\langle f\rangle:=\int\limits_{Y}f(y)\,dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\end{array}\right),

(5)

where in the last expression it is assumed that
\boldsymbol{f}
is a (three-component) vector-valued function. In line with (
4
), the notation
\overline{f}
and
\langle f\rangle
is naturally extended to vector-valued functions.

Next, denote by
\iota
the “embedding” operator

\iota:\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\qquad\iota\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}\\[1.99997pt]
a_{21}&a_{22}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}&0\\[1.99997pt]
a_{21}&a_{22}&0\\[1.99997pt]
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

(6)

Similarly, we define an operator
\iota:\mathbb{R}^{3\times 2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}.
We use the same notation for this operator and the operator defined in (
6
),
as it will be clear from the context which of the two embeddings is used in each particular case.
For
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we denote by
\iota_{1}
the mapping

\iota_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\qquad\iota_{1}(\boldsymbol{a})=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}\end{matrix}&a_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore, for
l>0
we define the “scaling” matrix

\pi_{l}:=\begin{pmatrix}l&0&0\\[1.99997pt]
0&l&0\\[1.99997pt]
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

We also define the space
H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
-valued
H^{1}
functions vanishing on
\gamma_{\rm D}
and the space
H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
of scalar
H^{2}
functions vanishing on
\gamma_{\rm D}
together with their first derivatives.

In what follows, we denote by
\mathcal{Y}
the flat unit torus in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by
\mathcal{Y}_{1}
the flat unit torus in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with a hole corresponding to the set
Y_{1},

by
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym}
the space of symmetric matrices,
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\skeww}
the space of skew-symmetric matrices, by
\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n}
the unit matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and by

\delta_{\alpha\beta}
the Kronecker delta function.
Furthermore,
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
,
H^{2}(\mathcal{Y})
denote the spaces of periodic functions in
H^{1}(Y)
,
H^{2}(Y).
Similarly, we denote by

H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y})
the space of functions in
H^{1}(I\times Y)
that are periodic in
y\in Y.

The spaces
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
,
\dot{H}^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y})
are defined to consist of functions in
H^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
,
H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y})
whose mean value is zero.
Similarly, we define the spaces
H^{k}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})
for
k=1,2
.
Note that every function in
H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{k})
can be naturally extended by zero to a function in
H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

The space
C^{k}(\mathcal{Y})

denotes the space of smooth functions on the torus
\mathcal{Y}
that have continuous derivatives up to order
k.
In a similar way we define the space
C^{k}(I\times\mathcal{Y})
. Furthermore,
C^{k}_{00}(I\times Y_{0})
denotes the space of
k
-differentiable functions on
I\times Y_{0}
whose derivatives up to order
k
are zero on
I\times\partial Y_{0}
.
For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the space
C^{k}_{\rm c}(A)
consists of functions with compact support in
A
that have continuous derivatives up to order
k.

For a function
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(I\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
we use the notation
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}
for the “anisotropically scaled” gradient whose third column is obtained from the usual gradient by scaling with
\delta^{-1}:

\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{u}:=\left(\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{u}|\,\delta^{-1}\partial_{x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}\right).

Next, for
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
, we denote

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})=\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{\varphi},

and for
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
\varphi_{2}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we use the notation

\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\boldsymbol{g})(x,y):=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}(\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{y}\varphi_{2}(\hat{x},y)\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}g_{1}(x,y)\\[3.00003pt]
g_{2}(x,y)\end{matrix}\\[10.00002pt]
\begin{matrix}g_{1}(x,y)&g_{2}(x,y)\end{matrix}&g_{3}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}.

(7)

Furthermore, for
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we define

\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})(x,y):=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{w}_{*}(x,y)\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}g_{1}(x)+\partial_{y_{1}}w_{3}(x,y)\\[3.00003pt]
g_{2}(x)+\partial_{y_{2}}w_{3}(x,y)\end{matrix}\\[10.00002pt]
\begin{matrix}g_{1}(x)+\partial_{y_{1}}w_{3}(x,y)&g_{2}(x)+\partial_{y_{2}}w_{3}(x,y)\end{matrix}&g_{3}(x)\end{pmatrix},

where
\boldsymbol{w}_{*}
is defined via (
5
).

For different values of
\delta,\kappa
, we introduce the spaces

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}(\Omega\times Y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\left\{\mathcal{C}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}):\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))\right\},\qquad\delta\in(0,\infty),\\[3.50006pt]
\left\{\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\boldsymbol{g}):\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2})),\varphi_{2}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y})),\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right\},\ \ \ \delta=0,\\[3.50006pt]
\left\{\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g}):\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})),\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right\},\qquad\delta=\infty;\end{cases}

\displaystyle V_{1,\delta,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{2}(\omega),\qquad\delta\in[0,\infty],\ \kappa=\infty,\\[3.50006pt]
H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{2}(\omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\times L^{2}(Y_{0})),\qquad\delta=0,\ \ \kappa\in(0,\infty),\\[3.50006pt]
H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{2}(\omega\times Y),\qquad\delta=0,\ \ \kappa=0;\end{cases}

\displaystyle V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})),\qquad\delta\in(0,\infty),\\[3.50006pt]
L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})),\qquad\delta=\infty,\\[3.50006pt]
L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\times L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),\qquad\delta=0;\end{cases}

\displaystyle H_{\delta,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\delta\in(0,\infty],\ \ \kappa=\infty,\\[3.50006pt]
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\delta=0,\ \ \kappa=\infty,\\[3.50006pt]
\left(L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)\times L^{2}(\omega\times Y),\qquad\delta=0,\ \ \kappa\in[0,\infty),\end{cases}

where functions defined on
\omega
are assumed to be constant across the plate whenever they are considered in
\Omega.
(In other words,
L^{2}(\omega\times Y)
is treated as naturally embedded in
L^{2}(\Omega\times Y).
)
We denote by
P_{\delta,\kappa}
and
P^{0}
the orthogonal projections
P_{\delta,\kappa}:L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})\to H_{\delta,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y)
and
P^{0}:L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)\to L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),
respectively.
The mappings

L^{2}(\Omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\ni u(x)+\mathring{u}(x,y)\mapsto u(x)\in L^{2}(\Omega)

and

L^{2}(\Omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\ni u(x)+\mathring{u}(x,y)\mapsto\mathring{u}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0})

are labelled by
S_{1}
and
S_{2},
respectively.
For Hilbert spaces
V,W
and a linear operator
\mathcal{A}:V\to W,
we denote by
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})\subset W
its range, and for a linear operator
\mathcal{A}:V\to V,
we denote by
\sigma(\mathcal{A})
its spectrum. Furthermore,
\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathcal{A})
and
\sigma_{\rm disc}(\mathcal{A})
denote the essential and discrete spectrum of
\mathcal{A},
respectively. Throughout, we denote by
\mathcal{I}
the identity operator on the appropriate ambient space.

For the definition of two-scale convergence, the related notation and properties of importance for our analysis, we refer the reader to Appendix (for the basic properties and introduction, see also
[
1
]
). Finally, for a Hilbert space
V
, we denote by
V^{*}
its dual, and
\rightharpoonup,

\to
denote, respectively, the weak and strong convergence.

3
Main results

3.1
Effective elasticity tensors

In this section we will define limit elasticity tensors that will appear in various regimes. For
\delta\in(0,\infty),
we define a symmetric tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}
via

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{A}&,\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=\\[3.50006pt]
&=\min_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}(y)\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right]:\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right]\,dy\,dx_{3},\\[3.50006pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\end{split}

(8)

as well as tensors
\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta},

\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend}_{\delta}
via

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}:=\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0}),\quad\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\quad\qquad\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B}:=\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B}),\quad\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.\end{split}

Remark 3.1
.

Under an additional assumption on the material symmetries, namely Assumption
2.1
(1), the tensor
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
can be written as the orthogonal direct sum

\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm hom}=\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm bend},

in the sense that

\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}&0\\[3.50006pt]
0&\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm bend}\end{bmatrix},

i.e.,

\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}+\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm bend}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B},\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.

For the case
\delta=0
the following tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}
will be important (in this case we assume that
Y_{0}
is of class
C^{1,1}
):

\begin{split}&\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=\\[3.50006pt]
&=\min\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}(y)\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\boldsymbol{g})(x_{3},y)\right]:\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\boldsymbol{g})(x_{3},y)\right]\,dy\,dx_{3},\\[-2.5pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\end{split}

(9)

where the minimum is taken over
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\varphi_{2}\in\dot{H}^{2}(\mathcal{Y})
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(I\times Y,\mathbb{R}^{3}).
Note that in (
9
) the definition (
7
) of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is used with
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},

\varphi_{2},

\boldsymbol{g}
independent of
\hat{x}
. Furthermore, we define a tensor function
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm red}(y),

y\in Y_{0},
by the formula

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm red}(y)(\boldsymbol{A}&,\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=\\[3.50006pt]
&=\min_{\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(I;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\int\limits_{I}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\iota_{1}(\boldsymbol{g}(x_{3}))\right]:\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\iota_{1}(\boldsymbol{g}(x_{3}))\right]\,dx_{3},\\[0.0pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.\end{split}

(10)

In addition, for
\alpha=0,1

we define a tensor-valued function
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}^{\rm r}(y)
,
y\in Y
, via the formula

\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}^{\rm r}(y)\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}=\min_{\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}(y)[\iota(\boldsymbol{A})+\iota_{1}(\boldsymbol{d})]:[\iota(\boldsymbol{A})+\iota_{1}(\boldsymbol{d})],\qquad\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\quad y\in Y_{\alpha}.

Remark 3.2
.

It is easily seen that for a
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},

\varphi_{2},

\boldsymbol{g}
on which the minimum in (
9
) is attained, one has
\boldsymbol{g}(x_{3},y)=\boldsymbol{g}_{0}(y)+x_{3}\boldsymbol{g}_{1}(y),
for some
\boldsymbol{g}_{0},

\boldsymbol{g}_{1}\in L^{2}(Y,{\mathbb{R}}^{3}).
It follows that

\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm memb,r}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}+\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm bend,r}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B},\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm memb,r}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}^{r}(y)[\boldsymbol{A}+\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}(y)]:[\boldsymbol{A}+\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}(y)]\,dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm bend,r}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\varphi\in\dot{H}^{2}(\mathcal{Y})}\frac{1}{12}\int_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}^{r}(y)[\boldsymbol{B}+\nabla_{y}^{2}\varphi(y)]:[\boldsymbol{B}+\nabla_{y}^{2}\varphi(y)]\,dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.

Similarly to the above, it is seen that the minimum in (
10
) is attained on the vector fields of the form
\boldsymbol{g}(x_{3})=\boldsymbol{g}_{0}+x_{3}\boldsymbol{g}_{1},
where
\boldsymbol{g}_{0},

\boldsymbol{g}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}.
Furthermore, we have the following decomposition:

\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm red}(y)(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm memb,r}(y)\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}+\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}(y)\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B},\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\qquad y\in Y_{0},

where

\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm memb,r}(y)\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}:=\mathbb{C}_{0}^{r}(y)\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A},\quad\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}(y)\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B}:=\frac{1}{12}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm r}(y)\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B},\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\qquad y\in Y_{0}.

For the case
\delta=\infty
, a tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}
will be important, which is defined by

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}(\boldsymbol{A}&,\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):=\\[3.00003pt]
&=\min\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}(y)\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})(x_{3},y)\right]:\left[\iota\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)+\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})(x_{3},y)\right]\,dy\,dx_{3},\\[-3.00003pt]
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},\end{split}

(11)

where the minimum is taken over
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(I;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(I;\mathbb{R}^{3}).
(As in the case of the expression
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
entering (
9
), for the expression
\mathcal{C}_{\infty}
in (
11
) we take the functions
\boldsymbol{w},

\boldsymbol{g}
to be independent of
\hat{x}.
)

Remark 3.3
.

It is easily seen that the minimum in (
11
) is attained on
\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{g}_{0}+x_{3}\boldsymbol{g}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{w}_{0}(y)+x_{3}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}(y),
for some
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{0},

{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},

\boldsymbol{w}_{0},

\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\in L^{2}(Y;{\mathbb{R}}^{3}).
It follows that

\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})=\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb,h}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}+\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend,h}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B},\qquad\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb,h}\boldsymbol{A}:\boldsymbol{A}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{w}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R}^{3}),\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}(y)[\boldsymbol{A}+\mathcal{C}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})]:[\boldsymbol{A}+\mathcal{C}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})]\,dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym},

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend,h}\boldsymbol{B}:\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\boldsymbol{w}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{Y},\mathbb{R}^{3}),\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{12}\int_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}(y)[\boldsymbol{B}+\mathcal{C}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})]:[\boldsymbol{B}+\mathcal{C}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{g})]\,dy,\qquad\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.

The following proposition is proved in Section
4.1
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The tensor
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}
(and consequently the tensors
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
,
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm bend}
as well) is bounded and coercive, i.e., there exists
\nu>0
such that

\nu\bigl(|\boldsymbol{A}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})\leq\nu^{-1}\bigl(|\boldsymbol{A}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}\bigr)\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\sym}.

Analogous claims are valid for tensors
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,r}
,
\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom,h}
,
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm red}
(and consequently tensors
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm memb,r}
,
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm bend,r}
,
\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb,h}
,
\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend,h}
,
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm memb,r}
,
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}
).

3.2
Limit resolvent equations

Our starting point is the following resolvent formulation. For
\tau,\lambda>0
and a given
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
find
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that the following variational formulation holds:

\frac{1}{h^{\tau}}\int\limits_{\Omega}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{v}\,dx+\lambda\int\limits_{\Omega}\rho^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,dx=\int\limits_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\,dx\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(12)

We derive the limit resolvent equation, as
h\to 0,
depending on various assumptions about the parameter
\delta=h/{\varepsilon}_{h},
the exponent
\tau,
and the scaling of the load density
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
. In Section
3.3
we discuss implications of these results for the limit spectrum and evolution equations. Different scalings of the operator will, in particular, yield different scalings of the spectrum and the time variable (or mass density) in the evolution problems.
Note that the load density scaling will also depend on the asymptotic regime considered.

It is standard in the theory of plates that one discusses limit equations (both static and dynamic) depending on an appropriate scaling of the external loads. Furthermore, we will see that the limit resolvent equation is always degenerate in some sense. From the mathematical point of view, this is a consequence of the fact that for thin domains the constant in Korn’s inequality blows up and by further analysis one can see that this implies that the out-of-plane and in-plane components of the solution are scaled differently in the limit problem. From the physical point of view, it is much easier (i.e., energetically more convenient) for the plate to bend then to stretch. As a result, bending and membrane waves propagate through the medium on different time scales. The effect of high-contrast is also non-negligible, yielding different behaviour depending on the asymptotic regime: the small elastic inclusions behave like three-dimensional objects (regime
h\sim{\varepsilon_{h}}
) or like small thin plates (regime
h\ll{\varepsilon_{h}}
). We next present our main results.

3.2.1
Asymptotic regime
h\sim\varepsilon_{h}

A. “Bending” scaling:
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},\tau=2

The following proposition provides an appropriate compactness result, namely a bound on the sequence of energies for a fixed value of
\delta,
see (
3
), and its consequences in terms of two-scale convergence.

Proposition 3.5
.

Consider a sequence
\{(h,{\varepsilon}_{h})\}
such that
\delta=\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon}_{h}\in(0,\infty)
, and suppose that
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=2
. The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
, such that for any sequence

(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
to the resolvent problem (
12
) one has

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}<C\bigl\|\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\qquad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then there exist functions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathcal{C}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathfrak{C}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}_{\delta}(\Omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
, such that for a subsequence, which we continue labelling with
{\varepsilon_{h}}
, one has

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\
\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}\,}\,}\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},\\
h^{-1}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y),\\
h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\bigl(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\
{\varepsilon_{h}}h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y),\end{split}

(13)

where
{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}
stands for the “dimension-reduction two-scale convergence” defined in Appendix
A.2
.

3.

If, additionally to 2, one assumes that

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{b})+\|\mathfrak{b}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where the form
a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}
is defined in (
17
), then one has the strong two-scale convergence (cf. Appendix
A.2
)

\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},

with
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
(for the definition of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
see (
16
) below).

Remark 3.6
.

It can be seen from the proof of Proposition
3.5
that the assumption in its third statement is equivalent to the convergence

\displaystyle h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\chi_{\Omega^{1}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\iota\bigl(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)\chi_{I\times Y_{1}}+\mathcal{C}(x,y)\chi_{I\times Y_{1}},

\displaystyle{\varepsilon_{h}}{h}^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}0,

\displaystyle\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top}.

Here
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
and
\mathcal{C}(x,\cdot)
solves the minimization problem (
8
) with
\boldsymbol{A}=\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})
and
\boldsymbol{B}=\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})
.
The analogous claim is valid in all other regimes. As we do not explicitly use it in what follows, we shall omit it.

The following theorem provides the limit resolvent equation. It can be seen that the limit equations do not couple
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
. This is not usual in high-contrast analysis and is a consequence of setting
\tau=2
. This case is thus less interesting and we shall omit its analysis in other regimes (
\delta=0
and
\delta=\infty
). However, we will study it here, as it resembles the standard model of a moderate-contrast plate (and so the corresponding evolution is obtained on a longer time scale).

Theorem 3.7
.

Under the notation of Proposition
3.5
, suppose that
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=2
, and consider a sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}
of load densities such that

\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(14)

Then the sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of
(
13
) to the unique solution of the following problem: Determine
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
, such that

\begin{split}&\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right):\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\langle{\rho}\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}){\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\\[3.50006pt]
&\hskip 10.0pt=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\bigl(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x}),\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\bigr){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,d\hat{x}-\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{x_{3}\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega,\mathbb{R}^{2}),\ {\theta_{3}}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega),\\[3.50006pt]
&\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y):\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}\\[3.50006pt]
&\hskip 10.0pt=\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\cdot\bigl(\mathring{\xi}_{1}(x_{3},y),\mathring{\xi}_{2}(x_{3},y),0\bigr)^{\top}\,dy\,dx_{3}\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.\end{split}

(15)

If additionally one assumes the strong two-scale convergence in (
14
), then one has

\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{2},\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top}.

Remark 3.8
.

Under Assumption
2.1
(1) the first identity in (
15
) uncouples into two independent identities (see Remark
3.1
)

\begin{split}&\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega,\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
&\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend}_{\delta}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}):\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\Omega}\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\,{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\\
&\hskip 90.0pt=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})\,{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}-\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{x_{3}\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall{\theta_{3}}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega).\end{split}

In connection with the limit problem, we consider a self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}
defined on the
\langle\rho\rangle
-weighted space
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{2}(\omega)
and corresponding to the differential expression
2
2

2

The repeated divergence
\div_{\hat{x}}\div_{\hat{x}}
is applied to matrices and corresponds to the usual divergence applied row-wise and column-wise sequentially.

\langle\rho\rangle^{-1}\left(-\div_{\hat{x}},\div_{\hat{x}}\div_{\hat{x}}\right)\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}},\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\right).

More precisely, the operator
\mathcal{A}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}
is defined via the bilinear form

\begin{split}a^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\bigl((\boldsymbol{u},v),(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\xi)\bigr):=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}v(\hat{x})\right):\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\xi(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x},\\
a^{\rm hom}_{\delta}:\left(H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

We also make use of the following observation.
Plugging
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{3}=0
into the first equation in (
15
) and using linearity, we decompose
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
are solutions to the integral identities

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

(16)

\displaystyle=-\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(0,\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right):\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x}),0\right)\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}=\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\bigr\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Notice that the in-plane deformation
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
can be calculated from the out-of-plane deformation
\mathfrak{b}
by solving the first identity alone. It is easily seen that the solutions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}
satisfy the estimates

\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\bigl\|\nabla^{2}_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\omega)},\quad\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\bigl\|\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}.

The first identity in (
16
)
defines a bounded linear operator
\mathcal{A}^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}};\mathfrak{b}}_{\delta}:H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)\to H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
by the formula

\mathcal{A}^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}};\mathfrak{b}}_{\delta}\mathfrak{b}:=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
.
Furthermore, the bilinear form
a^{\mathfrak{b}}_{\delta}:\bigl(H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle a^{\mathfrak{b}}_{\delta}(\mathfrak{b},\theta)

\displaystyle:=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}};\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right):\left(0,\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\theta(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x}

(17)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}};\mathfrak{b}}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right):\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}};\theta}\theta,\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\theta(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x},

defines positive definite (as a consequence of Proposition
3.4
) self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\omega),
which we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}_{\delta}
.
The first identity in (
15
) can now be written as follows:

\displaystyle a^{\mathfrak{b}}_{\delta}(\mathfrak{b},\theta)+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}},0\right):\left(0,\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\theta(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x}

(18)

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{f_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}-\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{x_{3}\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\nabla_{\hat{x}}\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=:\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{f})(\theta),\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\qquad\forall\theta\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega).

Notice that for
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
the
right-hand side of (
18
) can be interpreted as an element of
(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega))^{*},
which we denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{f}).
This reveals the resolvent structure of the limit problem (
15
).

B. “Membrane” scaling:
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},\tau=0

To formulate the convergence result for the present scaling, we consider a non-negative self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}
defined by the bilinear form

\begin{split}a_{\delta,\infty}((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{b}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(\boldsymbol{\theta},\varphi)+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}})&:=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\\
&+\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y):\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x,y)\,dy\,dx,\\[3.00003pt]
a_{\delta,\infty}:&\ \left(V_{1,\delta,\infty}(\omega\times Y)+V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

(19)

Notice that
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}
is degenerate with an infinite-dimensional kernel:

\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}(0,0,v)=0\quad\ \forall v\in L^{2}(\omega).

However, the restriction of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}
on the space
H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y)\cap L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
does not exhibit such degeneracies (under Assumption
2.1
(1)).

The following proposition gives a suitable compactness result, similar to Proposition
3.5
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Consider a sequence
\{(h,{\varepsilon}_{h})\}
such that
\delta=\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon}_{h}\in(0,\infty),

and suppose that
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0
. The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h,
such that for any sequence

(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})

and the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,

then
there exist functions
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,\delta,\infty}(\omega\times Y)
,
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}(\Omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
such that, up to extracting a subsequence, one has

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{*}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\\
\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\\
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y),\\
\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\
{\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y).\end{split}

(20)

3.

If, additionally to 2, one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\delta,\infty}\bigl((\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)+\bigl\|(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
a_{\delta,\infty}
is defined in (
19
), then the strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}

holds.

The following theorem provides the limit resolvent equation.

Theorem 3.10
.

Under the notation of Proposition
3.9
, suppose that
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0,
and consider a sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}
of load densities such that

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(21)

Then the sequence of solutions
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}
to the resolvent problems (
12
) converges in the sense of (
20
)
to the unique solution of the following problem: Determine
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,\delta,\infty}(\omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
, such that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\langle{\rho}\rangle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

(22)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{u}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})=\lambda^{-1}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})\quad\textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.

\displaystyle\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y):\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}

\displaystyle+\lambda\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}

\displaystyle+\lambda\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.

If, additionally, one assumes the strong two-scale convergence in (
21
), then one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y).

Corollary 3.11
.

Under Assumption
2.1
(1) and provided
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, in addition to the convergences in Proposition
3.9
one has

\tilde{u}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{H^{1}}0,

and thus
\mathfrak{b}=0
in the limit equations (
22
).

Remark 3.12
.

The limit system (
22
) can be written as a resolvent problem on the space
H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y)
, as follows:
3
3

3

Notice that this requires to take the inner product with the weight function
\langle\rho\rangle\chi_{Y_{1}}+\rho_{0}\chi_{Y_{0}}.

\left(\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)\boldsymbol{u}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P_{\delta,\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{f},\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

which is the usual resolvent structure for the limit problem in the high-contrast setting (see
[
30
]
and Section
A.4
).

Next, the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
on the space
H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y)\cap L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined via the form
\tilde{a}_{\delta}
given by the expression in (
19
) with a different domain:

\tilde{a}_{\delta}:\left(H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\{0\}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{2,\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\cap\left(L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

This operator can only be defined under Assumption
2.1
(1).

In relation to the limit problem, we also define the following operators. The operator
\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta},
referred to as
the Bloch operator, corresponds to the differential expression
4
4

4

The differential expression
\widetilde{\div}_{2,\delta}
stands for
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\cdot
(applied row-wise), i.e., it is in the same relation to the gradient
\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}
as the standard divergence is in the relation to the standard gradient.

-(\rho_{0})^{-1}\widetilde{\div}_{2,\delta}\bigl(\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\bigr),

and is defined via the bilinear form

\displaystyle a_{00,\delta}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=\int\limits_{I\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{u}(x_{3},y):\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{v}(x_{3},y)\,dx_{3}\,dy,\qquad a_{00,\delta}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

Similarly to the way the form
\tilde{a}_{\delta}
and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
were defined by restricting the form
a_{\delta,\infty}
, we define a form

\tilde{a}_{00,\delta}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\cap\left(L^{2,\rm memb}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R},

and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
by restricting the form
a_{00,\delta}.

We also define a positive self-adjoint
operator
\mathcal{A}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
on
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
corresponding to the differential expression

-\langle\rho\rangle^{-1}\div_{\hat{x}}\bigl(\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\bigr),

as the one defined (on an appropriate weighted
L^{2}
space) by the bilinear form

\begin{split}a^{\rm memb}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})&:=\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{v}(\hat{x})d\hat{x},\qquad a^{\rm memb}_{\delta}:\bigl(H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

(23)

In order to simplify the system (
22
), one is led to first solve the equation (where we replace
\lambda
with
-\lambda
)

(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}-\lambda\mathcal{I})\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}=\lambda\bigl(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top}+(\rho_{0})^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}(\hat{x},\cdot),

where the variable
\hat{x}
is treated as a parameter (see, e.g.,
[
40
]
).When
\boldsymbol{f}|_{Y_{0}}=0
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}_{+},
the equation (
23
) can be solved via the more basic problems

(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}-\lambda\mathcal{I})\boldsymbol{b}^{\lambda}_{i}=\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\qquad i=1,2,3.

The following matrix-valued function
\beta_{\delta}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
will be important for characterizing the spectrum of the limit operator:

\bigl(\beta_{\delta}(\lambda)\bigr)_{ij}=\lambda\langle\rho\rangle\delta_{ij}+\lambda^{2}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(b^{\lambda}_{i})_{j}}\bigr\rangle,\qquad i,j=1,2,3.

We refer to
\beta_{\delta}
as “Zhikov function”, to acknowledge its scalar version appearing in
[
39
]
. Its significance will be clear in the next section.
We can obtain an alternative representation of the Zhikov function as follows.

First, separate the spectrum of
{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
into two parts:

\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})=\{{\eta}_{1},{\eta}_{2},...\}\cup\{{\alpha}_{1},{\alpha}_{2},...\},

(24)

where the second subset corresponds to eigenvalues with all associated eigenfunctions having zero
\rho_{0}
-weighted mean
in all components. In each of the two subsets the eigenvalues are assumed to be arranged in the ascending order.
Next, denote by
({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the set of orthonormal eigenfunctions corresponding to the eigenvalues from the set
\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},...\}
in (
24
), repeating every eigenvalue according to its multiplicity. Using the spectral decomposition, one obtains

\bigl(\beta_{\delta}(\lambda)\bigr)_{ij}=\lambda\langle\rho\rangle\delta_{ij}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{{\eta}_{n}-\lambda}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{({\varphi}_{n})_{i}}\bigr\rangle\cdot\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{({\varphi}_{n})_{j}}\bigr\rangle,\qquad i,j=1,2,3.

(25)

Under Assumption
2.1
(1), one is actually only interested in the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
. We can then define a version of the Zhikov function, denoted by
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
and taking values in
