\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
(dropping the third row and the third column of
\beta_{\delta},
which necessarily vanish as a consequence of symmetries) as the one associated with this operator. Eliminating those values
\eta_{n}
and
\alpha_{n}
in (
24
) whose eigenfunctions belong to the subspace
L^{2,\rm bend}(I\times Y_{0},\mathbb{R}^{3}),
we write

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})=\{\tilde{\eta}_{1},\tilde{\eta}_{2},\dots\}\cup\{\tilde{\alpha}_{1},\tilde{\alpha}_{2},\dots\}.

Here, similarly to the above, the eigenvalues in the second set
are those whose all eigenfunctions have zero weighted mean in all of their components. (Note that due to symmetry the third component has zero weighted mean automatically.)
We use the notation
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}
for the set of such eigenvalues:

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}=\{\tilde{\alpha}_{1},\tilde{\alpha}_{2},\dots\}.

Analogously to (
25
), we can write a formula for the function
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
. Notice, in particular, that

\bigl(\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\bigr)_{\alpha\beta}=\bigl(\beta_{\delta}\bigr)_{\alpha\beta},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,2.

(26)

C. “Strong high-contrast bending” scaling:
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h,

\tau=2

As was shown above, in the case
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},

\tau=2
one does not see effects of high-contrast inclusions in the limit equations (i.e the limit equations are not coupled). Here we consider an asymptotic regime of higher contrast, where the limit equations are coupled. Proposition
3.13
below provides the relevant compactness result. Before proceeding to its statement, we introduce some auxiliary objects.

In order to analyse the spectral problem, we will require a positive self-adjoint operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
on the Hilbert space
\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0},\mathbb{R}^{3}),
as the one defined by the bilinear form

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{a}_{\delta}\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(0,0,\theta)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{b}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\theta)+\int_{\omega}a_{00,\delta}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\,d\hat{x},

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\delta}:\left(\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+V_{2,\delta}(\omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

(27)

We also define a scalar Zhikov function
\hat{\beta}_{\delta}
associated with this problem. Namely, we eliminate the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}
all of whose eigenfunctions have zero weighted mean in the third component and set

\hat{\beta}_{\delta}:=\beta_{\delta,33}.

(28)

We also define
\hat{\sigma}(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta})
as the set of the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}
all of whose eigenfunctions have zero weighted mean in the third component.

Proposition 3.13
.

Consider a sequence
\{(h,{\varepsilon}_{h})\}
such that
\delta=\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon}_{h}\in(0,\infty),

and suppose that
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2
.
The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
,
such that for any sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\qquad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then
there exist functions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\delta}(\Omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
, such that (up to a subsequence):

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\qquad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\[1.49994pt]
\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\bigl(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},\\[3.00003pt]
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y),\\[3.00003pt]
h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\[3.00003pt]
{\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y).\end{split}

(29)

3.

If, additionally to 2, one assumes that

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=\hat{a}_{\delta}((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})+\|(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
\hat{a}_{\delta}
is defined in (
27
), then one has the strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}.

The following theorem describes the limit resolvent equation.

Theorem 3.14
.

Suppose that
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h,
and
\tau=2,
and consider a sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}
of load densities such that

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(30)

Then the sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of (
29
) to the unique solution of the following problem: Determine
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
such that

\begin{split}&\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\right):\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x}),\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\right)\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl(\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{u}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})\bigr){\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\\[3.50006pt]
&\hskip 150.0pt=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x}){\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\quad\qquad\forall{\theta_{3}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega),\\[3.50006pt]
&\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y):\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\xi}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}+\lambda\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{3}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}\\[3.50006pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\lambda\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}\\[3.99994pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x_{3},y)\,dy\,dx_{3}\quad\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.\end{split}

(31)

In the case when the strong two-scale convergence holds in (
30
), one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Remark 3.15
.

The limit problem (
31
) can again be written as the resolvent problem on
\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
:

(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}+\lambda\mathcal{I})\boldsymbol{u}=\bigl(S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{1},S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{2},(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{3}\bigr)^{\top},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}

which is again the general desired structure.

Remark 3.16
.

Under Assumption
2.1
(1), the first equation in (
31
) decouples from the second (see Remark
3.1
) and one has

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=0,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend}_{\delta}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}):\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl(\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{u}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x})\bigr){\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{3}}\rangle(\hat{x}){\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall{\theta_{3}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega).

In the following sections we will analyse only those two cases for each regime when there is a coupling between the deformations inside and outside the inclusions.

3.2.2
Asymptotic regime
h\ll\varepsilon_{h}:
“very thin” plate

Throughout this section, we additionally assume that the set
Y_{0}
has
C^{1,1}
boundary, to ensure the validity of
some auxiliary extension results, see Appendix
A.3
.

A. “Membrane” scaling:
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},\tau=0

Similarly to Part B of Section
3.2.1
, where the membrane scaling is discussed for the regime
h\sim\varepsilon_{h},
we define the following objects using the limit resolvent in Theorem
3.19
below (expression (
34
) for the limit resolvent) :

•

For each
\kappa\in[0,\infty]
, a form
a_{0,\kappa}:\bigl(V_{1,0,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)+V_{2,0}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}_{0,\kappa}
on the space
V_{0,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y),
analogous to
a_{\delta,\infty}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}
of Part B, Section
3.2.1
. In this way the limit problem (
34
) can be written in the form

(\mathcal{A}_{0,\kappa}+\lambda\mathcal{I})\boldsymbol{u}=P_{\delta,\kappa}\boldsymbol{f},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}});

•

A form

\tilde{a}_{0}:\bigl(H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})+L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2}))\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
on
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})

(analogous to
\tilde{a}_{\delta}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
) — these are correctly defined under Assumption
2.1
(1);

•

A bilinear form

a_{0}^{\rm memb}:\bigl(H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated
operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\rm memb}
on
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})

(analogous to
a_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
);

•

A bilinear form

\tilde{a}_{00,0}:\bigl(H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0},;\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0}
on
L^{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})

(analogous to
\tilde{a}_{00,\delta}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
);

•

Functions
\beta_{0}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{0},
by analogy with
\beta_{\delta}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta};

•

A set
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0})^{\prime},
by analogy with
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}.

We do not write these definitions explicitly, since we assume their definition is natural. The following proposition provides a compactness result for solutions to (
12
).

Proposition 3.17
.

Suppose that
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0
. The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
,
such that for a sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\quad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then
there exist
({\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,0,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)
,
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\in L^{2}(\omega;\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
\varphi_{2}\in L^{2}(\omega;\dot{H}^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,0}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}_{|\Omega\times Y_{1}}=0
such that (up to a subsequence)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{*}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{3}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\begin{cases}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),&\quad\kappa=\infty,\\[2.5pt]
\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y),&\quad\kappa\in[0,\infty),\end{cases}\end{split}

(32)

\begin{split}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\begin{cases}\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\varphi_{2},\boldsymbol{g})(x,y),&\quad\kappa=\infty,\\[2.5pt]
\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\kappa\mathfrak{b},\boldsymbol{g})(x,y),&\quad\kappa\in(0,\infty),\\[2.5pt]
\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},0,\boldsymbol{g})(x,y),&\quad\kappa=0,\end{cases}\\[1.99997pt]
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(\hat{x},y),\\[1.99997pt]
{\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*},0,\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}})(x,y).\end{split}

3.

If, additionally to 2, one assumes that

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{0,\kappa}((\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})+\|(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where the form
a_{0,\kappa}
is defined above, then one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Remark 3.18
.

In the regimes
h\sim{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
and
h\ll{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
we are not able to identify the functions
\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)
and
\mathring{u}_{3}
separately on
\omega\times Y_{0}
(in the following theorem). However, we are able to identify their sum, which is the only relevant object, since the third component of solution converges to their sum. Thus we artificially set
\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)=0
on
\omega\times Y_{0}
, to have uniqueness of the solution of the limit problem. In the case when
\mathfrak{b}
is a function of
\hat{x}
only, the decomposition
\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)
is unique in
L^{2}(\omega\times Y)
, since we know that
\mathring{u}_{3|\omega\times Y_{1}}=0
.

The limit resolvent problem for the model of homogenized plate is given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.19
.

Let
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0
and suppose that the sequence of load densities converge as follows:

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(33)

Then the sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of
(
32
) to the unique solution (see also Remark
3.18
) of the following problems:
Find
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,0,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,0}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm memb,r}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl(\langle\overline{\rho}\rangle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}\rangle\bigr)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

(34)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm memb,r}(y)\sym\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{*}(\hat{x},y):\sym\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}_{*}(y)\,dy+\lambda\int_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}(\hat{x},y)\right)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}(y)\,dy,

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}(y)\,dy\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}\in H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega,

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\rho_{0}(y)\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)=\lambda^{-1}P^{0}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\qquad\kappa=\infty,

\displaystyle\left.\!\!\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{\kappa^{2}}{12}\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}^{r}(y)\nabla^{2}_{y}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y):\nabla_{y}^{2}v(y)\,dy+\lambda\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\rho_{1}(y)\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)v(y)\,dy\\[11.99998pt]
\hskip 130.0pt=\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y)v(y)\,dy\qquad\forall v\in H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}),\ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.\\[11.99998pt]
\rho_{0}(y)\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)=\lambda^{-1}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\quad\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)=0,\qquad y\in Y_{0}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\kappa\in(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(y)\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)+\rho_{0}(y)\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)=\lambda^{-1}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y);\quad\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)=0,\quad y\in Y_{0},\qquad\kappa=0.

If additionally we assume the strong two-scale convergence in (
33
), then we additionally have the strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y).

Corollary 3.20
.

Under the Assumption
2.1
(1) and provided
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega^{h},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, in addition to the convergences stated in Proposition
3.17
, we have

\tilde{u}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{H^{1}}0,\quad\mathring{u}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,

and thus we also have that
\mathfrak{b}=\mathring{u}_{3}=0
in the limit equations (
34
).

B. “Bending” scaling:
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}},\tau=2

In the regime
h\ll{\varepsilon_{h}}
the band gap structure of the spectrum for the spectrum of order
h^{2}
appears when we scale the coefficients in inclusions with
{{{\varepsilon_{h}^{4}}}}
. This can be seen from the a priori estimates obtained in Appendix.

We define, for every
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{0}(\boldsymbol{f})\bigl((0,0,\theta)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\bigr)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\omega}\langle\overline{f}_{3}\rangle\theta\,d\hat{x}+\int\limits_{\omega}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}(x,y)\,dy\,d\hat{x},

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\omega}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,\mathring{{\xi}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,dy\,d\hat{x}-\int\limits_{\omega}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{x_{3}\boldsymbol{f}}_{*}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\xi}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,dy\,d\hat{x},

(35)

\displaystyle\theta\in L^{2}(\omega),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*},\xi_{3})\in L^{2}\bigl(\omega;H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0})\bigr).

Furthermore, in connection with the limit problem described in Theorem
3.22
(expression (
38
) below), we introduce several objects:

•

A bilinear form

a^{\rm hom}_{0}:\bigl(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\rm hom}
on
L^{2}(\omega),
analogous to
a_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
of Part A, Section
3.2.1
(notice that here the situation is simpler since necessarily
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=0
);

•

The bilinear form

\hat{a}_{00,0}(\mathring{u},\mathring{\xi}):=\frac{1}{12}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}(y)\sym\nabla_{y}^{2}\mathring{u}:\sym\nabla^{2}\mathring{\xi}\,dy,\qquad\hat{a}_{00,0}:\bigl(H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0})\bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

and the associated “Bloch operator”
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0}
on
L^{2}(Y_{0}).

•

A scalar Zhikov function
\hat{\beta}_{0}
defined via the operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0}
(analogous to
\hat{\beta}_{\delta}
defined via the operator
\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta},
see Part C, Section
3.2.1
);

•

A set
\hat{\sigma}(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0})
(analogous to
\hat{\sigma}(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}));

•

The bilinear form

\hat{a}_{0}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u},\theta+\mathring{\xi}\bigr)=a_{0}^{\rm hom}(\mathfrak{b},\theta)+\int_{\omega}\hat{a}_{00,0}(\mathring{u},\mathring{\xi}),\quad\qquad\hat{a}_{0}:\Bigl(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0}))\Bigr)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

and the corresponding operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
on
L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0})).

The following proposition gives a suitable compactness result for the regime
h\ll\varepsilon_{h}.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
,
\tau=2
. The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
, such that for any sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and the corresponding solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\bigl\|\pi_{h/{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\quad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then there exist
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{0}(\Omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{u}_{\alpha}\in L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0})),

\alpha=1,2,

\mathring{u}_{3}\in L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0}))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}_{|\Omega\times Y_{1}}=0
such that (up to subsequence)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\[3.00003pt]
\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\bigl({\mathfrak{a}}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),{\mathfrak{a}}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},\\[3.00003pt]
\pi_{{\varepsilon_{h}}/h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\mathring{u}_{1}(\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\mathring{u}_{2}(\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\bigr)^{\top}\\[3.00003pt]
h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\bigl(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\[3.00003pt]
{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}{h}^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*},\mathring{u}_{3},\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}})(x,y).\end{split}

(36)

3.

If additionally to 2, one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=\hat{a}_{0}(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3},\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})+\|\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
\hat{a}_{0}
is defined below, then the strong two-scale convergence holds:

\pi_{{\varepsilon_{h}}/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})^{\top}.

The following theorem provides the limit resolvent equation.

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
,
\tau=2
and let the sequence of load densities satisfy

\pi_{h/{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(37)

Then the sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of
(
36
) to the unique solution of the following problem:
Determine
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
{\mathring{u}}_{\alpha}\in L^{2}(\omega,H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0})),

\alpha=1,2
,
{\mathring{u}}_{3}\in L^{2}(\omega,H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0}))
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=0,

(38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend,r}_{1}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x}):\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta}_{3}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl(\langle\rho\rangle{\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\mathring{u}_{3}\rangle(\hat{x})\bigr)\,{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{{f}}_{3}\rangle(\hat{x}){{\theta}}_{3}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\theta_{3}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega),

\displaystyle\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm memb,r}(y)\sym\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}}(\hat{x},y):\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}}(y)\,dy=\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}(y)\,dy\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{*}\in H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};{\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}(y)\nabla_{y}^{2}{\mathring{u}}_{3}(\hat{x},y):\nabla_{y}^{2}{\mathring{{\xi}}}_{3}(y)\,dy+\lambda\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\bigl(\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+{\mathring{u}_{3}}(\hat{x},y)\bigr)\mathring{{\xi}_{3}}(y)\,dy\qquad

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{f}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\mathring{{\xi}}_{3}(y)\,dy-\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\overline{x_{3}\boldsymbol{f}_{*}}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\xi}}_{3}(y)\,dy\qquad\forall\mathring{\xi}_{3}\in H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0}),\ \textrm{a.e.\,}\hat{x}\in\omega.

If the strong two-scale convergence in (
37
) holds, then additionally one has

\pi_{{\varepsilon_{h}}/h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\mathring{u}_{1}(\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\mathring{u}_{2}(\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y),\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\bigr)^{\top}.

The right-hand side of (
38
) can be interpreted as the element of dual of

\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0})).

Notice that the second equation in (
38
) is completely separated from the rest of the system.

3.2.3
Asymptotic regime
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h:
“moderately thin” plate

A. “Membrane” scaling:
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},

\tau=0

Similarly to Section
3.2.1
, we define the following objects using Theorem
3.24
(the expression for the limit resolvent (
41
)) :

•

A bilinear form

a_{\infty,\infty}:\left(V_{1,\infty,\infty}(\omega\times Y)+V_{2,\infty}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}_{\infty,\infty}
on the space
H_{\infty,\infty}(\Omega\times Y)
, analogous to
a_{\delta,\infty}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\infty}
of Part B, Section
3.2.1
. In this way the limit problem (
41
) can be written in the form

(\mathcal{A}_{\infty,\infty}+\lambda\mathcal{I})\boldsymbol{u}=P_{\infty,\infty}\boldsymbol{f},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}};

•

A bilinear form

\tilde{a}_{\infty}:\left(H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\{0\}+V_{2,\infty}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\cap\left(L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y,\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R},

and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
on the space

\left(L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\{0\}+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)\cap L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega\times Y,\mathbb{R}^{3})

(analogous to
\tilde{a}_{\delta}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
) — these are correctly defined under Assumption
2.1
(1);

•

A bilinear form

a_{\infty}^{\rm memb}:(H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}^{\rm memb}
on
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})

(analogous to
a_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
);

•

A bilinear form
\tilde{a}_{00,\infty}:(H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}))^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty}
on
L^{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})

(analogous to
\tilde{a}_{00,\delta}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
);

•

Functions
\beta_{\infty}
,
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\infty},
by analogy with
\beta_{\delta},

\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta};

•

A set
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})^{\prime},
by analogy with
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}
.

As in the case of other regimes, we first prove an appropriate compactness result, as follows.

Proposition 3.23
.

Let
\delta=\infty
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},\tau=0
. The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
, such that for any sequence

(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and the corresponding
solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\quad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

there exist
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,\infty,\infty}(\omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H_{\infty,\infty}(\Omega\times Y)
,

\mathcal{C}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\infty}(\Omega\times Y)
such that (up to subsequence)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{*}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{3}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\\
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\\
\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\
{\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\iota(\nabla_{y}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}).\end{split}

(39)

3.

If, additionally to 2, one has:

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\infty,\infty}\bigl((\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)+\bigl\|(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where the form
a_{\infty,\infty}
is defined above, then we have strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}.

The following theorem provides the limit resolvent equation.

Theorem 3.24
.

Let
\delta=\infty
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},\tau=0
and let the sequence of load densities satisfy the following convergence:

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(40)

The sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of
(
39
) to the unique solution of the following problem: Find
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\in V_{1,\infty,\infty}(\omega\times Y)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\infty}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb,h}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\Bigl(\langle\rho\rangle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\Bigr)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

(41)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{f}_{*}}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{*}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\langle\rho\rangle\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})+\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{u}}_{3}\bigr\rangle(\hat{x})=\lambda^{-1}\langle\overline{f}_{3}\rangle(\hat{x}),

\displaystyle\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}(x,y)\bigr):\sym\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)}\bigr)\,dy

\displaystyle+\lambda\int_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\bigl\{(\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}))^{\top}+{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}(x,y)\bigr\}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{Y_{0}}{\boldsymbol{f}}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)\,dy\qquad\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ \textrm{a.e.\,}x\in\Omega.

If we assume the strong two-scale convergence in (
40
), then the strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y)

holds.

Corollary 3.25
.

Under Assumption
2.1
(1) and provided
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
in addition to the convergences in Proposition (
3.23
) we have

\tilde{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{3}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,

and thus
\mathfrak{b}=0
in the limit equations (
41
).

Notice that the variable
x_{3}
is also just the parameter in the last equation in (
41
).

B. “Strong high-contrast bending” scaling
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h,

\tau=2

Here we define the following objects using Theorem
3.27
(the expression (
45
) for the limit resolvent):

•

A bilinear form
a^{\rm hom}_{\infty}:(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega))^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}^{\rm hom}
on
L^{2}(\omega),
analogous to
a_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
of Part A, Section
3.2.1
(notice that here the situation is simpler since necessarily
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=0
);

•

A scalar Zhikov function
\hat{\beta}_{\infty},
analogous to
\hat{\beta}_{\delta}
of Part C, Section
3.2.1
, so similarly to (
28
) we have

\hat{\beta}_{\infty}=\beta_{\infty,33};

•

A set
\hat{\sigma}(\mathcal{A}_{00,\infty}),
analogous to
\hat{\sigma}(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta});

•

The operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
on
\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined via the bilinear form

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\infty}\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(0,0,\theta)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a_{\infty}^{\rm hom}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathfrak{b},\theta)+\int_{\Omega}\tilde{a}_{00,\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}),

(42)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\left(\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{2,\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega\times Y_{0})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

Similarly to the regimes discussed above, a suitable compactness result is proved.

Proposition 3.26
.

Let
\delta=\infty
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2
.
The following statements hold:

1.

There exists
C>0
, independent of
h
, such that for a sequence
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
of load densities and
solutions
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the problem (
12
) one has

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

2.

If

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty,\quad(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then
there exist functions
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V_{2,\infty}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
,
\mathcal{C}\in\mathcal{C}_{\infty}(\Omega\times Y)
such that (up to subsequence)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,\\[1.00006pt]
\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}&{\,\xrightarrow{L^{2}}\,}\bigl({\mathfrak{a}}_{1}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),{\mathfrak{a}}_{2}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x}),\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},\\[3.00003pt]
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y),\\[3.00003pt]
h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x})\right)+\mathcal{C}(x,y),\\[3.00003pt]
{\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)&{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y)\bigr).\end{split}

(43)

3.

If, additionally to 2, one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{a}_{\infty}((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\|(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

where
\hat{a}_{\infty}
is defined in (
42
), then the strong two-scale convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}

holds.

The following theorem provides the limit resolvent equation.

Theorem 3.27
.

Let
\delta=\infty
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2

and let the sequence of load densities satisfy the following convergence:

\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(44)

Then the sequence of solutions to the resolvent problem (
12
) converges in the sense of
(
43
) to the unique solution of the following problem:
Determine
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}\in V_{2,\infty}(\Omega\times Y_{0})
such that

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=0,\\
&\frac{1}{12}\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm bend,h}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x}):\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}{\theta}_{3}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}+\lambda\int\limits_{\omega}\bigl(\rho_{0}(y){\mathfrak{b}}(\hat{x})+\langle\rho_{0}\overline{\mathring{u}}_{3}\rangle(\hat{x})\bigr)\,{\theta_{3}}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\\
&\,\hskip 100.0pt=\int\limits_{\omega}\langle\overline{{f}}_{3}\rangle(\hat{x})\,{{\theta}}_{3}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}\qquad\forall\theta_{3}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega),\\
&\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}(x,y)\bigr):\sym\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(y)\bigr)\,dy+\lambda\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\,\mathring{{\xi}}_{3}(y)\,dy+\lambda\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y){\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)\,dy\\
&\hskip 100.0pt=\int\limits_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}(x,y)\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)\,dy\qquad\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\ \textrm{a.e.\,}x\in\Omega.\end{split}

(45)

If strong two-scale convergence takes place in (
44
), then one additionally has

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y).

Remark 3.28
.

The limit problem (
45
) can be written as a resolvent problem on
\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\Omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
:

(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}+\lambda\mathcal{I})\boldsymbol{u}=\bigl(S_{2}(P_{\infty,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{1},S_{2}(P_{\infty,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{2},(P_{\infty,\infty}\boldsymbol{f})_{3}\bigr)^{\top},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Remark 3.29
.

The limit resolvent equations exhibit several differences between the regimes discussed: beside different effective tensors (this also happens in the moderate-contrast setting, see, e.g.,
[
27
]
in the case of nonlinear von Kármán plate theory), one has different kinds of behaviour on the inclusions: in the regime
h\sim{\varepsilon_{h}}
the inclusions behave like three-dimensional objects, while for
\delta=0
they can be seen as small plates. Furthermore, different scalings of load densities are required in different regimes, which does not happen in the case moderate contrast. Finally, the case
\delta=0
has an additional effective parameter
\kappa
; when
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
the elastic energy only resists
to oscillatory (spatial) motion (i.e., oscillations on the level of periodicity cells) in the out-of-plane direction.

3.3
Limit spectrum

In this section we will use the above resolvent convergence results to infer convergence of spectra of the operators
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon_{h}}.

As we shall see below in the proofs of the spectral convergence, one does not need to apply different scalings to different components of external loads, and thus only simplified versions of the limit resolvent equations will be necessary. Also, the presence of a spectrum of order
h^{2}
implies that any other scaling will cause the limit set to be the whole positive real line (see
[
10
]
). Thus, for the case when
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
in (
1
),
in order for the limit spectrum to have a “band-gap” structure we are forced to restrict ourselves to the “membrane” subspace
L^{2,\rm memb},
which is possible under Assumption
2.1
(1) concerning material symmetries. Otherwise, for the same case, the limit resolvent captures only the order-one part of the limit spectrum.
This is consistent with the standard result that the strong resolvent convergence only implies that the spectrum of the limit operator is contained in limit spectrum for
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon_{h}},
while an additional compactness argument
is necessary for the opposite inclusion (see, e.g,
[
39
]
). In our setting, compactness of eigenfunctions is lost when passing from the spectrum of order
h^{2}
(or order-one spectrum for the restriction to
L^{2,\rm memb}
) to the order-one spectrum for the full operator,
as the transversal component of an eigenfunction would converge only weakly two-scale.

Under Assumption
2.1
, for the membrane scalings of Part B of Section
3.2.1
and Parts A of Sections
3.2.2
,
3.2.3
, the resolvent equation can be restricted to the invariant subspace
L^{2,\rm memb},
where the solutions happen to be compact in the strong topology, see Corollaries
3.11
,
3.20
,
3.25
. This compactness property enables one to prove the convergence of spectra of order one for this restriction. Notice that in the regime
h\ll{\varepsilon_{h}}
there are different types of limit resolvents (distinguished by different values of the parameter
\kappa
) when Assumption
2.1
is not satisfied. In this regime, the convergence of the third component of the displacements is only weak two-scale, which is the reason why we do not invoke different resolvent limits in the analysis of the convergence of spectra in the mentioned regime. However, we will use this information in our study of the limit evolution equations.

Remark 3.30
.

For the case of spectra of order
h^{2},
in order to be able to obtain limit spectra with band gaps, one needs to consider different scalings of the coefficients on high-contrast inclusions. This motivated us for the analysis of this situation in Part C of
3.2.1
and Parts B of Sections
3.2.2
,
3.2.3
.

3.3.1
Preliminaries on spectral convergence

The Lax-Milgram theorem (see
[
28
]
) implies that for each
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
the equation

\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{f}

has a unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
understood in the weak sense. The operator

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{h}}:L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\to H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{f}:=\boldsymbol{u},

is compact due to the compact embedding
H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
(this compactness will be lost in the limit problem, except for the first case analysed). Therefore,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
has countably many eigenvalues forming a non-increasing sequence of positive numbers converging to zero, the only remaining element of the spectrum
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Therefore, the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
consists of eigenvalues ordered in a non-decreasing positive sequence
\lambda_{n}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
that tends to infinity.

In what follows, we are interested in understanding the relationship between the spectra of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
as
h\to 0
and eigenvalues of the limit operators discussed in Section
3.2
. To this end, the following standard notion of convergence will be referred to throughout.

Definition 3.31
.

We say that a sequence of sets
S_{h}
(e.g.,
S_{h}=\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
) converges in the Hausdorff sense to the set
S
if:

•

(
H_{1}
) For any
\lambda\in S
, there exists a sequence of
\lambda^{h}\in S_{h}
convergent to
\lambda
(as
h\to 0.
)

•

(
H_{2}
) The limit of any convergent sequence of
\lambda^{h}\in S_{h}
is an element of
S.

For various scalings of Section
3.2
, we will discuss the convergence in the Hausdorff sense of
\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}})
to the spectrum of the corresponding limit operator.

The first property of Hausdorff convergence of spectra is normally a direct consequence of the strong resolvent convergence, while the second property requires the compactness of the sequence of eigenfunctions in an appropriate topology.

3.3.2
Asymptotic regime
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, scaling
\tau=2

In this section we will analyse the limit spectrum of order
h^{2}
for
{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
-scaling of the coefficients in the inclusions. We will show that the high-contrast has no effect on the limit, in that the (scaled) limit spectrum is of the same type as for the ordinary plate (i.e., homogeneous or with moderate contrast), in particular the limit operator has compact resolvent. This is precisely the reason why we analyse this combination of scalings of the spectrum and the coefficients only for the asymptotic regime
h\sim{\varepsilon_{h}}
(i.e.,
\delta\in(0,\infty)
).

On the one hand we would like to show that in the case of an ordinary plate the resolvent approach can also provide information about the convergence of spectra (alternatively to, say, using Rayleigh quotients), and on the other hand we aim at demonstrating that in the mentioned case the limit problem does not exhibit spectral gaps and thus a
different scaling of the coefficients is required for them to appear.

The following theorem provides the relevant result concerning spectral convergence.

Theorem 3.32
.

Let
\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon_{h}}=\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
.
The spectra
\sigma\bigl(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}\bigr)
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}},
as
h\to 0
,
which is an increasing sequence of positive eigenvalues
(\lambda_{\delta,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that tend to infinity, each of finite multiplicity. More precisely, if by
\lambda_{n}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
we denote the
n
-th eigenvalue of
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
(by repeating each eigenvalue according to its multiplicity), then

h^{-2}\lambda_{n}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\to\lambda_{\delta,n},\quad h\to 0,

where
\lambda_{\delta,n}
is
n
-th eigenvalue of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}
(again repeated in accordance with multiplicity). Furthermore, for any fixed
n
and any choice of normalised eigenfunctions with eigenvalues
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{n},
there is
a (
h
-indexed) subsequence such that the corresponding eigenfunctions converge, as
h\to 0,
to an eigenfunction with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\delta,n}
of the limit problem.

3.3.3
Asymptotic regime
\delta\in[0,\infty),
scaling
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0

In this section we analyse the operator
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
in the space
L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In the regime
\delta=0
we require that the component
Y_{0}
has
C^{1,1}
boundary.
We define the following generalized eigenvalue problem: Find
\lambda>0
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega,\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\begin{split}\int_{\omega}\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\hat{x})d\hat{x}&=\int_{\omega}\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\hat{x})d\hat{x},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}).\end{split}

(46)

(In the case
\delta=0
we put
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{\rm memb,r}
instead of
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}
, in the case when
\delta=\infty
we put

\mathbb{C}^{{\rm memb},h}
instead of
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm memb},
see Section
3.1
for the relevant definitions.)
The following theorem contains the spectral convergence result for the regime considered here.

Theorem 3.33
.

Suppose
\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon_{h}}=\delta\in[0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
and let Assumption
2.1
(1) be valid.
The set of all
\lambda>0
for which the problem (
46
) has a non-trivial solution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
is at most countable.
The spectra of the operators
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
converge in the Hausdorff sense to the spectrum of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta},
and one has

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\mbox{ The generalized eigenvalue problem \eqref{generalizedeigenvaluemembrane} is solvable.}\right\}}.

(47)

Additionally, under Assumption
2.1
(2,3), the matrix
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}(\lambda)
is scalar and

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})^{\prime}\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta,11}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta,22}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm memb}_{\delta})\right\}}.

Remark 3.34
.

It was shown in
[
22
]
that each non-empty interval of the form
(\tilde{\omega}_{n},\tilde{\omega}_{n+1}),

n\in\mathbb{N},
contains a subinterval
(\tilde{\omega}_{n},\alpha)
,
\tilde{\omega}_{n}\leq\alpha<\tilde{\omega}_{n+1}
in which both eigenvalues of the matrix
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
are negative, a subinterval
(\alpha,\beta)
,
\alpha<\beta\leq\tilde{\omega}_{n+1}
in which one of its eigenvalues
is negative while the other is positive, and the interval
(\beta,\tilde{\omega}_{n+1})

in which both its eigenvalues
are positive. It follows, as is explained in
[
22
]
, that in the interval
(\tilde{\omega}_{n},\alpha)
there is no wave propagation in any direction, while in the interval
(\alpha,\beta)
one has evanescent solutions in the direction of the negative eigenvectors, and finally
in the intervals
(\beta,\tilde{\omega}_{n+1})
all directions allow wave propagation.

Under Assumption
2.1
(1–3), the above spectral structure can be quantified in a straightforward way and
\tilde{\omega}_{n}<\alpha=\beta<\tilde{\omega}_{n+1}
, see
[
38
,
39
]
.
In this case the matrix
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
is scalar and

\lim_{\lambda\to\tilde{\omega}_{n}^{+}}\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta,11}(\lambda)=-\infty,\qquad\lim_{\lambda\to\tilde{\omega}_{n+1}^{-}}\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta,11}(\lambda)=+\infty,

where
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta,11}
is one of the two equal diagonal elements, and the limits are taken as
\lambda
approaches
\tilde{\omega}_{n}
on the right and on the left, respectively.

The above properties of the limit spectrum are relevant in a variety of applications, such as noise suppression. Being peculiar to wave propagation in high-contrast media, they are often referred to as “high-contrast effects”.

3.3.4
Asymptotic regime
\delta\in(0,\infty),
scaling
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2
and asymptotic regime
\delta=0,
scaling
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
,
\tau=2

For the regimes considered here, we show that high-contrast effects occur in the limit as
h\to 0.
As before, when
\delta=0
we assume that
Y_{0}
has
C^{1,1}
boundary. We have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.35
.

Let
\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon_{h}}=\delta\in[0,\infty)
. In the cases
\delta=0,

\delta>0
we assume that
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
and
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h,
respectively. The spectrum of the operator
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
converges in the Hausdorff sense to the spectrum of the operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta},
given by

\displaystyle\sigma(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\sigma}(\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta})\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\hat{\beta}_{\delta}(\lambda)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}_{\delta})\right\}},\qquad\delta\in(0,\infty),\\[4.49997pt]
\hat{\sigma}(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0})\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\hat{\beta}_{0}(\lambda)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm hom}_{0})\right\}},\qquad\delta=0.\end{array}\right.

Remark 3.36
.

The operator
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}
is non-local when Assumption
2.1
is not satisfied. It is not known to us whether this has been commented on in the existing literature, even in the case of a homogeneous plate.

3.3.5
Asymptotic regime
\delta=\infty

As we see below, in the case
\delta=\infty,
the limit spectrum has points outside spectrum of the limit operator. From the intuitive point of view, the effective behaviour is similar to that of a cuboid with disjoint soft inclusions in the shape of long thin rods arranged parallel to each other and connecting two opposite sides of the body.

In order to formulate the result of this section, we define:

•

An operator as the one defined via the bilinear form

\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,\qquad\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R};

•

An operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the one defined via the form

\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{+}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,\qquad\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{+}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R};

•

An operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{-}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
as the one defined via the form

\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{-}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{-}_{0}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,\qquad\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{-}:\left(H_{00}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{-}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.

•

The restriction
\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
of the operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}
to the membrane subspace
L^{2,{\rm memb}}(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),
whenever Assumption
2.1
(1) holds.

First, we give characterisations of the limit spectra of
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
and
\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
, which in this regime play significant roles.

Theorem 3.37
.

Suppose that
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h
. Then one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})\cup\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}).

(49)

Moreover, one has

\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\pm})=\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}),

(50)

and there exists
m_{0}>0
such that

\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})=\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})=[m_{0},+\infty).

(51)

Under Assumption
2.1
(1), one additionally has

\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})=\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{\rm strip})\supset\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})=\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})=\sigma\bigl(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr).

(52)

Next we provide a characterisation of the limit spectrum for
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Theorem 3.38
.

Let
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
and
\tau=0
.
The set of all
\lambda>0
for which the problem (
46
) obtains a nontrivial solution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
is at most countable.
The spectra of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
converge in the Hausdorff sense to

\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})
, where

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})^{\prime}\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\mbox{ The generalized eigenvalue problem \eqref{generalizedeigenvaluemembrane} is solvable.}\right\}}.

(53)

Under Assumption
2.1
(2, 3), the matrix
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\infty}(\lambda)
is scalar and

\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})^{\prime}\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\infty,11}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\infty,22}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm memb}_{\delta})\right\}}.

(54)

Furthermore, one has
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})
.

Theorem 3.39
.

Suppose that
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2
.
The spectra of
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
converge in the Hausdorff sense to
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{-}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and

\sigma(\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})=\hat{\sigma}({\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})\cup\overline{\left\{\lambda>0:\hat{\beta}_{\infty}(\lambda)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}^{\rm hom}_{\infty})\right\}}.

Remark 3.40
.

As is shown in Lemma
4.2
, the set
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
(appropriately scaled) is always a subset of the limit spectrum. In the regime
\delta=\infty
, the operator has a scaling factor
{\varepsilon_{h}}/h
in front of the derivative in
x_{3}.
This allows eigenfunctions to oscillate in the out-of-plane direction (and thus weakly converge to zero). This is the reason for so-called “spectral pollution” (see, e.g.,
[
2
]
).

3.4
Limit evolution equations

It is expected from the results of Section
3.2
concerning the resolvent convergence for the operators
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
that the limit evolution equations have the form of a system that links the behaviour on the stiff matrix and the soft inclusions by means of coupled solution components, which can be viewed as macroscopic and microscopic variables. Representing the system in terms of the macroscopic component only leads to a non-trivial effective description exhibiting memory effects. This is one of the reasons what makes high-contrast materials interesting in applications.

The present section aims at providing a detailed study of the consequences of the form of the limit resolvent equations obtained for different asymptotic regimes in Section
3.2
on the limit evolution equations in the corresponding regimes. On the abstract level, this connection has been analysed in
[
30
]
. A key fact used in that paper is that the resolvent is the Laplace transform of the exponential function of the operator of the wave equation, obtained from an equivalent
system of equations of first order in time. In what follows, we adjust our analysis to these general results, in order to account for the particular features of our setup due to dimension reduction in linear elasticity. As we see below, in this context different scalings of spectra imply different scalings of time (i.e., bending waves propagate on a slower time scale than in-plane, “membrane”, waves). As far as we know, the effect of considering different time scalings has not been addressed in the literature; see
[
31
]
for the analysis of limit evolution of isotropic homogeneous plates for the commonly considered “long” time scaling of order
h^{-1}
.

It should also be noted that some load density scalings prevent us from using the results of
[
30
]
, in which case separate analysis is necessary to show weak convergence of solutions (see, e.g., the proof of Theorem
3.41
). This happens for the case
\tau=2
(i.e., for long times of order
h^{-1}
) in the regimes
\delta\in(0,\infty),

\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
and
\delta=0,

\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
. For these, to prove weak convergence we use the Laplace transform directly, following the same overall strategy as the one adopted in
[
30
]
in the abstract setting, see Apprendix. However, due to the said load density scalings, a modification of the results of
[
30
]
is required, in order to account for the specific structure of the right-hand side of the limit problem; this is also discussed in Appendix, see in particular Theorems
A.35
,
A.51
.

The starting point of this section is the family of Cauchy problems
(h>0)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(t)+{h^{-\tau}}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(t),\\[5.50003pt]
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\end{array}\right.

(55)

understood in the weak sense. The term
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(t)
represents the load density at time
t>0.
For each
h,
we suppose that
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
is provided on the time interval
[0,T_{h}],

T_{h}>0.

The functions
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
are the initial data for the displacement and velocity fields, respectively. We make the following assumptions:

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}^{1/2})=H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})),\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

In what follows, we shall analyse the “critical” cases
\tau=2
and
\tau=0
for the time scaling. Conditions for well-posedness of the problem (
55
) can be found in Appendix, see Section
A.4
.

In conclusion of this section, we reiterate that there are two ways to interpret the scaling
h^{-\tau}
of the differential expression in (
55
): by scaling the density of the material (with
h^{\tau}
) or by introducing the new time scale
\tilde{t}=t/h^{\tau/2}
. We adopt the latter interpretation throughout. Multiplying (
55
) by
h^{\tau}
and replacing
t
by
\tilde{t},
we obtain the family of problems
(h>0)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{\tilde{t}\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{t})+\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{t})=\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{t}),\\[5.50003pt]
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\quad\partial_{\tilde{t}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(0)=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\end{array}\right.

(56)

where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{t}):=h^{\tau}{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(h^{\tau}\tilde{t})
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=h^{\tau/2}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
. Thus discussing the solution of (
55
) on a time interval
[0,T]
(with an appropriate scaling of the load density) is equivalent to discussing the solution of (
56
) on the time interval
[0,T/h^{\tau/2}]
(with the corresponding scaling of the loads). While from now on we shall work in the framework of the equation (
55
), which is convenient from the mathematical point of view, it is the equation (
56
) that represents the actual physical wave motion, which thereby takes place on an appropriate time scale of order
h^{-\tau/2}.

3.4.1
Long-time behaviour for the regime
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=2

The case analysed here resembles the standard (moderate-contrast) plate model. The following convergence statement holds for the evolution problem.

Theorem 3.41
.

Suppose that
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon}_{h}
,
\tau=2
.
Let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
be a sequence of solutions
to (
55
) and assume that

\displaystyle\left(h\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\alpha}\right)_{h>0}

\displaystyle\subset L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y))\textrm{\ \ bounded},\ \ \alpha=1,2,

(57)

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})),

(58)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\hat{x})\in\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega),

(59)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(60)

and assume additionally that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

Then one has

\displaystyle\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{a}_{1}(t,\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{1}(t,x,y)\\[3.00003pt]
\mathfrak{a}_{2}(t,\hat{x})-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{2}(t,x,y)\\[3.00003pt]
\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(0,0,\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top},

(64)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in C([0,T];H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
\mathfrak{b}\in C([0,T];H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})))
are determined uniquely by solving the problem

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}\mathfrak{b}(t)+\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}_{\delta}\mathfrak{b}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\bigl({\boldsymbol{f}}(t)\bigr),\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\textrm{(see \eqref{anketa1})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(65)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}(0)=u_{0,3}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega),

\displaystyle\qquad\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}(0)=S_{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(P_{\delta,\infty}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1})_{3}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in L^{2}(\omega),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(t)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}(t)}+\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}(t)},\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textrm{(see \eqref{nadoknada11})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{00,\delta}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,\hat{x},\cdot)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{f}_{*}(t,\hat{x},\cdot),0)^{\top},

(67)

so that
\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\omega))
.
One also has

\limsup_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\,dt<\infty.

If one assumes strong two-scale convergence of load densities

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}\in L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right),

(68)

\displaystyle h\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{\alpha}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\ \ \textrm{strongly in}\ L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)\right),\ \ \alpha=1,2,

strong two-scale convergence of the initial data in
(
59
), (
60
), where
({\boldsymbol{u}}_{1})_{*}=0
,
{u}_{1,3}\in L^{2}(\omega),
and the condition

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}\right)=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}(0),\mathfrak{b}(0)\bigr)+\|\mathfrak{b}(0)\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

then one has

\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{\mathfrak{b}}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{\mathfrak{b}}-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{b})^{\top},\qquad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b})^{\top}.

(69)

Moreover, the following convergence of energy sequences holds for all
t\in[0,T]:

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}\bigl(\mathfrak{b}(t),\mathfrak{b}(t)\bigr)+\|\mathfrak{b}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Corollary 3.42
.

Suppose that for each
h>0,
a surface load density

\mathcal{G}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}([0,T];(H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})^{*})
is added to the right-hand side of (
55
). We assume that
\mathcal{G}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is generated by an
L^{2}
-function
\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
(representing the “true” surface load)
so that

\mathcal{G}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\int_{\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{\theta}\,d\hat{x},\qquad\boldsymbol{\theta}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

where an obvious shorthand for a sum of two integrals over
\omega
is used,
and make the following additional assumptions on
\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\alpha}\subset L^{2}\Bigl([0,T];L^{2}\bigl(\omega\times\bigl\{-1/2,1/2\bigr\}\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)\Bigr)\textrm{ is bounded },

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}\Bigl([0,T];L^{2}\bigl(\omega\times\bigl\{-1/2,1/2\bigr\}\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)\Bigr).

Then
a variant of Theorem
3.41
holds, where in the limit equations (
65
) the right-hand side has an additional term

\mathcal{G}^{1}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{g})\in L^{2}([0,T];(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega))^{*})
, represented by a limiting surface load
L^{2}
vector function
\boldsymbol{g}=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{1}(\boldsymbol{g})(t)(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\omega}\left(\bigl\langle g_{3}(t,\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)+g_{3}(t,\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\right)\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\omega}\left(\bigl\langle g_{1}(t,\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)-g_{1}(t,\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\right)\partial_{1}\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\omega}\left(\bigl\langle g_{2}(t,\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)-g_{2}(t,\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\right)\partial_{2}\theta(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}\bigl(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(t)},0\bigr):\bigl(0,\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\theta(\hat{x})\bigr)\,d\hat{x},\qquad\theta\in\bigl(H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)\bigr)^{*}.

In the above formula, for every
t\in[0,T],
the function
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(t)}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is the solution to the problem

\int\limits_{\omega}\mathbb{C}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(t)}(\hat{x}):\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}=\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{2}(\boldsymbol{g}_{*})(t)(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),

where the functional
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{2}(\boldsymbol{g}_{*})(t)
is defined by the formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{2}(\boldsymbol{g}_{*})(t)(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*})

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\omega}\left(\bigl\langle g_{1}(t,\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)+g_{1}(t,\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1}\,d\hat{x}

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\omega}\left(\bigl\langle g_{2}(t,\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)+g_{2}(t,\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\bigr\rangle\right)\boldsymbol{\theta}_{2}\,d\hat{x},\qquad\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Also, the right-hand side of (
66
) contains

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(t)}\in L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega,\mathbb{R}^{2}))
as an additional term,
while on the right-hand side of (
67
) one additionally has
\mathcal{G}^{3}(\boldsymbol{g}_{*})\in L^{2}([0,T];(H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})^{*})
defined by

\mathcal{G}^{3}(\boldsymbol{g}_{*})(t,\hat{x})(\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\{-1/2,1/2\}\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(t,\hat{x},\cdot)\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{*}(\cdot),\qquad\boldsymbol{\xi}\in H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}),\qquad t\in[0,T],\quad\hat{x}\in\omega.

Remark 3.43
.

For each of the other regimes studied, a statement analogous to Corollary
3.42
is valid.

Remark 3.44
.

The statement of Theorem
3.41
can be strengthened as follows. The boundedness and convergence conditions (
57
) and (
58
) can be replaced by the requirement of boundedness and convergence, respectively, of the sequences
(\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
and
(h\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\alpha})_{h>0}
in the corresponding spaces of
L^{1}
functions on
[0,T].
Under this weaker assumption, a still stronger version of (
3.41
), (
64
) holds, where the weak convergence in
L^{2}
spaces on
[0,T]
is replaced by a weak* convergence in the corresponding
L^{\infty}
spaces on
[0,T],
see the comment following Definition
A.17
.

Similarly, the
L^{2}
convergences (
68
) can be replaced by the weaker conditions

\displaystyle\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{t,1,{\rm dr}-2}(0,0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}\in L^{1}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\right)

\displaystyle h\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{\alpha}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\ \ \textrm{strongly in}\ L^{1}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)\right),\ \ \alpha=1,2,

to obtain a strong two-scale convergence
\xrightarrow{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}
for both sequences in (
69
); see the same comment at the end of Section
A.2
for the definition of
\xrightarrow{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}
.

These stronger versions of the claims in Theorem
3.41

follow immediately from a priori estimates, see also Remark
A.52
, Remark
A.53
, however we choose to remain in the
L^{2}
setting.
