A version of the discussion within this remark applies also to Theorem
3.49
, Theorem
3.53
, and Theorem
3.55
.

Remark 3.45
.

The limit equations (
65
)–(
67
) are obtained on a long time scale. The stiff component behaves like a perforated domain, and there is no coupling between its deformation and the deformation of the inclusions. The deformation of the inclusions and the even part of the in-plane deformation of the stiff component behave quasi-statically (i.e., without an inertia term), as a consequence of small forces slowly varying in time. (Recall that the physical equation is (
56
) with the right-hand side
\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
subject to an appropriate version of the condition (
57
).) Since there is no coupling in the limit between the inclusions and the stiff component, there are no memory effects in the time evolution. However, it is expected that high-contrast effects would be seen in higher-order terms (“correctors”) of the deformation, which we do not pursue here.

Without making additional symmetry assumptions about the material properties, the limit operator for the evolution of the out-of-plane component is spatially non-local, due the coupling between the in-plane and out-of-plane components.

Remark 3.46
.

We are not able to obtain pointwise in time convergence without additional assumptions on the load density. This is expected (replacing weak two-scale convergence with strong two-scale convergence) also as a consequence of the analysis presented in
[
30
]
.

Remark 3.47
.

The influence of in-plane forces on the limit model is seen through their mean value across the plate, represented above by an integral over the interval
I=[-1/2,1/2]
, as well as through the mean value of their moments over the same interval
I
. In the case of planar symmetries, see Assumption
2.1
(1), moments of in-plane forces have the same effect on the limit deformation as out-of-plane forces, i.e., they produce out-of-plane displacements. This is expected from the physical point of view and is standard for plate theories (see, e.g.,
[
18
]
).

Remark 3.48
.

Considering whether different components of the load density should be scaled differently is important from the modelling perspective. Indeed, if its in-plane and out-of-plane components had the same magnitude, one would not see the effects of the in-plane components in the (leading order of the) deformation. On the other hand, it is expected that sufficiently large in-plane loads do influence the limit deformation.
However, for some of the asymptotic regimes analysed here the effects on the in-plane and out-of-plane loads on the limit deformation are similar, in which case these loads are set to have the same magnitude in the equations. This kind of situation also occurs in the context of linear elastic shells, see
[
19
]
for shells as compared to the case of linear elastic
plates
[
18
]
.

3.4.2
Real-time behaviour for
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=0
in different regimes

Here we discuss a class of evolution problems with “non-standard” effective behaviour, which manifests itself, in particular, through time non-locality.

Theorem 3.49
.

Suppose that
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon}_{h}
,
\tau=0
,
\delta,\kappa\in[0,\infty],
and
consider the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
of solutions to the problem (
55
), assuming that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})),

(70)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y)\in V_{1,\delta,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)+V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(71)

Assume also that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

Then one has

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},

(72)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\partial_{t}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr),

(73)

where
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in C([0,T];V_{1,\delta,\kappa}(\omega\times Y)+V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0}))

is the unique weak solution of the problem

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(t)+\mathcal{A}_{\delta,\kappa}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(t)=P_{\delta,\kappa}\boldsymbol{f}(t),

\displaystyle\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y),\qquad\partial_{t}\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(0)=P_{\delta,\kappa}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(x,y),

such that
\partial_{t}\left((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right)\in C([0,T];H_{\delta,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y))
.
Furthermore, the following limit energy bound holds:

\limsup_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\bigr)+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

If strong two-scale convergence holds in (
70
)–(
71
) with
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{\delta,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y))
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{\delta,\kappa}(\Omega\times Y),
and

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\delta,\kappa}\bigl(((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(0),((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(0)\bigr)+\bigl\|\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(0)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

then strong two-scale convergence holds in (
72
)–(
73
). Moreover, one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\Bigl(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\bigr)+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\Bigr)=a_{\delta,\kappa}\bigl(((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(t),((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},0)^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(t)\bigr)+\bigl\|\bigl((\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(t)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

for every
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 3.50
.

The models obtained here are degenerate with respect to the out-of-plane component of the displacement.
Indeed, in the static case it is substantially easier for the plate to bend than to extend in-plane; however, in the dynamic case in real time, for the forces of magnitude one, there is no elastic resistance to out-of-plane motions, which are therefore entirely due to external loads.

It is also worthwhile noting that in the high-contrast setting out-of-plane loads
{\boldsymbol{f}}=(0,0,f_{3})
for which
\overline{f}_{3}=0
do produce some in-plane motion in the case when
\delta\in(0,\infty]
, as a consequence of the coupling between the deformations on the stiff component and on the inclusions, which is not possible in the setting of homogenisation with moderate contrast. (In the regime
\delta=0,
inclusions behave like small plates and thus only the effects of the average loads
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}
in the variable
x_{3}
are seen in the limit.) On a related note, from the point of view of quantitative analysis, it is not expected that the effect elastic resistance to out-of-plane motions disappears entirely, as it may manifest itself
in lower-order terms, see
[
17
]
for a quantitative analysis of the resolvent equation for a thin infinite plate in moderate contrast.

Remark 3.51
.

To the best of our knowledge, dynamic models representing “real time behaviour” have not been discussed in the literature, even in the case of an ordinary plate. Certainly, these models are not as physically relevant as those in which elastic resistance to out-of-plane motions is observed. This might be due to the fact that for most materials mass density is much smaller than Lamé constants (in dimensionless terms). However, since these models exhibit high-contrast effects, which does not happen when the time is scaled (unless the coefficients on the inclusions are scaled in a non-standard way in relation to the coefficients on the stiff component), we find it is important to discuss them also.

Remark 3.52
.

In the limit problem, due to the coupling of the deformation on the stiff component, given by
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top},
and the oscillatory part of the deformation on the soft component, given by
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},
there are memory effects (under the assumption that the micro-variable
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
is unknown). The emergence of these memory effects can be seen as follows. If one would like to know the deformation on the stiff component at time
T,
given by
(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\mathfrak{b})^{\top}(T)
, one would not only require the initial data (deformation and speed) on the stiff component at an “initial” time
t_{0}<T
and loads
\boldsymbol{f}
on the time interval
[t_{0},T],
but also the value of the micro-variable
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
and its speed at time
t_{0}.
It one cannot measure this micro-variable (which is a physically meaningful scenario), then the corresponding degree of freedom becomes “hidden” internally, which results in a non-local time dependence macroscopically.

3.4.3
Long-time behaviour for
\delta\in(0,\infty]
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h
,
\tau=2

Here we demonstrate that by varying the contrast between material properties of the two components (“stiff” and “soft”), the evolution problem may be shown to exhibit time non-locality also in the regime of long times.

Theorem 3.53
.

Suppose that
\delta\in(0,\infty]
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon}_{h}h
,
\tau=2
, and
let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
be the sequence of solutions of the problem (
55
), assuming that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}\bigl([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})\bigr),

(74)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\hat{x})+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}(x,y)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(75)

Assume also that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

Then one has

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}},

(76)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\partial_{t}\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr),

(77)

where
(0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in C([0,T];\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+V_{2,\delta}(\Omega\times Y_{0}))

is the unique weak solution to the problem

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}

\displaystyle\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(t)+\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(t)=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigl(S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f}(t))_{1},S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f}(t))_{2},(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{f}(t))_{3}\bigr)^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},

\displaystyle\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(x,y),\qquad\partial_{t}\bigl(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr)(0)=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigl(S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{1})_{1},S_{2}(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{1})_{2},(P_{\delta,\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{1})_{3}\bigr)^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(x,y),

such that
\partial_{t}\left((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right)\in C([0,T];H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y))
.
Furthermore, the following limit energy bound holds:

\limsup_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

If strong two-scale convergence holds in (
74
)–(
75
) with
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y))
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{\delta,\infty}(\Omega\times Y),

and

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=\hat{a}_{\delta}\bigl(((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(0),((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(0)\bigr)+\bigl\|\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(0)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

then strong two-scale convergence holds in (
76
)–(
77
). Moreover, for every
t\in[0,T]
one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=\hat{a}_{\delta}\bigl(((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(t),((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(t)\bigr)+\bigl\|\bigl((0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}})(t)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Remark 3.54
.

The above limit model exhibits memory effects, due to the coupling of the deformations on the stiff component and on the inclusions, similarly to what happened in Section
3.4.2
. As before, see Remark
3.45
, in the case when
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and no additional symmetries are imposed on the material properties, the limit macro-operator
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\delta}
is spatially non-local.

3.4.4
Long-time behaviour for
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}
,
\tau=2

Here we discuss an analogue of the result of the previous section for the case
\delta=0,
in which we need to apply different scalings to the in-plane and out-of-plane loads. As already emphasized in Sections
3.2.2
(resolvent convergence),
3.3.4
(limit spectrum), in this regime we require that
Y_{0}
have
C^{1,1}
boundary.

Theorem 3.55
.

Suppose that
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon}_{h}^{2}
,
\tau=2,
and let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
be the sequence of solutions to the problem (
55
), assuming that

\displaystyle\left((h/{\varepsilon_{h}})\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\alpha}\right)_{h>0}

\displaystyle\subset L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y))\textrm{\ \ is\ bounded},\qquad\alpha=1,2,

(78)

\displaystyle\pi_{h/{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\hat{x},y)\in\{0\}^{2}\times H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),

(79)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

(80)

and assume additionally that

\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right)<\infty.

Then one has

\displaystyle\pi_{{\varepsilon_{h}}/h}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathring{u}_{1}(t,\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y)\\[3.00003pt]
\mathring{u}_{2}(t,\hat{x},y)-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y)\\[3.99994pt]
\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(0,0,\partial_{t}\left(\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y)\right)\bigr)^{\top},

where the pair
\mathfrak{b}\in C([0,T];H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})))
form the unique weak solution of the problem

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}\left(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3}\right)(t)+\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\left(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3}\right)(t)=\mathcal{F}_{0}(\boldsymbol{f}),\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textrm{(see \eqref{pomoc5})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3}\right)(0)=u_{0,3}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),\hskip 21.52771pt\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(0)=P^{0}u_{1,3}\in L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}(t,\hat{x},\cdot)=\boldsymbol{f}_{*}(t,\hat{x},\cdot),

(81)

such that
\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y))

\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}))
.
Furthermore, the following limit energy bound holds:

\limsup_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)\,dt<\infty.

If one additionally assumes that

\displaystyle\pi_{h/{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}\in L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})\right),

\displaystyle(h/{\varepsilon_{h}})\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0\ \ \textrm{strongly in}\ L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)\right),\qquad\alpha=1,2,

the two-scale convergence in (
79
) and (
80
) holds in the strong sense with
(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})_{*}=0
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1,3}\in L^{2}(\omega)+L^{2}(\omega\times Y_{0}),
and that

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}\right)=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}\bigl((\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(0),(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(0)\bigr)+\bigl\|(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(0)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

then one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})+\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y))^{\top},\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}(0,0,\partial_{t}(\mathfrak{b}(t,\hat{x})++\mathring{u}_{3}(t,\hat{x},y)))^{\top}.

Moreover, for every
t\in[0,T]
the convergence

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t),\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t))+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}\bigl((\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(t),(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(t)\bigr)+\bigl\|(\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{3})(t)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

holds.

Remark 3.56
.

In the regime
\delta=0
inclusions behave like small plates and thus the corresponding deformation satisfies a version of the classical Kirchhoff-Love ansatz. Using the rationale discussed in Remark
3.48
, we argue that in order to see the effects of both in-plane and out-of-plane components of loads in the limit model, we should scale them differently to one another.

Similarly to the regime
\delta\in(0,\infty)
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=2,
we impose a restriction on the time derivatives of in-plane forces, see (
78
), which in terms of the “physical” time corresponds to slowly acting loads. This results in a (partial) quasi-static evolution in the limit, see (
81
).
Furthermore, in order to obtain strong two-scale convergence of solutions, akin to (
69
), we impose a further restriction that properly scaled in-plane forces together with their time derivatives, in the spirit to (
68
), go to zero as
h\to 0.

4
Proofs

4.1
Proof of Proposition
3.4

Proof.

We provide the proof for the case
\delta\in(0,\infty)
; the other cases are dealt in a similar fashion, bearing in mind Remark
3.2
and Remark
3.3
.

Consider the minimiser
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})
in the variational formulation (
8
). Then for arbitrary symmetric matrices
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\rm sym}
one has a lower bound for elastic stored energy density, as follows:

\begin{split}\mathbb{C}^{\rm hom}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}):(\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B})&\geq C\int\limits_{I}\left\|\iota(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})+\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{2,\gamma}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}(x_{3},\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}dx_{3}\\
&\geq C\int\limits_{I}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}+\sym\nabla_{y}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}(x_{3},\cdot)\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}dx_{3},\end{split}

(82)

due to the coercivity of the tensor
\mathbb{C}_{1}
representing the elastic properties on the stiff component. In order to eliminate the corrector
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}
from the bound (
82
), we first construct an extension for it from
Y_{1}
to the whole cell
Y
for each
x_{3}\in I
. To this end, we first define the symmetric affine part of an arbitrary
H^{1}
function, as follows. For
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\xi}_{2})^{\top}\in H^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2}),
we consider the function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2})
defined by

\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)&:=\fint\limits_{Y_{1}}\boldsymbol{\xi}(y)dy+\fint\limits_{Y_{1}}\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\xi}(y)dy\,\biggl(y-\fint\limits_{Y_{1}}ydy\biggr).\end{split}

Notice that the operator
\hat{\cdot}
is linear and satisfies the following properties:

\begin{split}\nabla_{y}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\sym\nabla_{y}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\fint\limits_{Y_{1}}\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\xi}(y)dy,\qquad\fint\limits_{Y}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(y)dy=\fint\limits_{Y_{1}}\boldsymbol{\xi}(y)dy,\\
\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}\leq|Y|/|Y_{1}|\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\xi}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}.\end{split}

Now we define the extension operator
\hat{E}:H^{1}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2}),
via

\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\xi}:=E(\boldsymbol{\xi}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})+\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}},

where
E
is the extension operator from
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
, which satisfies the estimate

\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(E\boldsymbol{\xi})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\xi}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}.

It is easy to see that

\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\xi})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\xi}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}.

(83)

Next, consider the function

\boldsymbol{\psi}(y):=\left(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right)y+\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}(y).

Clearly, one has

\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\psi}(y)=E(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{*})(y)+(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})y+\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{*}(y)=\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}(y)+(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})y.

Furthermore, from (
83
) one has

\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\psi})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}=C\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}+\sym\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y_{1};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}.

(84)

At the same time, the following bound holds:

\begin{split}\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\psi})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}&=\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*})+(\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}\\[3.00003pt]
&=\left\|\sym\nabla_{y}(\hat{E}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{*})\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}+\left\|\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}\right\|_{L^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}^{2}\geq|\boldsymbol{A}-x_{3}\boldsymbol{B}|^{2}.\end{split}

(85)

Integrating (
85
) over
I
and taking into account (
84
) and then (
82
), the claim follows.
∎

4.2
Proofs for Section
3.2

A. Proof of Proposition
3.5

Proof.

Notice first that using
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
as a test function in (
12
) immediately yields

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}\leq C\,\|\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

(86)

Next, we define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
by applying Theorem
A.19
to extend
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the whole domain
\Omega
and set

\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Furthermore, Theorem
A.19
and Lemma
A.22
imply

\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq Ca_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),\qquad h^{-2}\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq Ch^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}).

(87)

Combining Corollary
A.5
with (
87
), we obtain

\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq 2\|\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+2\|\pi_{1/h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}).

(88)

The claim in part 1 now follows directly from (
86
) and (
88
).

Proceeding to the proof of part 2, we notice that the fourth convergence in (
13
) is a direct consequence of (
87
) and Theorem
A.13
(1b).
To prove the first and second convergence in (
13
), we use Lemma
A.8
and (
87
).
Lemma
A.8
(3) now yields the following decomposition of the sequence
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:

\frac{1}{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}\\[3.00003pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}\mathfrak{b}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{a}_{1}\\[1.99997pt]
\mathfrak{a}_{2}\\[1.99997pt]
0\end{array}\right)+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\qquad\qquad\frac{1}{h}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=\iota\left(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}\right)+\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}},

where
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that
h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\longrightarrow 0
in
L^{2}.

To prove the third convergence in (
13
), we first assume that
\omega
has
C^{1,1}
boundary. By virtue of Lemma
A.10
(3), there are sequences
(\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
({\boldsymbol{o}}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
such that

\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=-x_{3}\iota\bigl(\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}}\bigr)+\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}+{\boldsymbol{o}}^{\varepsilon_{h}},

where

\displaystyle\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,\quad\nabla_{\hat{x}}\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,\quad\lVert\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C,

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,\quad\lVert\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{o}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0.

In view of Lemma
A.15
(1) and Theorem
A.13
(1a),
there exist

z\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))

such that (up to extracting a subsequence)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\nabla_{y}^{2}z(\hat{x},y),

\displaystyle\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)

\displaystyle{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x,y).

Introducing the function

\boldsymbol{\varphi}(x,y):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}z(\hat{x},y)\\[3.00003pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}z(\hat{x},y)\\[3.00003pt]
\delta^{-1}z(\hat{x},y)\end{array}\right)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x,y),

we have

\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}(x,y)=-x_{3}\iota\bigl(\nabla_{y}^{2}z(\hat{x},y)\bigr)+\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x,y),

from which the third convergence in (
13
) follows.

Next we can extend this result to the case of an arbitrary Lipschitz domain.
In the general case we can only conclude that since
h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}(x)
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
there exists
\mathcal{C}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
such that

h^{-1}\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\,\iota\bigl(\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})\bigr)+\mathcal{C}(x,y).

Take a sequence
(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of increasing domains with
C^{1,1}
boundary such that
\omega_{n}\subset\omega
,
\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\omega_{n}=\omega.
By the preceding analysis we conclude that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{n}\in L^{2}(\omega_{n};H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that

\mathcal{C}(x,y)=\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{2,\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{n}(x,y)\quad\mbox{a.e.}\ \hat{x}\in\omega_{n},\ (x_{3},y)\in I\times\mathcal{Y}.

Furthermore, notice that

\bigl\|\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{2,\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{n}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\omega^{n}\times I\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq\lVert\mathcal{C}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Finally, we extend
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{n}
by zero outside
\omega_{n}\times I
.
The claim follows from the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}(\Omega\times I)
is weakly closed, which in turn is a consequence of Korn’s inequality for functions in
\dot{H}^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3})
(see
[
26
, Theorem 6.3.8]
).

To prove part 3, we first notice that

\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{b}).

Using lower semicontinuity of convex functionals with respect to weak two-scale convergence and the definition of
a_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b}},
we conclude that
\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}(x,y)=0
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
and that
\mathcal{C}(x,\cdot)
solves the minimisation problem (
8
) with
\boldsymbol{A}=\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}(\hat{x})
and
\boldsymbol{B}=\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})
.

The strong two-scale convergence claim of part 3 as well as Remark
3.6
follow from the strict convexity of the tensors
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,2,
viewed as quadratic forms on symmetric matrices.
∎

B. Proof of Theorem
3.7

Proof.

We choose the test function
\boldsymbol{v}
in (
12
) to be of the form

\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}h\theta_{1}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{1}\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\\[3.00003pt]
h\theta_{2}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{2}\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\\[3.00003pt]
\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\end{array}\right)+h{\varepsilon_{h}}\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}\left(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right)+h\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\theta_{3}\in C_{\rm c}^{2}(\omega)
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\Omega;C^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})).

The arbitrary choice of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
and a density argument imply

\int\limits_{I}\int\limits_{Y_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{1}(y)\left[\iota\left(\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}-x_{3}\nabla^{2}_{\hat{x}}\mathfrak{b}\right)+C(\hat{x},\cdot)\right]:\sym\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}\,dydx_{3}=0\qquad{\rm a.e.\hat{x}\in\omega},

from which the effective tensor
\mathbb{C}_{\delta}^{\rm hom}
is then obtained. Another density argument and Proposition
3.5
now provide the validity of the equations (
15
).
The uniqueness of the solution to (
15
) follows from Lax-Milgram and Proposition
3.4
, while the last claim follows by energy considerations or by duality arguments
[
38
, Proposition 2.8]
, see also the proof of Theorem
3.35
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

It is not difficult to incorporate surface loads into the statement of Theorem
3.7
. Namely, if one adds to the right-hand side of (
12
) the term

\int_{\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{\theta}\,d\hat{x},\qquad\boldsymbol{\theta}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

where
\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\};\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the integral over
\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}
represents a sum of two integrals over
\omega,
and assumes that

\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{g}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3}),

then using the proof of Theorem
3.7
and Remark
A.23
, one concludes that the limit equations (
15
) have an additional term

\int_{\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\}}\langle\boldsymbol{g}\rangle(\hat{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x}-\int_{\omega}\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(\hat{x},1/2,\cdot)\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{*}(\hat{x},-1/2,\cdot)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rangle\right)\cdot\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{3}(\hat{x})\,d\hat{x},

in the first equation and

\int_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{g}(\hat{x},-1/2,y)\cdot\bigl(\mathring{\xi}_{1}(-1/2,y),\mathring{\xi}_{2}(-1/2,y),0\bigr)^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dy\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\int_{Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{g}(\hat{x},1/2,y)\cdot\bigl(\mathring{\xi}_{1}(1/2,y),\mathring{\xi}_{2}(1/2,y),0\bigr)^{\top}\,dy,

in the second equation.

C. Proof of Proposition
3.9
and Corollary
3.11

Proof.

The proof partially follows the proof of Proposition
3.5
. Part 1 is obtained immediately by plugging
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}={\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}
in (
12
).

Proceeding to part 2, we perform an extension procedure similar to that undertaken in the proof of Proposition
3.5
. Using Theorem
A.19
, we define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
as the extension of
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to the whole domain
\Omega
and then set

\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}-\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Theorem
A.19
and Lemma
A.22
now imply the estimates (
87
).

Next, we characterise the behaviour of the sequence
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}.
To this end, notice that Lemma
A.8
yields the following decomposition of the sequence
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:

\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}\\[3.00003pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}\\[3.00003pt]
h^{-1}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{a}_{1}\\[3.00003pt]
\mathfrak{a}_{2}\\[3.00003pt]
0\end{array}\right)+\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}},\qquad\quad\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=\iota\bigl(-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}+\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\bigr)+\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}},

where
\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
,
\mathfrak{a}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
and
h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow 0
in
L^{2}
. Since
{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}},
and hence
\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
as well, is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
(see Lemma
A.22
), we infer that

\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}=h\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}-h\psi_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0,

so consequently
\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}=0
. By Theorem
A.3
, we can decompose the third component as
\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=\hat{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{3}+\bar{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{3}
, where
\hat{\psi}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{3}=\int_{I}\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
and
\lVert\bar{\psi}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq Ch
. Thus, by two-scale compactness, we conclude that there exists
\mathfrak{b}\in L^{2}(\omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\tilde{u}_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)=\psi_{3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y).

Furthermore, by invoking Remark
A.7
and applying Lemma
A.14
(1), we note that
\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x},y)=\mathfrak{b}(\hat{x})
. The rest of the proof is analogous to that of Proposition
3.5
.

To prove Corollary
3.11
,
we invoke Remark
A.6
, Remark
A.21
, as well as the symmetries of the solution due to the assumption concerning the symmetries of the elasticity tensor.
∎

D. Proof of Theorem
3.10

Proof.

We begin by plugging in (
12
) test functions of the form

\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{1}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{1}\theta_{3}(\hat{x}))\\[3.00003pt]
\theta_{2}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{2}\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\\[3.00003pt]
\theta_{3}(\hat{x})\end{array}\right)+{\varepsilon_{h}}\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}\left(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right)+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\left(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{*}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\theta_{3}\in C_{\rm c}^{2}(\omega)
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\Omega;C^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C_{00}^{1}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})),

and using the compactness result from Proposition
3.9
. The rest of the argument follows the proof of Theorem
3.7
.
∎

E. Proof of Proposition
3.13
and Theorem
3.14

Proof.

To obtain part 1 of Proposition
3.13
, we plug
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
in (
12
). The rest of the proof of Proposition
3.13
and the proof of Theorem
3.14
follow the steps of the proofs of Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.7
, respectively.
∎

F. Proof of Proposition
3.17

Proof.

To prove part 1, we first plug in
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}={\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}
in (
12
). Next, using Theorem
A.24
, Corollary
A.5
, and Remark
A.26
, we obtain the following a priori bounds:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}=E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}+h^{2}\bigl\|\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\\[2.5pt]
-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\\[4.49997pt]
h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\end{array}\right)+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},

\displaystyle\|\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\bigl\|\nabla\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+{{{\varepsilon_{h}^{4}}}}\bigl\|\nabla^{2}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\bigl\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq Ca_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

\displaystyle h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\|\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\right),

where
\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.

Proceeding to part 2, we note that the first convergence in (
32
) follows directly from Theorem
A.3
and Remark
A.7
.
To prove the remaining convergence statements, by analogy with the argument of Proposition
3.5
we first assume that
\omega
has
C^{1,1}
boundary. Using Lemma
A.8
and Lemma
A.10
, 3 we have

\tilde{u}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=h^{-1}\varphi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+w^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\tilde{\psi}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},

where
(\varphi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
is bounded in
H^{2}(\omega)
,
(w^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\omega)
and
\tilde{\psi}_{3}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightarrow{L^{2}}0
. Since
h^{-1}\varphi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is bounded in
L^{2},
the first part of the second convergence in (
32
) follows from Lemma
A.14
(2). Furthermore, the first part of the third convergence in (
32
) follows from Remark
A.7
, Remark
A.9
, Theorem
A.13
(2) and Lemma
A.15
(1) (in addition to the more standard Lemma
A.8
and Lemma
A.10
(3).) The second and third parts of the third convergence statement in (
32
) need to be additionally combined with the second convergence statement in (
32
) through Lemma
A.15
(2). Finally, the fourth and fifth convergence in (
32
) follow from Lemma
A.15
(3) and Lemma
A.16
(1) by noticing that
\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow 0
in
L^{2}
as a consequence of the fact
h\ll{\varepsilon_{h}}
. This concludes the proof of part 2 for the case when
\omega

has
C^{1,1}
boundary. For the general case of
\omega
with Lipschitz boundary, we now use Lemma
A.10
(3), Theorem
A.13
(2) and Lemma
A.15
(1) in combination with the approach of the proof of Proposition
3.5
.

The argument for part 3 is analogous to that for Proposition
3.5
.
∎

G. Proof of Theorem
3.19

Proof.

The proof is carried out by taking appropriate vest functions
\boldsymbol{v}={\boldsymbol{v}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
in
12
and then passing to the limit as
h\to 0,
for which we invoke a combination of Proposition
3.17
, Remark
3.2
, and a density argument.

Different equations in (
34
) are obtained by using different kinds of test functions. For the first equation, we use test functions of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=\bigl(\theta_{1}(\hat{x}),\theta_{2}(\hat{x}),0\bigr)^{\top}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\,\left(\zeta_{1}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\zeta_{2}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),0\right)^{\top}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\frac{1}{h}\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)

\displaystyle+h\int_{0}^{x_{3}}{\boldsymbol{r}}\biggl(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\,dx_{3},

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C^{2}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
\psi\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\Omega;C^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.
Next, for the second equation we use test function of the form

v^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=\left(\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{1}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{2}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),0\right)^{\top}+\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{0}^{x_{3}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{r}}\biggl(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\,dx_{3},

where
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C_{\rm c}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{r}}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\Omega;C_{\rm c}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
.
Further, for the third equation (
\kappa=\infty
) we use test equation of the form

\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=(-hx_{3}\partial_{1}\theta(\hat{x}),-hx_{3}\partial_{2}\theta(\hat{x}),\theta(\hat{x}))^{\top}+\left(-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top},

where
\theta\in C_{\rm c}^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\xi}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C_{\rm c}^{2}(Y_{0}))
. For the fourth equation (
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
) we use test functions of the form

\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=\left(-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top},

where
v\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega,C^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
. For the fifth equation (
\kappa\in(0,\infty)
) we use test functions of the form

\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=\left(-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top},

\mathring{\xi}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega,C_{\rm c}^{2}(Y_{0}))
. Finally, for the sixth equation (
\kappa=0
) we use test functions of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)=\Biggl(-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),

\displaystyle-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),v\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\Biggr)^{\top}

\displaystyle+\left(-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\mathring{\xi}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top},

where
v\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
,
\mathring{\xi}\in C_{\rm c}^{1}(\omega;C^{2}_{\rm c}(Y_{0}))
.
The proof of the remaining claims follow an analogous part of the proof of Theorem
3.7
.
∎

H. Proof of Corollary
3.20

Proof.

The proof follows easily from Remark
A.6
, Remark
A.11
, and Remark
A.27
. ∎

I. Proof of Proposition
3.21

Proof.

Part 1 follows easily from Theorem
A.24
(in particular, (
139
)–(
141
)) and Corollary
A.5
, after plugging
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}={\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}
into (
12
).
To justify the scaling, notice that as a consequence of the above mentioned statements we have

\|\pi_{{\varepsilon_{h}}/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ch^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

see also the last expression in (
89
) below.
To prove part 2 we use Theorem
A.24
and Corollary
A.5
again and obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},

(89)

\displaystyle\left\|\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\right\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq{C}h^{-2}\bigl\|\sym\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq{C}h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

\displaystyle\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\\[2.5pt]
-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\end{array}\right)+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},

\displaystyle\bigl\|h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega)}+{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\bigl\|h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\nabla\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}+{\varepsilon}_{h}^{4}\bigl\|h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\nabla^{2}\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\bigl\|h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}

\displaystyle+{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\bigl\|h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq Ch^{-2}{{{\varepsilon_{h}^{4}}}}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq Ch^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

where
\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H^{2}(\omega)
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathring{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
Assuming first that
\omega
has
C^{1,1}
boundary, part 2 follows by using Lemma
A.8
, Lemma
A.10
(3), Theorem
A.13
(2), Lemma
A.15
(1,3), Lemma
A.16
(1), and Theorem
A.24
.
For general Lipschitz domains we follow the approach of Proposition
3.5
and Proposition
3.17
. Finally, part 3 is obtained the same way as part 3 of Proposition
3.5
. ∎

, and test functions of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl(h\theta_{1}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{1}\theta_{3}(\hat{x}),h\theta_{2}(\hat{x})-hx_{3}\partial_{2}\theta_{3}(\hat{x}),\boldsymbol{\theta}_{3}(\hat{x})\bigr)^{\top}+h{\varepsilon_{h}}\,\left(\zeta_{1}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\zeta_{2}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),0\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle+{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(-hx_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),-hx_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr),\psi\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top}+h^{2}\int_{0}^{x_{3}}{\boldsymbol{r}}\biggl(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\,dx_{3}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\left(\mathring{\xi}_{1}-x_{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\mathring{\xi}_{3}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right),\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\left(\mathring{\xi}_{2}-x_{3}\partial_{y_{2}}\mathring{\xi}_{3}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right),\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{3}\biggl(\hat{x},\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\right)^{\top}
