4.3
Proofs for Section
3.3

A. Proof of Theorem
3.32

Proof.

It is easy to see from Proposition
3.4
that the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}
is positive definite, coercive, and has compact inverse. This, in particular, allows one to obtain immediately a characterization of its spectrum, which we omit.

From Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.7
we infer that if
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in
L^{2},
then the sequence of solutions
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
of (
12
) for
\lambda=1
satisfies
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}
, where
\mathfrak{b}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
solves

\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}+\mathcal{I}\bigr)\mathfrak{b}=\langle\rho\rangle^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}_{3}.

Using the proof of
[
38
, Proposition 2.2]
, we show that the property
(H_{1})
in Definition
3.31
holds. To prove the property
(H_{2}),
we take a sequence
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
of eigenvalues of the operator
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
converging to
\lambda>0
. Next, consider the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
of the corresponding eigenfunctions

h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\quad\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}=1.

Multiplying the above equation by
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
using the compactness result from Proposition
3.5
, and invoking an argument similar to that of Theorem
3.7
, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow(0,0,\mathfrak{b})^{\top}
in
L^{2}
, where
\mathfrak{b}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{\rm D}}(\omega)
solves

\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}\mathfrak{b}=\lambda\mathfrak{b},\quad\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{L^{2}}=1,

which completes the proof of
(H_{2})
. This also proves the convergence of eigenfunctions.

To prove a refined version of the Hausdorff convergence concerning the convergence of eigenvalues ordered in the increasing order, we take an arbitrary closed curve
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{C}
, intersecting an interval in
(0,\infty)
and not passing through any of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{\delta,n}
and define the following projection operators:

P_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{1}{h^{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}-z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}dz,\quad P_{\Gamma}=-\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{\Gamma}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}-z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}dz.

We claim that for small enough
{\varepsilon_{h}}>0
the dimensions of the ranges
\mathcal{R}(P_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon_{h}})
and
\mathcal{R}(P_{\Gamma})
coincide. (Note that they are finite by the compactness of the resolvent.) Indeed, from the compactness result in Proposition
3.5
and Lebesgue theorem on dominated convergence it follows that if
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
(\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
are such that
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{f}
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}
weakly in
L^{2},
then one has

\bigl(P_{\Gamma}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)\to(P_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}).

It follows that
P_{\Gamma}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow P_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{f}
in
L^{2}.
This immediately implies that the dimensions of
\mathcal{R}(P_{\Gamma}^{\varepsilon_{h}})
and
\mathcal{R}(P_{\Gamma})
coincide for sufficiently small
{\varepsilon_{h}}.

Next, fix a closed curve
\Gamma_{\delta,n}\subset\mathbb{C}
containing in its interior the eigenvalue
\lambda_{\delta,n}
and no other eigenvalues, intersecting the real line at
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
such that
\lambda_{\delta,n-1}<w_{1}<\lambda_{\delta,n}<w_{2}<\lambda_{\delta,n+1}
, where we set
\lambda_{\delta,0}=0
. The multiplicity
k_{\delta,n}
of this eigenvalue equals
\dim\mathcal{R}(P_{\Gamma_{\delta,n}})
. By using the above claim, we know that for small enough
{\varepsilon_{h}}
exactly
k_{\delta,n}
eigenvalues of
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon_{h}}
(including their multiplicities) are contained in the interval
(w_{1},w_{2}).
∎

Before giving the rest of the proofs we will state and prove one helpful lemma:

Lemma 4.2
.

1.

If
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},
one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}).

2.

If
\delta\in(0,\infty]
,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}h,
one has

\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-2}\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}).

3.

If
\delta=0
,
\mu_{h}={{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}},
one has

\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-2}{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}h^{-2}\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}).

Proof.

We prove part 1 for the case
\delta\in(0,\infty)
only, as the cases
\delta=0
and
\delta=\infty
are dealt with by similar arguments. We take
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\in H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}
.
The convergence properties of
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}
immediately imply that the sequence

\Bigl(\|\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\|_{L^{2}}\Bigr)_{h>0}

is bounded.
5
5

5

Actually it can be concluded that the sequence

(\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\|_{H^{1}})_{h>0}

is bounded, see
[
17
, Section 7]
.

For each
h
we take a cube
Q^{h}=q^{h}\times I
such that
q^{h}\subset\omega
has vertices in
{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and side length
2n^{h}{\varepsilon_{h}}
, where
n^{h}
is an integer. Furthermore, we assume that
n^{h}{\varepsilon_{h}}
converge to some positive number as
h\to 0
. We define
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
as follows. Consider
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that the cube of size
{\varepsilon_{h}}
whose left corner is at
{\varepsilon_{h}}z
is contained in
q^{h}.
On the inclusion
{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times I,
we set
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
to be equal to
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}(\hat{x}/{\varepsilon_{h}}-z,x_{3})
if
z_{1}
(the first coordinate of
z
) is even and to
-\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}(\hat{x}/{\varepsilon_{h}}-z,x_{3})
if
z_{1}
is odd. We then extend
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
by zero outside
{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times I.
This procedure is repeated for all
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the above property, and finally
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is set to zero on
\Omega\setminus Q^{h}.

It can be easily checked that for
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
one has

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\Omega}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}\biggl(\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\xi}(x)\,dx-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{\Omega}\rho\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}

(92)

\displaystyle=\int_{Q^{h}\cap\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}\biggl(\frac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x)\,dx-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{Q^{h}\cap\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx,

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, with
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
being the extension provided by Theorem
A.19
. Recall that, as a consequence of Corollary
A.5
,

\bigl\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\bigr\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}},

where
C>0
does not depend on
h.
Using this fact and the definition of
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
(noting that the mean value of
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is zero on each two neighbouring small cubes of size
{\varepsilon_{h}}
in the
x_{1}
direction) it can be easily seen that the right hand side of (
92
) can be written in the form

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx+\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx,

(93)

where
\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(Q^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(Q^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3}),
and
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
,
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
h\to 0
.
To see this, we divide the domain into small rectangles containing two neighbouring cubes, where the first coordinate of the left corner is even and odd respectively, and apply the Poincaré inequality. This yields an estimate for the right-hand side of (
92
) by the expression
C{\varepsilon_{h}}(\|\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{L^{2}}),
where
C>0
is
h
-independent.
By using the Riesz representation theorem (applied first on the physical domain and then moved on the canonical domain) and the fact that on
Q^{h}
the norm

\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla_{h}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}
is equivalent to the norm

\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}+\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}},

we conclude that the right-hand side of (
92
) can be written in the form

{\varepsilon_{h}}\left(\int_{Q^{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x)\,dx+\int_{Q^{h}}\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx\right),

where
\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}
is bounded independently of
h
. The claim follows by taking
\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}={\varepsilon_{h}}\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}={\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{r}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
in (
93
).

To conclude the proof of part 1, we note that there exists
C>0
such that
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\geq C
and hence, by applying a suitable version of Lemma
A.57
(see also Remark
A.58
), one has

\dist\bigl(\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\bigr)\to 0\ \ {\rm as}\ \ h\to 0.

(94)

The proof of part 2 proceeds in a similar way. Part 3 requires an additional explanation while following the same kind of argument. We again take

\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\sigma({{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}h^{-2}\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\in H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}h^{-2}\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}
.
Using the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
3.32
(notice that here the
n
-th eigenvalue is of order
h^{-2}{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}
),
we infer immediately that
\bigl({\varepsilon_{h}}h^{-1}\|\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)_{h>0}
is bounded. Furthermore, invoking Corollary

A.5
, we obtain

\biggl\|\biggl(\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}\mathring{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r,1},\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}\mathring{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r,2},\mathring{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r,3}\biggr)\biggr\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}\Bigl\|\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{r}\Bigr\|_{L^{2}}

for some
C>0
independent of
h
. The rest of the proof follows the proof of part 1. ∎

Remark 4.3
.

Using a standard approach (resolvent convergence and compactness of eigenfunctions), it can be easily shown that when
\delta\in(0,\infty)
one has
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})
and
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})
. To obtain this result one needs to use uniform (in
\delta
) Korn inequality (see, e.g.,
[
17
, Section 7]
).

In the case
\delta=0
one can prove (similarly to the proof of Theorem
3.32
) that
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,0})
and
\lim_{h\to 0}{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}h^{-2}\sigma(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{00,0})
.

The analogous claim is not valid for
\delta=\infty.
This is the main reason why in this regime the limiting spectrum is different than the spectrum of the limit operator. Here, due to the fact that only resolvent convergence (as in Theorem
3.24
) holds and no compactness of eigenfunctions is available, one only has
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
,
\sigma({\mathcal{A}}_{00,\infty})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
.

B. Proof of Theorem
3.33

Proof.

The countability of the solutions of (
46
) is proved in Proposition
A.56
.

The equality (
47
) is proved in the same way as in
[
39
, Section 8]
, by analysing the resolvent equation for the limit operator.

The proof of the Hausdorff convergence consists of two parts: the statement
(H_{1})
is the direct consequence of the strong resolvent convergence established in Theorem
3.10
and Theorem
3.19
. The statement
(H_{2})
is proved by following the strategy of Theorem
3.32
: taking the sequence of the solutions to

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\quad\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}=1,

(95)

where
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
. One only needs to establish that the
L^{2}
weak limit of
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is not zero, so
(H_{2})
then follows by letting
{\varepsilon_{h}}\to 0
in (
95
). This claim is verified by proving that the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
converges strongly two-scale to the limit
\boldsymbol{u}
, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{u}.

(96)

Note that, due to Lemma
4.2
, one can assume without loss of generality that
\lambda\notin\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})=\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta})
.
One can then prove (
96
) in the same way as in
[
39
, Lemma 8.2]
, see also
[
12
, Theorem 6.2]
for an analogous proof in the stochastic setting as well as the proof of Theorem
3.35
below. It is important to emphasize that the proof requires strong convergence in
L^{2}
of the sequence of extensions
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})_{h>0}
, which can be ensured by imposing Assumption
2.1
(1) and using Corollary
3.11
and Corollary
3.20
.

The claim about the symmetry of
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}_{\delta}
is a direct consequence of Assumption
2.1
.
∎

C. Proof of Theorem
3.35

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of the proof of Theorem
3.33
. The analysis of the spectrum of limit operator is carried out as in
[
39
, Section 8]
, by studying the limit resolvent equations in Theorem
3.14
and Theorem
3.22
. Furthermore, in Theorem
3.22
we take
\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}=0
, which implies
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}=0
. Strong resolvent convergence is then obtained as the last statement in the mentioned theorem, and compactness of an appropriate sequence of eigenfunctions can be proved by invoking
[
39
, Lemma 8.2]
. The only fact we will additionally comment on is the strong two-scale convergence of the eigenfunctions in the regime
\delta=0.
We take
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\sigma(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that
\liminf_{h\to 0}\dist(\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},h^{-2}{{\varepsilon_{h}^{2}}}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}))>0
(this is again the only situation that requires special analysis, due to Lemma
4.2
) and
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
. Next, we take
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
, such that
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
In order to prove that
\lambda
is in the spectrum of the limit operator, we show that the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is compact in the sense of strong two-scale convergence.
We decompose
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
, where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
, where
E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is an extension given in Theorem
A.24
. In the same way as in Proposition
3.21
, we infer that (
89
) holds. Taking test function
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
that vanish on
\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int\limits_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}

\displaystyle\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)\,dx-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\rho\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx=

(97)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x)\,dx-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\rho\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx.

To prove the strong two-scale convergence, we shall use a duality argument. To this end, consider the identity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int\limits_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathbb{C}^{\mu_{h}}\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{z}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x):\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\rho\mathring{\boldsymbol{z}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx=\int_{\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\mathring{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\,dx,

(98)

\displaystyle\forall\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=0\textrm{ on }\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},

where
\mathring{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{z}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{z}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0
on
\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}.

Denoting by
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\rm c}
the solution of (
98
) with
\mathring{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\rho\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
subtracting (
98
) from (
97
), and using an appropriate version of Lemma
A.57
(see Remark
A.58
), we obtain that

{\varepsilon}_{h}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}-\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{c})\bigr\|_{L^{2}}+\bigl\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}-\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\rm c}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\to 0\ \ \textrm{ as }\ h\to 0.

(99)

Notice also that
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}_{*}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{2}}}{{\to}}0
as the consequence of apriori estimates, see also (
89
).
We now take
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Furthermore, we take
\mathring{\boldsymbol{s}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
as the solution of (
98
) with
\mathring{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
. Substituting
\mathring{\boldsymbol{s}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
as a test function in the equation for
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\rm c}
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{\rm c}
as a test function in the equation for
\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
we obtain by the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
3.22
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}\int\limits_{\omega\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}^{\rm bend,r}(y)\nabla_{y}^{2}{\mathring{u}}_{3}(\hat{x},y):\nabla_{y}^{2}{\mathring{{s}}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,d\hat{x}dy-\lambda\int\limits_{\omega\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y){\mathring{u}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{{s}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,d\hat{x}dy

\displaystyle\hskip 8.61108pt=-\lambda\int\limits_{\omega\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(y)\tilde{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{{s}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,d\hat{x}dy=\int\limits_{\omega\times Y_{0}}\overline{\mathring{g}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{{u}}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\,d\hat{x}dy,

where
\mathring{u}_{3}
,
\mathring{s}_{3}\in L^{2}(\omega;H_{0}^{2}(Y_{0}))
are weak two-scale limits of
\mathring{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{3},

\mathring{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{3}
while
\mathring{\boldsymbol{s}}_{*}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\longrightarrow 0
in
L^{2},
and
\tilde{u}_{3}\in H^{2}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega)
is the strong limit of
\tilde{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{3}
while
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{*}\longrightarrow 0
in
L^{2}
. It follows that

\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\mathring{\boldsymbol{g}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\,dx=-\lambda\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\rho\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\mathring{\boldsymbol{s}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\,dx=-\lambda\int\limits_{\Omega\times Y}\rho\tilde{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)\cdot\mathring{{s}}_{3}=\int_{\Omega\times Y}g_{3}(x,y)\mathring{u}_{3}(\hat{x},y)d\hat{x}dy.

Therefore, the sequence
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
and consequently
{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
converges strongly two-scale. Passing to the limit in the (weak formulation of the) equation
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
we immediately obtain
\hat{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\boldsymbol{u}=\lambda\boldsymbol{u}
, where
\boldsymbol{u}\neq 0
is the two-scale limit of
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
∎

D. Proof of Theorem
3.37

Proof.

The proof uses some ideas given in
[
2
]
adapted to the present, simpler, setup.

Step 1.
We prove (
51
).

By applying the Fourier transform, it is easily seen that the generalised eigenfunctions of
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}
are of the form

\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}(y_{1},y_{2},x_{3})=e^{i\eta x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}(y_{1},y_{2}),\quad\eta\in\mathbb{R},

(100)

where
\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}\in H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})

is an eigenfunction of the self-adjoint operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}
on
L^{2}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})
defined via the bilinear form

\displaystyle\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\int_{Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\bigl(\partial_{y_{1}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{y_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,{\rm i}\eta\boldsymbol{u}\bigr):\sym\bigl(\partial_{y_{1}}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\,|\,\partial_{y_{2}}\overline{\boldsymbol{v}}\,|\,\overline{{\rm i}\eta\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr)dy,

\displaystyle\hskip 43.05542pt\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}:H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})\times H_{0}^{1}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})\to\mathbb{C}.

It is easily seen that for each
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
the operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}
is positive definite and has compact resolvent, and thus it has an increasing sequence of eigenvalues
\{\alpha_{1}^{\eta},\alpha_{2}^{\eta},\dots\}
diverging to
+\infty
. It follows that

\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})=\bigcup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl\{\alpha_{1}^{\eta},\alpha_{2}^{\eta},\dots\bigr\}.

By using a suitable Korn’s inequality on the on
I\times Y_{0}
(applied to the function
(x_{3},y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto e^{i\eta x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}(y_{1},y_{2})
) and (
2
), we obtain that there exists a constant
C>0
, which is independent of
\eta
, such that

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\bigl(\partial_{y_{1}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{y_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,{\rm i}\eta\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\mathring{a}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3}).

(101)

Furthermore, using the characterisation of eigenvalues through a Rayleigh quotient, we obtain

\alpha_{1}^{\eta}=\min_{\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\frac{\mathring{a}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})}{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}.

(102)

Combining this with (
101
), we infer that there exists
c>0
, independent of
\eta
, such that

\alpha_{1}^{\eta}\geq c\min_{\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{0}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})}\frac{\left\|\bigl(\partial_{y_{1}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,\partial_{y_{2}}\boldsymbol{u}\,|\,{\rm i}\eta\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}.

Finally, using Poincaré’s inequality on
Y_{0},
we obtain the existence of
c>0
such that
\alpha_{1}^{\eta}\geq c+\eta^{2}
. The continuity of
\alpha_{1}^{\eta}
with respect to
\eta
(which can also be inferred from (
102
)) implies that the range of the mapping
\eta\mapsto\alpha_{1}^{\eta}
is
[m_{0},+\infty)
for some
m_{0}>0.
This concludes the characterisation of the set
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}),
provided by (
51
).

Step 2.
We prove (
50
).

Proceeding to the discussion of the sets
\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\pm}),
we show that they in fact coincide
with
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}).
The proof of this claim, for which we just provide a sketch, is similar to the argument of
[
2
, Proposition 7.5]
. Consider a Weyl sequence associated to
\lambda\in\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
, i.e.,

(\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
such that

\|\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\|_{L^{2}}=1,\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}{\,\xrightharpoonup{L^{2}\,}\,}0,\quad\bigl\|\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}-\lambda\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\to 0.

(103)

The properties (
103
) imply that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
H^{1}
. Next, take a smooth positive function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
that takes zero values on
(-\infty,1]
and is equal to unity on
[2,+\infty)
and show that
for all
v\in H^{1}_{00}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R})
one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\nabla(\psi\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}):\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(\psi\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

(104)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\nabla(\psi\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}):\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}(\psi\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle=\int_{[1,2]\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\bigl(0\,|\,0\,|\partial_{x_{3}}\psi\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\bigr):\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle+\int_{[1,2]\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}:\sym\bigl(0\,|\,0\,|\partial_{x_{3}}\psi\boldsymbol{v}\bigr)\,dx_{3}dy.

Combining (
103
) with compact embedding of
H^{1}
into
L^{2}
on bounded domains, we conclude that for all bounded sets
A
one has
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\|_{L^{2}(A)}\to 0
.
Furthermore, considering a smooth non-negative compactly supported function
\psi_{A}
that is equal to one on
A
and noting that by virtue of (
103
) one has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{0}^{+}\times Y_{0}}\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}-\lambda\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\bigr)\psi_{A}\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\to 0,

we obtain that actually
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\|_{H^{1}(A)}\to 0
.

Thus we conclude that the right-hand side of (
104
) can be written in the form

\int_{[1,2]\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{n}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy+\int_{[1,2]\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,

where
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
and
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. By combining a suitable version of Lemma
A.57
with (
104
), we conclude that
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})
. In a similar fashion, starting from the generalised eigenfunction (
100
), we conclude that
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
.

By repeating the above argument for
{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-},
we also obtain

\sigma_{\rm ess}\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}\bigr)=\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}\bigr).

This establishes the property (
50
).

Step 3.
We prove (
52
).

First, by virtue of the symmetries of the elastic tensor (and considering appropriate Weyl sequences), we easily obtain the equality
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})=\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-})
.
Next we show that

\sigma_{\rm ess}\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\bigr)\subset\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr),\qquad\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr)=\sigma\bigl(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr)

(105)

To show the first inclusion in (
105
), we take a Weyl sequence associated to the
\lambda\in\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
, i.e.,

(\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
such that

\|\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\|_{L^{2}}=1,\qquad\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}{\,\xrightharpoonup{L^{2}\,}\,}0,\qquad\bigl\|\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}-\lambda\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\to 0.

Using the elastic symmetries once again, we infer that for the functions

\boldsymbol{u}_{*}^{-,n}(x_{3},y):=\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}_{*}(-x_{3},y),\qquad u_{3}^{-,n}(x_{3},y):=-u^{+,n}_{3}(-x_{3},y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{0}^{+}\times Y_{0},

one has

\|\boldsymbol{u}^{-,n}\|_{L^{2}}=1,\qquad\boldsymbol{u}^{-,n}{\,\xrightharpoonup{L^{2}\,}\,}0,\qquad\bigl\|\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}\boldsymbol{u}^{-,n}-\lambda\boldsymbol{u}^{-,n}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\to 0.

We also note that the sequences
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\pm,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are bounded in
H^{1}
. We now define

\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(x_{3},y):=\psi(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{+,n}(x_{3},y)+\psi(-x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{-,n}(x_{3},y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0}.

In the same way as in (
104
), we conclude that for every
v\in H^{1}_{00}(\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R})
one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}:\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}Y_{0}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle=\int_{([1,2]\cup[-2,-1])\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{n}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy+\int_{([1,2]\cup[-2,-1])\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{n}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy,

where
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
and
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty,
from which it follows that
\lambda\in\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})
.

For the last equality in (
105
) it suffices to argue that

\sigma(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})
. To this end, we apply the Fourier transform and for
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
we consider generalised eigenfunctions of the operator
\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
of the form

\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}(x_{3},y)=e^{i\eta x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}(y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0},

where
\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}\in H^{1}_{00}(Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})

is an eigenfunction of the operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}
, i.e.,
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}(y_{1},y_{2})=\alpha_{i}^{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}(y_{1},y_{2})
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}\neq 0
, for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Invoking the symmetries, we infer that for each
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}

\boldsymbol{(}\boldsymbol{u}^{-\eta}_{\rm strip})_{*}(x_{3},y):=(\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip})_{*}(-x_{3},y),\qquad u_{\rm strip,3}^{-\eta}(x_{3},y):=-u^{\eta}_{\rm strip,3}(-x_{3},y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0},

is also a generalised eigenfunction of the operator
\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
associated with the same eigenvalue
\alpha_{i}^{\eta}
. Therefore, the function
\bigl(\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}+\boldsymbol{u}^{-\eta}_{\rm strip}\bigr)/2

is a generalised eigenfunction of the operator
\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
(and hence the operator
\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
) associated with the same eigenvalue. Since every element of the spectrum of the operator
\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}
coincides with
\alpha_{i}^{\eta}
for some
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude that
\sigma(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})
.
This construction also proves that
\sigma_{\rm ess}(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})=\sigma\bigl(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr)
.
Since we have already established that
\sigma\bigl(\mathring{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr)\subset\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\bigr),
the property (
52
) follows.

Step 4.
We prove (
49
).

We start by proving the inclusion

\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\rm strip}^{+})\cup\sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\rm strip}^{-}).

(106)

Let us take
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
and

\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\quad\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}=1.

Consider smooth positive functions
\psi_{i},

i=1,2,3
on
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}+\psi_{3}=1
,
\textrm{supp}\ \psi_{2}\subset[-1/4,1/4]
,
\textrm{supp}\ \psi_{1}\subset(-\infty,-1/8]
,
\textrm{supp}\ \psi_{3}\subset[1/8,\infty),
and
\psi_{3}(x_{3})=\psi_{1}(-x_{3})
.
Then there exists
i\in\{1,2,3\}
such that (up to a subsequence)

\bigl\|\psi_{i}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{3}\quad\forall h.

If
i=2
, we extend
\psi_{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

by zero on
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}
and, by scaling the variable
x_{3},
define

\boldsymbol{u}_{\rm strip}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x_{3},y)=\sqrt{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\psi_{2}\biggl(\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}x_{3}\biggr)\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}x_{3},y\biggr),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0}.

It is straightforward to see that

\bigl\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\rm strip}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{3}

and that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{00}(\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})
one has

\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\rm strip}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy-\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{u}_{\rm strip}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{v},

where
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
,
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
h\to 0
.
By using an appropriate analogue of Lemma
A.57
(see also Remark
A.58
) adapted to the operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}},
we conclude that

\lambda\in\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}\bigr).

(107)

If
i=1
or
i=3
we argue similarly that
\lambda\in\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}
, i.e.,
\lambda\in\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}
respectively.

Next, we prove that
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
. Considering
\alpha_{i}^{\eta}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{\eta}_{\rm strip})
such that

\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}=\alpha_{i}^{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta},\qquad\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}\|_{L^{2}}=1

we set

\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}(x_{3},y)=e^{i\eta x_{3}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}(y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times Y_{0}.

It is easily seen that
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}=\alpha_{i}^{\eta}\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}
.
We define

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x_{3},y)={\biggl\|\psi_{2}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3},y\biggr)\biggr\|_{L^{2}}}^{-1}{\psi_{2}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\eta}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}x_{3},y\biggr)},\qquad(x_{3},y)\in I\times Y_{0}.

It then follows easily that for every
h>0
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in H^{1}_{00}(I\times Y_{0};\mathbb{C}^{3})
one has

\displaystyle\int_{I\times Y_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{0}(y)\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}:\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

\displaystyle-\alpha_{i}^{\eta}\int_{I\times Y_{0}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy

(108)

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:\sym\nabla_{\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\boldsymbol{v}dx_{3}dy+\int_{I\times Y_{0}}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v},

where
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
,
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
h\to 0
. By using a result analogous to Lemma
A.57
(see also Remark
A.58
) we obtain

\dist\bigl(\alpha_{i}^{\eta},\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\bigr)\to 0\quad{\rm as}\ \ \ h\to 0.

(109)

It can be also easily deduced that
\sigma_{\rm disc}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
. Namely, for an eigenvalue
\alpha_{\rm strip}^{+}
of
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}
and associated eigenfunction
\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip}\|_{L^{2}}=1,
i.e.,

\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip}=\alpha_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip},
it can be easily shown that the sequence

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x_{3},y)={\biggl\|\psi_{1}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}+\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)\biggr\|_{L^{2}}}^{-1}{\psi_{1}(x_{3})\,\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha^{+}}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}+\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)},\qquad(x_{3},y)\in I\times Y_{0},

satisfies (
108
) with
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
,
\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0
as
h\to 0
and with
\alpha_{i}^{\eta}
replaced by
\lambda
. It follows that
\lambda\in\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
. In view of (
50
), we obtain the opposite inclusion in (
106
).

It remains to prove, under the Assumption
2.1
(1), the characterisation of
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
provided by (
49
).
By the same argument as in the case without planar symmetries, we obtain

\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)\subset\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip}\bigr)\cup\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\bigr)\cup\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}\bigr),

(110)

and
\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm strip})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
. By virtue of (
50
) and (
52
), it remains to prove the inclusion

\sigma_{\rm disc}\bigl(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\pm}\bigr)\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma\bigl(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr).

This will be done by a slightly different argument, as follows.
For
\alpha\in\sigma_{\rm disc}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
we take the associated eigenfunction
\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+})
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}\|_{L^{2}}=1,
of the operator
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+},
i.e.,
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{+}\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}=\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}
. Using the elastic symmetries, we infer that the functions
\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}
defined by

\boldsymbol{u}_{*}^{\alpha,-}(x_{3},y):=\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{*}(-x_{3},y),\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{3}^{\alpha,-}(x_{3},y):=-\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{3}(-x_{3},y),\qquad(x_{3},y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{0}^{+}\times Y_{0},

satisfy
\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-})
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-}\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}=\alpha\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}
.
Finally, we define

\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(x_{3},y)=\frac{\psi_{1}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}+\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)+\psi_{3}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}-\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)}{\biggl\|\psi_{1}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,+}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}+\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)+\psi_{3}(x_{3})\boldsymbol{u}^{\alpha,-}_{\rm strip}\biggl(\dfrac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggl(x_{3}-\dfrac{1}{2}\biggr),y\biggr)\biggr\|_{L^{2}}},\qquad(x_{3},y)\in I\times Y_{0},

and use an argument similar to that employed for showing that
\alpha\in\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
under no symmetry assumptions.

Similarly, we demonstrate that

\sigma_{\rm disc}(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{-})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

which concludes the proof of the opposite inclusion in (
110
). ∎

Remark 4.4
.

In the same way as in
[
2
, Proposition 7.5]
, it can be shown that eigenfunctions associated with eigenvalues in
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm strip}^{\pm})
have exponential decay at infinity.

E. Proof of Theorem
3.38
and Theorem
3.39

Proof.

The equality (
53
) is proved in the same way as in
[
39
, Section 8]
. The inclusion
\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
follows from resolvent convergence provided by Theorem
3.24
and Corollary
3.25
, while the inclusion
\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})=\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
follows from Theorem
3.37
and Lemma
4.2
.

It remains to show that
\lim_{h\to 0}\sigma({\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\subset\sigma(\mathring{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\rm strip})\cup\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty})
. To this end, consider
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\sigma(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that

\liminf_{h\to 0}\dist\bigl(\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\sigma(\mathring{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\bigr)>0

(111)

(which is the only case that requires analysis, due to Lemma
4.2
) and
\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\to\lambda
. Furthermore, consider
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
such that

\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\lambda^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
.
The strong two-scale compactness of
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is proved in the same way as in the proof of Theorem
3.35
by combining (
111
) with
Lemma
A.57
, see also Remark
A.58
.
The equation (
54
) is a direct consequence of the symmetry assumptions.

The proof of Theorem
3.39
is carried out in a similar fashion.
∎

4.4
Proofs for Section

A. Proof of Theorem
3.41

Proof.

It is not possible to put the first claim in the framework of Theorem
A.49
or Theorem
A.51
directly (i.e., using Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.7
) and we will provide a direct proof instead, using Laplace transform similarly to how it was done in the proofs of these theorems.
The reason why we cannot put the first claim in the framework of Theorem
A.49
or Theorem
A.51
directly comes from the fact that
\boldsymbol{f}_{*}\neq 0
and they influence the (quasistatic) behavior of the part of in-plane deformation.

For every
{\varepsilon_{h}}>0,
we write the system (
55
) for
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\tau=2
, using the formula (
156
), where
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}

is given by formula (
150
) and the associated operator
\mathcal{A}
is given by
h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
. Furthermore, we set
H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}^{1/2})=H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
H=L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
H_{0}=\{0\}^{2}\times L^{2}(\omega)
,
V=\{0\}^{2}\times\mathcal{D}((\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}})^{1/2})=\{0\}^{2}\times H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
. The space
H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is equipped with the
L^{2}
inner product with weight
\rho^{h}
, while the space
H
is equipped with the
L^{2}
inner product with weight
\rho.

In accordance with the abstract approach of Section
A.4
, for
\boldsymbol{v}\in V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
we set
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}:=\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{2}}
and, similarly, for
\boldsymbol{v}\in V
we set
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{V}:=\|(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\mathfrak{b},{\rm hom}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{2}}
. Furthermore, the convergence
\xrightharpoonup{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}
is given by two-scale convergence. Next, for
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we define the vectors
\boldsymbol{f}_{v}:=(0,0,f_{3})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{h}:=(\boldsymbol{f}_{*},0)^{\top}
.
We apply the estimate (
158
) to the case of the loads
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{v}
and initial conditions
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
and the estimate (
161
) to the case of the loads
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{h}
and zero initial conditions. This yields

\displaystyle\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}\leq

(112)

\displaystyle Ce^{\top}\Big(\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1}\|_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}

\displaystyle+\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathcal{I})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(0)\right\|_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}+\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathcal{I})^{-1/2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}\Big).

In order to obtain the boundedness of the last two terms in (
112
), notice that for
\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}^{*}
one has

\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\mathcal{I})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|^{2}_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}=h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})+(\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),

(113)

where
\boldsymbol{s}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
is the solution of the problem

h^{-2}a_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}({\boldsymbol{s}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{v})+({\boldsymbol{s}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\boldsymbol{v})_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}={\boldsymbol{l}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}.

(114)

Combining the result of Proposition
3.5
(1) with (
113
) and (
114
), we obtain the existence of
C>0
, independent of
h,
such that

\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|^{2}_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\leq C\|\pi_{h}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{H_{{\varepsilon}}},\qquad\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}.

(115)

Taking into account (
57
) and (
58
), this implies the stated boundedness property. Also, a consequence of (
57
) and (
58
), we have

\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightarrow{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}\boldsymbol{f}_{h},\quad\pi_{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{t,{\rm dr}-2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{h}.

(116)

From (
112
) and Corollary
A.5
we conclude that
\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T];V_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
and
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}),
and hence there exists
\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];V)
,
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];H)
such that

\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}\boldsymbol{u}_{l},\qquad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{l}.

As in Section
A.4
, we use the notation
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=(\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})
. Similarly, we introduce
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}
, as well as

\pi_{1/h}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\bigl(\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)^{\top},\quad\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\bigl(0,0,0,(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})^{\top}\bigr)^{\top},\quad\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}_{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\bigl(0,0,0,(\boldsymbol{f}_{v}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})^{\top}\bigr)^{\top},\quad\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\bigl(0,0,0,\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\bigr)^{\top}.

We then follow the proof of Theorem
A.49
or Theorem
A.51
. On the one hand, for every
\lambda>1,
we have

\pi_{1/h}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}(\pi_{1/h}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l})(\lambda)\quad{\rm as}\quad h\to 0,

where
\mathcal{L}
denotes the Laplace transform. On the other hand, by combining

\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)=(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)+(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}}_{0}\qquad\forall\lambda>1,

the representation (
153
), and Theorem
3.7
, we obtain

\pi_{1/h}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)=\mathcal{L}(\pi_{1/h}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}})(\lambda)\qquad\forall\lambda>1,

where
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}=({\mathfrak{a}}_{1}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{1},{\mathfrak{a}}_{2}-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}+\mathring{u}_{2},\mathfrak{b},0,0,\partial_{t}\mathfrak{b})^{\top}
, with the functions

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}
,
\mathfrak{b}
,
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
being the solutions of the equations (
65
)–(
67
) for the loads
\boldsymbol{f}
. It follows that
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}=\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}
.

The existence and uniqueness of the solution of the limit problem follows from Theorem
A.32
and Theorem
A.35
. Note that one can split the limit problem into two: the one with initial conditions
u_{0,3},

S_{1}P_{\delta,\infty}u_{1,3}
and out-of-plane loads, given by the part of
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{f})
depending on
\boldsymbol{f}_{3}
(where we apply Theorem
A.32
), and the one with zero initial conditions and in-plane loads, given by the part of
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{f})
depending on
\boldsymbol{f}_{\!*}
(where we apply Theorem
A.35
.) The last claim of the theorem follows by combining Theorem
A.50
applied to initial conditions
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
and loads
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{v}
and the second claim of Theorem
A.51
applied to initial conditions equal to zero and loads
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{h}
(using the resolvent compactness and convergence proved in Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.7
). The conditions (
173
) follow by applying (
115
) to
\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}=\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(0)
and
\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)=\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}_{h}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(t)
and integrating over the interval
[0,T]
.

∎

B. Proof of Corollary
3.42

Proof.

The proof follows from the first part of Theorem
A.51
for the weak convergence and from the second part of the same theorem for the strong two-scale convergence. We will just briefly outline the proof of the weak convergence. From (
161
) we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 21.52771pt\leq Ce^{\top}\Big(\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1/2}\mathcal{G}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})(0)\right\|_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}+\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1/2}\partial_{t}\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}})\right\|_{L^{1}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}})}\Big).

Similarly to the argument of Section A above (see (
116
)), we have

\pi_{h}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightarrow{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}\boldsymbol{g},\quad\pi_{h}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\xrightharpoonup{t,{\rm dr}-2}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{g}.

Furthermore, using (
113
) and (
114
)
we infer by Theorem
A.19
, Remark
A.23
, and Corollary
A.5
that for
\boldsymbol{l}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\in L^{2}(\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\};\mathbb{R}^{3})
one has

\left\|(h^{-2}\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\right\|^{2}_{H_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}}\leq\|\pi_{h}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega\times\{-1/2,1/2\};\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

The remainder of the argument follows the proof of Theorem
3.41
, using Remark
4.1
.
∎

C. Proof of Theorem
3.49
and Theorem
3.53

Proof.

The claims are established directly by applying Theorem
A.49
, Theorem
A.50
, and the results of Section

concerning resolvent convergence. For example, in the case

,
\mu_{h}={\varepsilon_{h}},

\tau=0
we set

,
\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}=\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
,

,

. The first part of the statement, which concerns weak two-scale convergence, is proved separately, by using the Laplace transform, Proposition

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Decompositions of symmetrised scaled gradients

Theorem A.1

(Korn inequalities)
.

[
29
]

Let
p>1
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and suppose that
\Gamma\subset\partial\Omega
has a positive measure. There exist positive constants
C_{\rm K}^{1}
,
C_{\rm K}^{2}
and
C_{\rm K}^{\Gamma},
which depend on
p
,
\Omega,
and
\Gamma
only, such that then the following inequalities hold for all
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in W^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}):

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{W^{1,p}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm K}^{1}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\right),

(117)

\displaystyle\inf_{\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\skeww},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{A}x-\boldsymbol{b}\|^{p}_{W^{1,p}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm K}^{2}\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{p}_{L^{p}},

(118)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{W^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm K}^{\Gamma}\left(\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}+\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\right).

(119)

Within this appendix, we will also use the following version of Korn’s inequality.

Proposition A.2
.

Suppose that
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
has Lipschitz boundary.
Then for every
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
one has

\biggl\|\boldsymbol{\psi}-\fint_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\psi}\biggr\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\bigl(\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}}+\dist(\psi,\mathcal{G})\bigr),

where
C>0
depends only on
\omega,

\mathcal{G}:=\bigl\{\nabla\phi:\phi\in H^{1}(\omega)\bigr\},

and the distance is understood in the sense of the
L^{2}
metric.

Proof.

The proof follows a standard contradiction argument. Suppose the claim is false, i.e., there exists a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\omega}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}

\displaystyle=0,

(120)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq n\bigl(\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}+\dist(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n},\mathcal{G})\bigr)\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(121)

Without loss of generality,

\|\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}=1.

(122)

and (
121
) can be written as

\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}+\dist(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n},\mathcal{G})\leq n^{-1}\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}.

By Korn’s inequality, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{n}{\rightharpoonup}\boldsymbol{\psi}
, weakly in
H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Combining this with (
121
), we infer that
\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathcal{G}
. From
\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
we obtain

\boldsymbol{\psi}=(-ax_{2},ax_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\boldsymbol{b},\quad a\in\mathbb{R},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Together with (
120
)
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathcal{G}
this implies
\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
, which contradicts (
122
). ∎

Theorem A.3

(Griso’s decomposition,
[
22
]
)
.

Let
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with Lipschitz boundary and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}).
Then one has

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{r}(x^{\prime})\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\hat{\psi}_{1}(x^{\prime})+\ r_{2}(x^{\prime})x_{3}+\bar{\psi}_{1}(x),\\[3.50006pt]
\hat{\psi}_{2}(x^{\prime})-r_{1}(x^{\prime})x_{3}+\bar{\psi}_{2}(x),\\[3.50006pt]
\hat{\psi}_{3}(x^{\prime})+\bar{\psi}_{3}(x),\end{array}\right.

(123)

where

\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}(x^{\prime})=\int_{I}\boldsymbol{\psi}(x^{\prime},x_{3}){\mathrm{d}}x_{3}\,,\quad\boldsymbol{r}(x^{\prime})=\frac{3}{2}\int_{I}x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\wedge\boldsymbol{\psi}(x^{\prime},x_{3}){\mathrm{d}}x_{3},

(124)

the following inequality holds for arbitrary
h>0,
with a constant
C>0
that depends on
\omega
only:

\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bigl\|\nabla_{h}\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}+h^{-2}\|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(125)

Remark A.4
.

Notice that

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}e_{3})\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}(\hat{\psi}_{1},\hat{\psi}_{2}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{12}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}(r_{2},-r_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

(126)

\displaystyle+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Thus from Korn’s inequality it follows

\displaystyle h^{2}\bigl\|\pi_{1/h}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\bigr\|^{2}_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle+\bigl\|(r_{1},r_{2}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

(127)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{r}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{2}\|\pi_{1/h}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{r}\wedge x_{3}\boldsymbol{e}_{3})\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{2}\|\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

The following corollary is the direct consequence of (
125
), (
127
), and Korn inequalities.

Corollary A.5

(Korn’s inequality for thin domains)
.

Suppose that
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is such that
\gamma\subset\partial\omega
has positive measure.
Then there exist constants
C_{\rm T},C_{\rm T}^{\gamma}>0
that depend on
\omega
and
\gamma
only, such that the following inequalities hold for all
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}):

\displaystyle\|\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm T}\left(\|\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\|\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\rm T}^{\gamma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\left(\|\pi_{1/h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+h^{-2}\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(128)

Remark A.6
.

If it is known that the components
\psi_{\alpha},

\alpha=1,2,
are even in
x_{3}
and
\psi_{3}
is odd in
x_{3}
, then additionally
\boldsymbol{r}=0
,
\hat{\psi}_{3}=0
.

Remark A.7
.

If a sequence of deformations
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}
is such that
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}
and
(\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}
are bounded in
L^{2}
and if
\boldsymbol{r}^{h}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}
are the terms in the decomposition (
123
), then the relations (
125
)–(
127
) imply that
\boldsymbol{r}^{h}\,\overset{H^{1}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,0
and
h\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\,\overset{H^{1}}{\to}\,0
.

The following lemma provides additional information on the weak limit of sequences with bounded symmetrised scaled gradients and is proved in
[
6
, Lemma A.4]
as a direct consequence of Griso’s decomposition.

Lemma A.8
.

Consider a bounded set
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with Lipschitz boundary. Suppose that a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}<\infty.

Then there exists a subsequence (still labelled by
h>0
) for which

\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{a}_{1}-x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathfrak{b}\\[2.5pt]
\mathfrak{a}_{2}-x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathfrak{b}\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}\mathfrak{b}\end{array}\right)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h},

in particular

\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}=\imath\bigl(-x_{3}\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\mathfrak{b}+\sym\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\bigr)+\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h},

for some

\mathfrak{b}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{2}(\omega)
,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the sequence
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H_{\gamma_{\rm D}}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})

satisfies
h\pi_{1/h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\overset{L^{2}}{\to}\,0
.

Remark A.9
.

It can be easily seen that
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}
is the weak limit of
(\hat{\psi}_{1}^{h},\hat{\psi}_{2}^{h}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in
H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})

and
\mathfrak{b}
is the weak limit on
h\hat{\psi}^{h}_{3}
in
H^{1}(\omega).
(More precisely, in this case
(-r^{h}_{2},r^{h}_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\overset{H^{1}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,x_{3}\nabla\mathfrak{b}
, where
\boldsymbol{r}^{h}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}
come from the decomposition (
123
) of
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}
.)

We will now establish a decomposition that can be viewed as a consequence of Griso’s decomposition. Note that another proof of the first part of Lemma
A.10
is given in
[
9
]
. The proof provided here uses the strategy of
[
37
]
.

Lemma A.10
.

Suppose that
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a connected set with
C^{1,1}
boundary and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

1.

There exist
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}_{\skeww}
,
v\in H^{2}(\omega)
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{B}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}\\[2.5pt]
x_{2}\\[2.5pt]
hx_{3}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}v\\[2.5pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}v\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}v\end{array}\right)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}},

(129)

and the estimate

\|v\|^{2}_{H^{2}}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C(\omega)\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

holds for some
C(\omega)>0.

2.

If
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
on
\partial\omega\times I
, then in (
129
) one can take
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{B}=0
. In addition,
v,

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}
can be chosen so that
v=\nabla v=0
on
\partial\omega
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\partial\omega\times I
.

3.

If a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
is such that

h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\overset{L^{2}}{\to}\,0,\qquad\qquad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}<\infty,

then there exist sequences
(\varphi^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{2}(\omega)
,
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})

such that

\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}=-x_{3}\iota(\nabla_{\hat{x}}^{2}\varphi^{h})+\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}+o^{h},

where
(o^{h})_{h>0}\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
is such that
o^{h}\,\overset{L^{2}}{\to}\,0
, and the following properties hold:

\lim_{h\to 0}\left(\|\varphi^{h}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}}\right)=0,\qquad\qquad\limsup_{h\to 0}\left(\|\varphi^{h}\|_{H^{2}}+\|\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\leq C\limsup_{n\to\infty}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}},

where
C>0
depends on
\omega
only.
Moreover, one has

\psi^{h}_{3}=h^{-1}\varphi^{h}+w^{h}+\tilde{\psi}_{3}^{h},

where
w^{h}\in H^{1}(\omega)
with

\|w^{h}\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\left(\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}+\bigl\|h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}\right),

for some
C>0
that depends on
\omega
only.

Proof.

We decompose
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
123
). From (
118
), (
125
) and Proposition
A.2
we conclude that there exist
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\biggl\|\boldsymbol{r}-\fint_{\omega}\boldsymbol{r}\biggr\|^{2}_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle+\bigl\|(\hat{\psi}_{1},\hat{\psi}_{2}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-(-cx_{2},cx_{1})^{\top}-\boldsymbol{d}\bigr\|_{H^{1}}+h^{-2}\bigl\|\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bigl\|\nabla_{h}\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\bigl\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We do the regularization of
\boldsymbol{r}
, i.e.,
we look for the solution of the problem

\min_{\varphi\in H^{1}(\omega),\ \int_{\omega}\varphi=h\int_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{3}}}\int_{\omega}\bigl|\nabla_{\hat{x}}\varphi+(r_{2},-r_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}.

(130)

The Euler-Lagrange equation and the associated boundary conditions for the problem (
130
) read

\displaystyle-\Delta^{\prime}\varphi=\nabla_{\hat{x}}\cdot(r_{2},-r_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \ \text{in}\ \omega,\quad\qquad\partial_{\nu}\varphi=-(r_{2},-r_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot\nu\ \ \text{on}\ \partial\omega.

Since
\nabla_{\hat{x}}\cdot(r_{2},-r_{1})\in L^{2}
, by
standard regularity estimates we obtain the inclusion
\varphi\in H^{2}(\omega)
and the estimate

\biggl\|\varphi-h\fint_{\Omega}\hat{\psi}_{3}\biggr\|_{H^{2}(\omega)}\leq C(\omega)\|\boldsymbol{r}\|_{H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})},

(131)

for which we require the
C^{1,1}
regularity of
\partial\omega
. In particular, one has

\left\|\varphi+\fint_{\omega}r_{2}x_{1}-\fint_{\omega}r_{1}x_{2}-h\fint_{\omega}\hat{\psi}_{3}\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left\|\boldsymbol{r}-\fint_{\omega}\boldsymbol{r}\right\|_{H^{1}}.

Furthermore, from (
130
) we have the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{1}\varphi+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{2}\varphi-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\hat{x}}\bigl(\hat{\psi}_{3}-h^{-1}\varphi\bigr)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq h^{-2}\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\|\partial_{1}\varphi+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+h^{-2}\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\|\partial_{2}\varphi-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}).

The claim follows by taking

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a}

\displaystyle=\biggl(d_{1},d_{2},\fint_{\omega}\hat{\psi}_{3}\biggr)^{\top},\quad\boldsymbol{B}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-c&h^{-1}{\fint r_{2}}\\[3.00003pt]
c&0&-h^{-1}{\fint r_{1}}\\[3.00003pt]
-h^{-1}{\fint r_{2}}&h^{-1}\fint r_{1}&0\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle v=\varphi+\fint_{\omega}r_{2}x_{1}-\fint_{\omega}r_{1}x_{2}-h\fint_{\omega}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{3},

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=\bar{\psi}+\left(\hat{\psi}_{1},\hat{\psi}_{2},\hat{\psi}_{3}-h^{-1}\varphi\right)^{\top}+\left(x_{3}(r_{2}+\partial_{1}\varphi),x_{3}(-r_{1}+\partial_{2}\varphi),0\right)^{\top}-(-cx_{2},cx_{1},0)^{\top}-(\boldsymbol{d},0)^{\top}.

This finishes the proof of part 1 of the lemma. To prove part 2, we only need to note that if
\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
on
\partial\omega
, then
\boldsymbol{r}=0
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\partial\omega
.
Combining this (
126
), we infer

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{r}\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\|(\hat{\psi}_{1},\hat{\psi}_{2}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle+h^{-2}\|\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}+\|\nabla_{h}\bar{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 43.05542pt+h^{-2}\|\partial_{1}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})+r_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+h^{-2}\|\partial_{2}(h\hat{\psi}_{3})-r_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Furthermore, due to the condition
\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
on
\partial\omega,
in the “regularisation” of
({r_{2}},-{r}_{1}){}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
provided by the variational problem (
130
) we can minimise over
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\omega)
and we immediately obtain that

\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}}\leq C(\omega)\|\boldsymbol{r}\|_{H^{1}},

which replaces (
131
).

Part 3 is proved in
[
37
]
; alternatively, one can follow the argument used for part 1, taking into account (
127
) and noting that
h\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\to 0
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
implies the convergence
\boldsymbol{r}^{h}{\rightharpoonup}0
in
H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
h\pi_{1/h}\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}{\rightharpoonup}0
in
H^{1}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
where
r^{h}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}
are from the decomposition (
123
) applied to
{\boldsymbol{\psi}}^{h}.
(Note that within the described argument here one can set to zero
the vectors
\boldsymbol{a}^{h},

\boldsymbol{B}^{h}
in the decomposition (
129
) for
\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}.
)

∎

Remark A.11
.

Following Remark
A.6
, we note that if
\psi_{\alpha},

\alpha=1,2
, are even in the variable and
\psi_{3}
is odd in
x_{3}
variable, then on has
v=0
,
a_{3}=0
,
B_{13}=B_{23}=0
in (
129
). Moreover, the estimate

\|\psi_{3}\|_{L^{2}}=\|\tilde{\psi}_{3}\|_{L^{2}}=\biggl\|\tilde{\psi}_{3}-\fint_{I}\tilde{\psi}_{3}\biggr\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\partial_{3}\tilde{\psi}_{3}\|_{L^{2}}\leq Ch\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{L^{2}}.

holds with
C>0
that depends on
\omega
only.

A.2
Two-scale convergence

In this chapter we assume that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if not otherwise stated, is a bounded open set with Lipschitz boundary.
As before, we use the notation
I=(-{1/2},{1/2})
.
For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
we denote by
\hat{x}
the first
n-1
coordinates, thus
x=(\hat{x},x_{n})
. Depending on the context, the unit cell is
Y=[0,1)^{n}
or
Y=[0,1)^{n-1},
while
\mathcal{Y}
denotes the unit flat torus in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n-1},
respectively.

Definition A.12
.

(Dimension-reduction two-scale convergence).
Let
(u^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. We say that
u^{\varepsilon}
weakly two-scale converges to
u\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
with respect to
Y
if (in settings where
Y=[0,1)^{n-1}
)

\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}(x)\phi\biggl(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,dx\,\xrightarrow{\,}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y}u(x,y)\phi\left(x,y\right)\,dydx\qquad\forall\phi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}\bigl(\Omega;C(\mathcal{Y})\bigr),

or (in settings where
Y=[0,1)^{n}
)

\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}(x)\phi\biggl(x,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,dx\,\xrightarrow{\,}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y}u(x,y)\phi\left(x,y\right)\,dydx\qquad\forall\phi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}\bigl(\Omega;C(\mathcal{Y})\bigr).

We write

u^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}u(x,y).

Furthermore, we say that
(u^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
strongly two-scale converges to
u\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
if

\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}(x)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x)\,dx\to\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y}u(x,y)\phi\left(x,y\right)\,dydx,

for every weakly two-scale convergent sequence
\phi^{\varepsilon}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\phi(x,y)
. We write

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}u(x,y).\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

The following theorem is given in
[
26
, Theorem 6.3.3]
.

Theorem A.13
.

Let
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is bounded and has Lipschitz boundary, and
let
{\varepsilon_{h}}>0
be a sequence such that
{\varepsilon_{h}}\to 0
as
h\to 0
so that
\lim_{h\to 0}h/{\varepsilon_{h}}=\delta\in[0,\infty]
. Let
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}
be a weakly convergent sequence in
H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
with limit
\boldsymbol{u}
and suppose that

\limsup\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}||\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}||_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}<\infty.

(132)

1.

(a)

If
\delta\in(0,\infty)
then there exists a function
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and a subsequence (not relabelled) such that

\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}(\hat{x})\,|\,0\bigr)+\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{w}(x,y).

(b)

If
\delta\in(0,\infty)
and in addition (
132
) we assume that

\limsup\limits_{h\rightarrow 0}h^{-1}\bigl\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}<\infty,

then there exists a function
\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(I\times\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and a subsequence (not relabeled) such that

h^{-1}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{w}(x,y),\quad\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\widetilde{\nabla}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{w}(x,y).

2.

If
\delta=0
then there exits

\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
and
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}(\hat{x})\,|\,0\bigr)+\bigl(\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{w}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}\bigr).

3.

If
\delta=\infty
then there exists

\boldsymbol{w}\in L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that

\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon_{h}}(x){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\bigl(\nabla_{\hat{x}}\boldsymbol{u}(\hat{x})\,|\,0\bigr)+\bigl(\nabla_{y}\boldsymbol{w}\,|\,\boldsymbol{g}\bigr).

We will need the following helpful lemma.

Lemma A.14
.

1.

Suppose that
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\varphi(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})
. Suppose additionally that
{\varepsilon}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}\to 0
strongly in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Then
\varphi(x,y)
depends on
x
only.

2.

Suppose
(\varphi^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset H^{2}(\Omega)
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\varphi(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})
. Suppose additionally that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}\to 0
strongly in
H^{2}(\Omega)
. Then
\varphi(x,y)
depends on
x
only.

Proof.

We write
