\varphi(x,y)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}a_{k}(x)\exp\bigl(2\pi{\rm i}(k,y)\bigr),\qquad a_{k}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\int|a_{k}(x)|^{2}<\infty.

We want to show that for
k\neq 0
we have that
a_{k}=0
. We take an arbitrary
b\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
k_{i}\neq 0
and calculate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}a_{k}(x)b(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times Y}\varphi(x,y)b(x)\exp\bigl(2\pi{\rm i}(k,y)\bigr)dxdy

\displaystyle=\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}(x)b(x)\exp\biggl(2\pi{\rm i}\biggl(k,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\biggr)dx

\displaystyle=\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi{\rm i}k_{i}}\varphi^{\varepsilon}(x)b(x)\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\exp\biggl(2\pi{\rm i}\biggl(k,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\biggr)\right)dx

\displaystyle=-\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left\{\int_{\Omega}\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi{\rm i}k_{i}}\partial_{x_{i}}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}(x)b(x)\exp\biggl(2\pi{\rm i}\biggl(k,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\biggr)+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi{\rm i}k_{i}}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}\partial_{x_{i}}b(x)\exp\biggl(2\pi{\rm i}\biggl(k,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\biggr)\right\}=0.

From this we infer that for
k\neq 0
,
a_{k}=0
, and the claim follows. The proof of the second claim is similar.
∎

The following claim can be proved directly by integration by parts.

Lemma A.15
.

1.

Let
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset H^{2}(\Omega)
be a bounded sequence. Suppose that

\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}\to\varphi_{0}
strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\nabla^{2}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\boldsymbol{\psi}
, where
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Then there exists
\varphi_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
such that

\nabla^{2}\varphi^{{\varepsilon}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\nabla^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)+\nabla^{2}_{y}\varphi_{1}(x,y).

2.

Suppose that
(\varphi^{\varepsilon_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{2}(\Omega)
is a bounded sequence such that
h^{-1}\varphi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\varphi(x,y)
and
\lim_{h\to 0}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}^{-2}h=\kappa\in[0,\infty).

Then
\nabla^{2}\varphi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\kappa\nabla^{2}_{y}\varphi(x,y).

3.

(a)

Let
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset H^{2}(\omega)
be such that the sequences
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
,
(\varepsilon\nabla\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
are bounded in the corresponding
L^{2}
spaces. Suppose that
\varphi^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\varphi(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
. Then
\varphi\in L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
and
{\varepsilon}\nabla\varphi^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\nabla_{y}\varphi(x,y)
.

(b)

Let
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset H^{2}(\omega)
be such that the sequences
(\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
,
(\varepsilon\nabla\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
,
({\varepsilon}^{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
are bounded in
L^{2}.
Suppose that
\varphi^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\varphi(x,y)\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)
. Then
\varphi\in L^{2}(\Omega;H^{2}(\mathcal{Y}))
and
{\varepsilon}\nabla\varphi^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\nabla_{y}\varphi(x,y)
,
{\varepsilon}^{2}\nabla^{2}\varphi^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\nabla^{2}_{y}\varphi(x,y)
.

We will prove the following lemma.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
\Omega=\omega\times I
, where
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
a bounded set with Lipschitz boundary and let
(\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
be such that there exists
C>0
such that

\|\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+{\varepsilon}_{h}^{2}\|\nabla_{h}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}\leq C.

(133)

1.

If
h\ll{\varepsilon}_{h}
then
there exist
\psi_{1}\in H^{1}(\omega\times\mathcal{Y})
,
\psi_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})
such that (on a subsequence)

\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\psi_{1},\quad{\varepsilon}_{h}\nabla_{h}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi_{1},\partial_{y_{2}}\psi_{1},\psi_{2}).

(134)

The opposite claim is also valid, i.e., for every
\psi_{1}\in H^{1}(\omega\times\mathcal{Y})
,
\psi_{2}\in H^{1}(\Omega\times\mathcal{Y})
we have that
there exists
(\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
such that (
133
) and (
134
) are satisfied.

2.

If
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h
then there exists
\psi\in L^{2}(\Omega,H^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
such that

\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\psi,\quad{\varepsilon}_{h}\nabla_{h}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi,\partial_{y_{2}}\psi,0).

(135)

The opposite claim is also valid, i.e., for every
\psi\in L^{2}(\Omega;H^{1}(\mathcal{Y};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
there exists
(\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}})_{h>0}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
such that (
133
) and (
135
) hold.

Proof.

To prove the first part of the lemma,
we take
\psi_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\psi_{1}
on a subsequence. Since, by assumption,

\|\partial_{x_{3}}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\frac{h}{{\varepsilon}_{h}},

we immediately conclude that
\psi_{1}

does not depend on
x_{3}
. Denote the two-scale limit of

h^{-1}{\varepsilon}_{h}\partial_{x_{3}}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}

by
\psi_{2}.
Invoking integration by parts in a standard fashion, it is easy to check that

{\varepsilon}_{h}(\partial_{1}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\partial_{2}\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi_{1},\partial_{y_{2}}\psi_{1}).

In order to prove the second claim of part 1, it suffices to consider the case
\psi_{1}\in C^{1}(\omega;C^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
,
\psi_{2}\in C^{1}(\Omega;C^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
. We can then take

\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\psi_{1}\biggl(x_{1},x_{2},\frac{x_{1}}{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\frac{x_{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\biggr)+\frac{h}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\int_{-1/2}^{x_{3}}\psi_{2}\biggl(x_{1},x_{2},s,\frac{x_{1}}{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\frac{x_{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{h}}\biggr)\,ds.

This completes the proof of part 1.

To prove part 2, we take
\psi\in L^{2}(\Omega\times Y;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}\psi
on a subsequence. Again, using integration by parts, we obtain

{\varepsilon}_{h}(\partial_{1}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}},\partial_{2}\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}){\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}(\partial_{y_{1}}\psi,\partial_{y_{2}}\psi).

Next, for
b\in C_{0}^{1}(\Omega),v\in C^{1}(\mathcal{Y})
we have

\int_{\Omega}\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}\partial_{x_{3}}\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}b(x)v\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\,dx=-\int_{\Omega}\frac{{\varepsilon_{h}}}{h}\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\partial_{x_{3}}b(x)v\biggl(\dfrac{\hat{x}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)\,dx\to 0.

It follows that
\partial_{x_{3}}\psi^{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}0
. To prove the last claim, we set

\psi^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}:=\psi\biggl(x,\frac{x_{1}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\frac{x_{2}}{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\biggr)

for
\psi\in C^{1}(\Omega,C^{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
and pass to the limit as
h\to 0.

∎

The definition of two-scale convergence (Definition
A.12
) naturally extends to time dependent spaces.

Definition A.17
.

Let
(u^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
. We say that
(u^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
weakly two-scale converges to
u\in L^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega\times Y)),
and write

u^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightharpoonup{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}u(t,x,y),

if

\int\limits_{0}^{T}\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\phi\biggl(x,\frac{\hat{x}}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,\varphi(t)\,dxdt\,\xrightarrow{\,}\int\limits_{0}^{T}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y}u(t,x,y)\phi\left(x,y\right)\varphi(t)\,dydxdt,

i.e.,

\int\limits_{0}^{T}\int\limits_{\Omega}u^{\varepsilon}(t,x)\phi\biggl(x,\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,\varphi(t)\,dxdt\,\xrightarrow{\,}\int\limits_{0}^{T}\int\limits_{\Omega}\int\limits_{Y}u(t,x,y)\phi\left(x,y\right)\varphi(t)\,dydxdt,

for every
\phi\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\Omega;C(\mathcal{Y}))
,
\varphi\in C(0,T)
.
If in addition one has

u^{{\varepsilon}}(t,x){\,\xrightarrow{{\rm dr}-2\,}\,}u(t,x,y)\ \ \ \ \textrm{a.e.}\ \ t\in[0,T]

(136)

and

\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|u^{{\varepsilon}}(t,\cdot)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,dt\to\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|u(t,\cdot)\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,dt,

then we will say that
(u^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
strongly two-scale converges to
u
and write

u^{\varepsilon}{\,\xrightarrow{{\rm t,dr}-2\,}\,}u(t,x,y).

Similarly, we define the notions of weak two-scale convergence and strong two-scale convergence of sequences in
L^{p}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
, for any
1\leq p<\infty,
denoted by
\xrightharpoonup{t,p,{\rm dr}-2}
and
\xrightarrow{t,p,{\rm dr}-2},
respectively. The convergence
\xrightharpoonup{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}
is understood in the weak* sense with respect to the time variable
t
, while
\xrightarrow{t,\infty,{\rm dr}-2}
is understood in the sense of simultaneous pointwise convergence (
136
) and boundedness of
\|u^{{\varepsilon}}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

t\in[0,T],
in the space
L^{\infty}(0,T)
.
The following lemma is standard (see, e.g., the proof of
[
30
, Lemma 4.7]
.

Lemma A.18
.

If
(u^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{p}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
,
p>1,
then it has a subsequence that converges weakly two-scale in the sense of Definition
A.17
.

A.3
Extension theorems

A.3.1
Asymptotic regime
h\sim\varepsilon_{h}

We use the extension theory in order to decompose the sequence of displacements into two functions for which we can get enough compactness for passing to the limit as
h\to 0
in the original equations for displacements.
We use the following result, which can be found in
[
28
]
:

Theorem A.19
.

For every
h>0
there exists a linear extension operator
\tilde{\cdot}:H^{1}(\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}};\mathbb{R}^{3})\to H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}
on
\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
and

\displaystyle\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

Proof.

The way in which we introduce the extensions here is to look at every single cell inside the thin domain
\Omega^{h}.
The extension of the function
\boldsymbol{u}
(defined on
\Omega^{h}
) is constructed as follows. First we “inflate” the cell (with the scaling factors
\varepsilon_{h}^{-1}
and
h^{-1}
in the in-plane and out-of plane directions, respectively) and translate it to the reference cell
Y\times I
. On this reference cell we apply a linear extension operator
E:H^{1}(Y_{1}\times I)\to H^{1}(Y\times I)
(see
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
to extend
\boldsymbol{u}
to the function
E\boldsymbol{u}=:\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}
. By passing to the original coordinates and concatenating the extensions we construct functions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3})
which is the extension of
\boldsymbol{u}
from
\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1}
to
\Omega^{h}
and satisfy the estimate

\left\|\sym\nabla\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

where the constant
C
does not depend on the thickness
h
. We finish the proof by rescaling the estimates back to
\Omega
.
∎

Remark A.20
.

It is not difficult to see that if we have a sequence
\{(h,{\varepsilon_{h}})\}
such that
0<\alpha<h/{\varepsilon_{h}}<\beta<\infty
, where
\alpha,\beta
do not depend of
h
, then the constant in Theorem
A.19
depends on
\alpha,\beta
only.

Remark A.21
.

By inspecting the construction of the extension operator in
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
, it can be easily seen that if
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
or
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
, then the same inclusion holds for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}
.

Using the result above we conclude the following lemma.

Lemma A.22
.

For all
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,

the following Poincaré and Korn inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\left\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C{\varepsilon_{h}}\left\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}},

(137)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}},

(138)

where the constant
C
is independent of
h.

Proof.

We again look the problem on the physical domain
\Omega^{h}
. The function
\boldsymbol{u}
(scaled properly), when restricted to a single cell within the domain and then rescaled and translated to
Y\times I,
satisfies the Poincare inequality, as well as Korn’s inequality (
119
) with a constant determined by the domain
Y\times I
. Scaling back to the physical domain
\Omega^{h}
and summing up the norms over all cells, we obtain a version of the estimates (
137
) and (
138
) for
\Omega^{h}
. Finally, rescaling to
\Omega,
we obtain (
137
) and (
138
).
∎

Remark A.23
.

Using continuity of embeddings into spaces on the boundary, it follows immediately that

\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap\overline{\Omega}_{0})}\leq C{\varepsilon_{h}}\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

where
\Gamma:=\omega\times\{-{1/2},{1/2}\}
.

A.3.2
Asymptotic regime
h\ll\varepsilon_{h}

In this section we assume that
h\ll\varepsilon_{h}
.
We will assume that
Y_{0}\subset Y
does not touch the boundary of
Y
and is of class
C^{1,1}
.
First, we provide an extension property, in the spirit of Theorem
A.19
. We denote by
\Omega_{\alpha}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
,
\alpha=1,2
, the same sets as in Section
2
.
We have the following theorem.

Theorem A.24
.

There exists a linear extension
E^{\varepsilon_{h}}:H^{1}(\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}};\mathbb{R}^{3})\to H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3})
,
E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}
on
\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
and

\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

(139)

for some
C>0
independent of
h
.
Moreover, there exist
\mathring{v}\in H^{2}(\omega)
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\mathring{v}=\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}=0
on
\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
and

\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}:=\boldsymbol{u}-E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{1}\mathring{v}\\[2.5pt]
-{\varepsilon_{h}}x_{3}\partial_{2}\mathring{v}\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}{\varepsilon_{h}}\mathring{v}\end{array}\right)+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}},

(140)

with the estimate

{\varepsilon_{h}}^{-2}\|\mathring{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\mathring{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon_{h}^{2}\bigl\|\nabla^{2}\mathring{v}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+{\varepsilon_{h}}^{-2}\bigl\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\bigl\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\bigl\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

(141)

where
C
depends on
Y_{0}
only.

Proof.

We consider a domain
\tilde{Y}_{0}\times(h/\varepsilon_{h})I
, such that
\tilde{Y}_{0}
has
C^{1,1}
boundary,
\overline{Y}_{0}\subset\tilde{Y}_{0}
, and
\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}
is connected.
For the extension
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
we apply the decomposition of Part 1 of Lemma
A.10
to obtain

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{B}(x_{1},x_{2},\varepsilon_{h}^{-1}hx_{3})^{\top}+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}v\\[2.5pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}v\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}\varepsilon_{h}v\end{array}\right)+\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}},

(142)

where
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}_{\skeww}
,
v\in H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\in H^{1}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
and the following estimate holds:

\|v\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})}+\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|\sym\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

(143)

where
C
depends on
Y_{0}
only.
It is not difficult to construct extension operators

E_{1}:H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\to H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}),\qquad E_{2}:H^{1}\bigl((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I\bigr)\to H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\times I)

such that

E_{1}\varphi=\varphi
on
\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash Y_{0}
and
E_{2}w=w
on
(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash Y_{0})\times I
and

\|E_{1}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})},\quad\|E_{1}\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})}\qquad\forall\varphi\in H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}),

\displaystyle\|E_{2}w\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})\times I)}\leq C\|w\|_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}))\times I)},

(144)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}E_{2}w\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\|\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}w\|_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}))\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\qquad\forall w\in H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I),

for some
C>0
. Indeed,
E_{1}
is constructed by using the standard reflection principle. Also using the reflection principle, we first construct
\tilde{E}_{2}:H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\to H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{E}_{2}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})},\quad\ \|\tilde{E}_{2}\varphi\|_{H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})}\qquad\forall\varphi\in H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}),

for some
C>0
.
On the basis of
\tilde{E}_{2},
we construct
E_{2}
as follows. For
w\in C^{2}(\overline{\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I})
we set
E_{2}w(\cdot,x_{3})=\tilde{E}_{2}w(\cdot,x_{3})
for all
x_{3}\in I.
It is easy to check that for
w\in C^{2}(\overline{\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I})
one has

\partial_{x_{3}}E_{2}w=E_{2}(\partial_{x_{3}}w)
, from which we infer the property (
144
) for
w\in C^{2}(\overline{\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I})
. We then extend
E_{2}
to the whole of
H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I)
by density, which concludes the construction.

For
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0}\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3}),
using the expression (
142
),
we define
\tilde{E}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3})
as follows:

\tilde{E}^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{B}(x_{1},x_{2},\varepsilon_{h}^{-1}hx_{3})^{\top}+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}E_{1}v\\[2.5pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}E_{1}v\\[2.5pt]
\varepsilon_{h}h^{-1}E_{1}v\end{array}\right)+E_{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}.

(145)

Recalling (
143
), we obtain the estimate

\|E_{1}v\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0})}+\|E_{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}E_{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\tilde{Y}_{0};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|\sym\nabla_{h/\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{\psi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}((\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\overline{Y}_{0})\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

We construct
E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}
by considering
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
\varepsilon_{h}(Y+z)\subset\omega
and applying the extension
\tilde{E}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
to the function
x\mapsto{\boldsymbol{u}}({\varepsilon_{h}}\hat{x}+{\varepsilon_{h}}z,x_{3})
. In this way we obtain

E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}\big|_{\varepsilon_{h}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z)\times I}=\boldsymbol{a}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{z}+\boldsymbol{B}^{\varepsilon_{h}}_{z}\left(x_{1},x_{2},hx_{3}\right)^{\top}+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-x_{3}\partial_{1}v_{z}\\[2.5pt]
-x_{3}\partial_{2}v_{z}\\[2.5pt]
h^{-1}v_{z}\end{array}\right)+\boldsymbol{\psi}_{z},

(146)

with the estimate

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{h}^{-4}\|v_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z))}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon_{h}^{-2}\|\nabla v_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z);\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\bigl\|\nabla^{2}v_{z}\bigr\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z);\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})}

\displaystyle+{{\varepsilon_{h}}}^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z)\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\nabla_{h}{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z)\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\leq C\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\varepsilon_{h}}(\tilde{Y}_{0}\backslash\bar{Y}_{0}+z)\times I;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

This concludes the proof of (
139
).
To prove (
140
), for each
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
consider the deformation

\boldsymbol{u}-E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}|_{\varepsilon(\tilde{Y}_{0}+z)\times I}

and apply the above rescaling as well as the second claim of Lemma
A.10
. The linearity of
E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}
follows from the decompositions in Theorem
A.3
and Lemma
A.10
.
∎

Remark A.25
.

Notice that as a consequence of
Corollary
A.5
and the estimate (
139
), if
\boldsymbol{u}=0
on
\gamma_{\rm D}\times I
, where
\gamma_{\rm D}\subset\partial\omega
is the set of positive measure, then

\displaystyle\left\|\pi_{1/h}(E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u})\right\|_{H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|\pi_{1/h}\boldsymbol{u})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{D};\mathbb{R}^{3})}+h^{-2}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}},\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}^{2}\Bigr),

where
C
is obtained by combining
C_{\rm T}^{\gamma}
in (
128
) and the constant in the extension inequality (
139
).

Remark A.26
.

We can assume, without loss of generality, that in the above proof
E_{1}
maps affine expressions
a_{1}x_{1}+a_{2}x_{2}+a_{3}
, for
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{R},
to themselves. (Indeed, as in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, on the orthogonal complement of affine maps in
L^{2}
the extension is constructed by reflection.)
From (
146
), recalling (
142
) and (
145
), we also have the estimate

\|E^{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}};\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right),

for some
C>0
. From (
140
), (
145
) we then additionally obtain that

{\varepsilon_{h}}h^{-1}\|\mathring{v}\|_{L^{2}(\omega)}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\right).

Remark A.27
.

Following Remark
A.6
and Remark
A.11
we infer that if
u_{\alpha}
is even in the variable
x_{3}
for
\alpha=1,2
and
u_{3}
is odd in the variable
x_{3},
the extension
E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u}
has the same properties.
Noting that by Lemma
A.10
and Theorem
A.3

one has
a_{z,3}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=B_{z,13}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=B_{z,23}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=v_{z}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=\mathring{v}^{\varepsilon_{h}}=0
in (
146
), we also infer that

\displaystyle\bigl\|(E^{{\varepsilon_{h}}}\boldsymbol{u})_{3}\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Ch\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{{\varepsilon_{h}}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{\psi}}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C{\varepsilon_{h}}^{-1}h{\varepsilon_{h}}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}=Ch\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

for some
C>0
.

A.3.3
Asymptotic regime
\varepsilon_{h}\ll h

In this regime the extension theorem is analogous to Theorem
A.19
.

Theorem A.28
.

For every
\varepsilon_{h}>0
there exists a linear extension operator
u\mapsto\tilde{u}:H^{1}(\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}};\mathbb{R}^{3})\to H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}
on
\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}
and

\displaystyle\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{u}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}.

Proof.

Since
{\varepsilon_{h}}\ll h,
the way in which we introduce the extensions here is to look at every single cell inside the thin domain
\Omega^{h}
.
The cells are
\varepsilon_{h}
-cubes

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y+z)\times[k{\varepsilon_{h}},(k+1){\varepsilon_{h}}],

where
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z},
are chosen so that each cube is entirely inside
\Omega
. We use the extension operator on the cube
Y\times I
(see
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
), followed by a scaling argument.
We label the resulting extension by
E_{1}.

The problem is that we can have a mismatch at the lines
x_{3}=k{\varepsilon_{h}}
, where
k\in\mathbb{Z},
and there are possible “boundary layers” at the sides
x_{3}=\pm h/2
where the extension is not defined (due to the fact that
h/{\varepsilon_{h}}
is not necessarily an integer). We deal with this by introducing another series of extensions to cubes

\varepsilon_{h}(Y+z)\times\biggl[\biggl(k+\frac{1}{2}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}},\biggl(k+\frac{3}{2}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr],\qquad z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

from the complements of the corresponding “perforations”

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times\biggl[\biggl(k+\frac{3}{4}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}},\biggl(k+\frac{5}{4}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr].

(On the parts

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times\biggl[\biggl(k+\frac{1}{2}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}},\biggl(k+\frac{3}{4}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr],\quad{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times\biggl[\biggl(k+\frac{5}{4}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}},\biggl(k+\frac{3}{2}\biggr){\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]

we continue using the extension
E_{1}.
) In this way we correct the first extension and eliminate the mismatch.
We denote the resulting extension by
E_{2}
.
We deal with the upper layers at
x_{3}=\pm h/2
in a different way, namely, we first consider the extension on the cubes

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2}\,-\frac{h}{2}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

(referring to this as
E_{3}
), and then we correct the possible mismatch between
E_{2}
and
E_{3}
by performing extensions to the cubes

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

from the complements of the corresponding perforations

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{5}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{5}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

(On the part

{\varepsilon}(Y_{0}+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}-\frac{5}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{5}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

we take the above extension
E_{2}
, while on the part

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{4}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

we take the extension
E_{3}
.)
We refer to this extension on the cube

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y+z)\times\left(\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\right)

as
E_{4}
.
The final extension is given
by
E_{4}
on the layer

\Biggl\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\Omega^{h}:x_{3}\in\biggl[\frac{h}{2}-\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}},\frac{h}{2}\biggr]\cup\biggl[-\frac{h}{2},-\frac{h}{2}+\frac{3}{2}{\varepsilon_{h}}\biggr]\Biggr\}

and by
E_{2}
on the remaining part of
\Omega^{h}
. The required extension on
\Omega
is now then by scaling in
x_{3}.
(A procedure analogous to this has been described in
[
28
, Chapter 4]
for some specific domains.)
∎

Remark A.29
.

It is easy to see that if
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2,\rm bend}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
or
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2,\rm memb}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
then the same is valid for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}
(see the extension operator in
[
28
, Lemma 4.1]
).

The following statement is proved analogously to Theorem
A.28
, see also the proof of Lemma
A.22
.

Lemma A.30
.

For
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in V(\Omega)
be such that
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon_{h}}|_{\Omega_{1}^{\varepsilon_{h}}}=0,

the following Poincaré and Korn inequalities hold:

\left\|\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq{\varepsilon_{h}}C\left\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}},\qquad\qquad\left\|\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{L^{2}},

(147)

where the constant
C
does not depend on
h.

Proof.

As in the case of Theorem
A.28
, we work on the physical domain
\Omega^{h}
.
The restrictions of the inequalities (
147
) to those cylinders

{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times[-h/2+k{\varepsilon_{h}},-h/2+(k+1){\varepsilon_{h}}]
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0},
that are contained in
\Omega
are obtained by combining a scaling argument, the Korn inequality, and the Poincare inequality on the unit cube.
We similarly obtain the restrictions of the inequalities (
147
) to the cylinders
{\varepsilon_{h}}(Y_{0}+z)\times[h/2-{\varepsilon_{h}},h/2]
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The argument for the physical domain
\Omega^{h}
is now completed by summing up all above inequalities. To obtain the inequality on the canonical domain
\Omega
(where gradients are replaced by scaled gradients), we simply perform the corresponding rescaling.
∎

A.4
Hyperbolic evolution problems of second order

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a non-negative, self-adjoint operator with domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
in a separable Hilbert space
H,
and let
\mathcal{A}^{1/2}
its unique non-negative self-adjoint root in
H
. We define the following norm on
V:=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2})
:

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{V}:=\bigl((\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u},(\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}\bigr)_{H},\quad\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}).

V
is a Hilbert space
and the inclusion
V\geq\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is dense.
Let
V^{*}
be the dual of
V
making
(V,H,V^{*})
the Gelfand triple
[
4
,
21
]
.
Due to the density argument, the operators
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{1/2}
can be uniquely extended to bounded linear operators:

\mathcal{A}:V\to V^{*},\quad\mathcal{A}^{1/2}:H\to V^{*}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I}:V\to V^{*}
as well as
(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2},

\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+\mathcal{I},
viewed both as operators from
V
to
H
and form
H
to
V^{*},
are isomorphisms.
Consider also the following evolution problem:

\begin{split}&\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u}(t)=\boldsymbol{f}(t),\\[1.99997pt]
&\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\\[1.00006pt]
&\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H,\quad\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*}).\end{split}

(148)

Definition A.31
.

We say that
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];V)
is a weak solution of the problem (
148
) if it satisfies:

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];V),\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];H),\\[3.50006pt]
&\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v})_{H}+a(\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v})=(\boldsymbol{f}(t),\boldsymbol{v})_{V^{*},V}\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in V\mbox{ in the sense of distributions on }(0,T),\\[3.50006pt]
&\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}.\end{split}

(149)

The problem (
148
) can be restated as a first-order form, as follows. Consider the product space
E=V\times H
endowed with the inner product

(\vec{\boldsymbol{v}},\vec{\boldsymbol{w}})_{E}=\bigl((\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2})^{\top},(\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\boldsymbol{w}_{2})^{\top}\bigr)_{E}:=\bigl((\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{1},(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\bigr)_{H}+(\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{w}_{2})_{H},

and set

\mathbb{A}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\mathcal{I}\\[1.99997pt]
\mathcal{A}&0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{A})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\times\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}),

(150)

with the embedding
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{\mathbb{A}})\hookrightarrow E
being dense. It is easily seen that

((\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})\vec{\boldsymbol{v}},\vec{\boldsymbol{v}})_{E}=(\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1})+(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1})+(\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{v}_{2})-(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\geq((\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{1})+(\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{v}_{2}))/2,

(151)

where by
\mathbb{I}
we have denoted the identity operator on
E
.
Moreover for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
one has

(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})\vec{\boldsymbol{v}}=\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}+\lambda^{2}\mathcal{I})^{-1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{f}_{1}+\boldsymbol{f}_{2}),\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{2}=\lambda\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{f}_{1},

(152)

which implies

(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda(\mathcal{A}+\lambda^{2}\mathcal{I})^{-1}&(\mathcal{A}+\lambda^{2}\mathcal{I})^{-1}\\[3.50006pt]
\lambda^{2}(\mathcal{A}+\lambda^{2}\mathcal{I})^{-1}-\mathcal{I}&\lambda(\mathcal{A}+\lambda^{2}\mathcal{I})^{-1}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\lambda\neq 0.

(153)

As a consequence of (
151
) we can conclude that
-(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})
is a dissipative operator in the sense that

\bigl\|(\lambda\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{A})\vec{\boldsymbol{v}}\bigr\|_{E}\geq\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+\lambda}\|\vec{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{E}\geq\lambda\|\vec{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{E},\qquad\forall\lambda>0.

(154)

The property (
152
) implies that
\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I}
is a closed operator. (Note that a dissipative operator
\mathcal{S}
is closed if there exists
\lambda>0
such that the range of
\lambda\mathcal{I}-\mathcal{S}
is closed.)
From (
152
) and (
154
) we conclude that
(\lambda+1)\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{A}
is a bijection

\bigl\|((\lambda+1)\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{A})^{-1}\bigr\|\leq\lambda^{-1},\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\forall\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda>0.

It follows that
-(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})
generates a contraction semigroup (by Hille-Yosida Theorem) and

e^{-t\mathbb{A}}=e^{t}e^{-t(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})},\quad\bigl\|e^{-t(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})}\bigr\|\leq 1,\quad\bigl\|e^{-t\mathbb{A}}\bigr\|\leq e^{t}.

(155)

The problem (
148
) can be formally written in the form

\partial_{t}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}(t)+\mathbb{A}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}(t)=\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(t),\qquad\ \ \vec{\boldsymbol{u}}(0)=\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0},

(156)

with
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}=(\boldsymbol{u},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}:=(\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{1})^{\top}
,
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}=(0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}\in E
.
The following two theorems establish sufficient conditions for the problem (
148
) to be well posed.

Theorem A.32
.

Under the additional assumption
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
, there exists a unique weak solution of the problem (
148
), understood in the sense of Definition
A.31
.

Proof.

The existence can be obtained by the variation of constants formula

\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}(t)=e^{-t\mathbb{A}}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{T}e^{-(t-s)\mathbb{A}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(s)ds.

(157)

The uniqueness is given by parabolic regularisation and can be found in
[
21
, Theorem 3, p. 572]
in a more general setting, where also a proof of existence is obtained by the same method while working directly with the problem (
148
).
∎

Remark A.33
.

It can be easily seen from (
149
) that in Theorem
A.32
one additionally has
\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*}).
Furthermore, if
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];H)
then
\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];V^{*})
.

Remark A.34
.

It follows from (
155
) and (
157
) that there exists
C>0
such that

\|\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];E)}\leq Ce^{T}\left(\|\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}\|+\|\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];E)}\right),

from which one directly concludes that

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];V)}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H)}\leq Ce^{T}\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{V}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{H}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];H)}\right).

(158)

Note that as a consequence of (
152
) the operator
\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I}
has bounded inverse for every
\lambda\neq 0
.

Theorem A.35
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{f},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*})
Then, there exists a unique weak solution in the sense of Definition
A.31
of the problem (
148
).

Proof.

Notice that we actually have
\boldsymbol{f}\in C([0,T];V^{*})
.
The existence of solution follows from the formula

\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}(t)=e^{-t\mathbb{A}}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}-\int_{0}^{T}e^{(s-t)\mathbb{A}}(\mathbb{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1}\Bigl(\partial_{s}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(s)-\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(s)\Bigr)ds+(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(t)-e^{-t\mathbb{A}}(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(0),

(159)

which can be obtained formally from (
157
) by using integration by parts. Here we also use the fact that (see (
153
)):

(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})^{-1}(0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}=\bigl((\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{f},(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\bigr),

(160)

from which it follows that if
f,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*})
then
(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})^{-1}(0,\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}
, i.e.,
(\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{I})^{-1}(0,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f})^{\top}
is an element of
L^{2}([0,T];V)
.
As in the proof of Theorem
A.32
, the uniqueness follows from
[
21
, Theorem 3,
p. 572]
. (Notice that considering the difference of the solutions associated with two different load densities gives us a solution to the problem with zero load density, which is then necessarily zero by the cited theorem.)
∎

Remark A.36
.

It can be easily seen that in Theorem
A.35
one additionally has
\partial_{tt}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];V^{*})
. This follows from (
149
) and the fact that
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];V^{*})
.

Remark A.37
.

It follows from (
155
) and (
159
) that there exists
C>0
such that

\|\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];E)}\leq Ce^{T}\left(\|\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0}\|+\|\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}(0)\|_{V^{*}}+\|\partial_{t}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];V^{*})}\right),

from which one directly concludes that

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];V)}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H)}\leq Ce^{T}\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{V}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{H}+\|\boldsymbol{f}(0)\|_{V^{*}}+\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{1}([0,T];V^{*})}\right).

(161)

We will now give an overview of the results of
[
30
]
, which we will then extend with the concept of solution discussed in Theorem
A.35
. While this extension is not considered in
[
30
]
, its validity follows from the formula (
159
).

Suppose that we are given a sequence of Hilbert spaces
(H_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
endowed with norms
||\cdot||_{H_{\varepsilon}}
and some type of weak convergence
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\in H
of sequences
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})\subset H_{\varepsilon}
. Our assumption on this type of weak convergence is that every weakly convergent sequence
({\boldsymbol{u}}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded, i.e.,
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}||\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}||_{H_{\varepsilon}}<\infty.
We additionally assume that the “limit space”
H
is separable.

Definition A.38
.

We say that a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\subset H_{\varepsilon}
strongly converges to
\boldsymbol{u}\in H
and write
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}
if
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{u}
and

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon})_{H_{\varepsilon}}=(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})_{H},

(162)

for every weakly convergent sequence
\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{v}\in H
,
\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon}
.

Additionally, we assume the following properties of this abstract weak convergence:

(compactness principle)

Every bounded sequence contains a weakly convergent subsequence.

(approximation principle)

For every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H
, there exists a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\subset H_{\varepsilon}
, such that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}
.

(norm convergence)

\displaystyle\mbox{If }\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\mbox{, then }\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{H_{{\varepsilon}}}=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H}.

As a consequence of these properties, to guarantee strong convergence
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}
it suffices to have the property (
162
) or, alternatively, to have
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{u}
and norm convergence (see the proof of Lemma
[
30
, Lemma 6.3]
).
The following kind of operator convergence is convenient in the analysis of parameter-dependent problems.

Definition A.39
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of non-negative self-adjoint operators acting on the respective spaces
H_{\varepsilon}.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator on some closed subspace
H_{0}
of
H
, and consider the orthogonal projection
P:H\to H_{0}.
We say that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
converge to
\mathcal{A}
in the weak resolvent sense if

\forall\lambda>0,\quad\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}P\boldsymbol{f},\quad\forall(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0},\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon},\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f}\in H.

(163)

Similarly, we say that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
converge to
\mathcal{A}
in the strong resolvent sense if

\forall\lambda>0,\quad\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)^{-1}P\boldsymbol{f},\quad\forall(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0},\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon},\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f}\in H.

(164)

Lemma A.40
.

The convergence (
163
) is equivalent to the convergence (
164
).

Proof.

The proof is based on a duality argument. Take
\lambda>0
and consider
(\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
such that
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f}
and
(\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
such that
\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{g}
. Then one has

\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon},\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon}}\right)_{H_{{\varepsilon}}}=\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\left(\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{g}^{{\varepsilon}}\right)_{H_{{\varepsilon}}}=\left(\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda I\right)^{-1}P\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{g}\right)_{H}=\left(\boldsymbol{f},\left(\mathcal{A}+\lambda\mathcal{I}\right)^{-1}P\boldsymbol{g}\right)_{H}.

These equalities show that (
163
) implies (
164
). In a similar fashion, one shows that (
164
) implies (
163
).
∎

Henceforth we work within the framework of Definition
A.39
.
For the sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
we construct the associated Hilbert spaces
V_{\varepsilon}
endowed with norms
||\cdot||_{V_{\varepsilon}},
defined as follows:

\|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{V_{\varepsilon}}:=((\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon},(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{I})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{H_{\varepsilon}}.

Similarly, we define
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{V}
, where
V=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2})
. It is easily seen that, since
H
is separable, the spaces
H_{0}
and
V
are also separable. (Indeed,
H_{0}
is a subspace of
H,
and if
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a dense subset of
H
, then
\{(\mathcal{A}+I)^{-1/2}h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a dense subset of
V.
)

Definition A.41
.

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\subset V_{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{u}\in V
. We say that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}
converge weakly to
\boldsymbol{u}
in
V_{\varepsilon},
and write
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u},
if

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\limsup_{\varepsilon>0}||\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}||_{V_{\varepsilon}}<\infty.

(165)

Additionally, we say that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
converges strongly to
\boldsymbol{u}
in
V_{\varepsilon},
and write
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u},
if

(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon})_{V_{\varepsilon}}\to(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})_{V}\quad\mbox{ for all }\quad\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{v}.

The following statement is
[
30
, Lemma 6.2, Lemma 6.3]
.

Lemma A.42
.

If
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}
, then
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}
. Moreover, one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\ \textrm{ and }\ \|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{V_{{\varepsilon}}}\to\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{V}.

It can be shown that there exists a dense subset
S\subset V
, such that for every
\boldsymbol{z}\in S
there exists a subsequence
(\boldsymbol{z}^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
such that
\boldsymbol{z}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{V_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{z}
(see
[
30
, Lemma 6.5]
).

We also introduce convergence notions convenient for the analysis of time-dependent problems.

Definition A.43
.

Suppose that a sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\subset L^{2}([0,T];H_{\varepsilon})
is bounded. We say that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
weakly converges to
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];H)
, and write
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u},
if

\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon})_{H_{\varepsilon}}\varphi(t)dt\to\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v})\varphi(t)dt,

for all
\boldsymbol{v}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{v}
and
\varphi\in L^{2}(0,T)
.

Definition A.44
.

Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}\subset L^{2}([0,T];V_{\varepsilon})
. We say that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
weakly converges to
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];V),
and write
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u},
if

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon},\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{H_{\varepsilon}}dt<\infty.

In the same way we can define the weak convergence in
L^{p}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon}})
and
L^{p}([0,T];V_{{\varepsilon}})
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
(which denote by
\xrightharpoonup{t,p,H_{{\varepsilon}}}
and
\xrightharpoonup{t,p,V_{{\varepsilon}}}
, respectively.)
The following lemma is stated in
[
30
, Lemma 4.7]
.

Lemma A.45
.

The spaces
L^{2}([0,T];H_{\varepsilon})
and
L^{2}([0,T];V_{\varepsilon})
satisfy the weak compactness principle.

The next lemma can also be easily established, see
[
30
, Lemma 2.3, Lemma 4.5]
.

Lemma A.46
.

If
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{H_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{u}
, then
\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{H_{{\varepsilon}}}\geq\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H}
. The same is valid for the weak convergence in
V_{{\varepsilon}}
. If
\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{u}
, then
\liminf_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon}})}\geq\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H)}
. The same is valid for the weak convergence in
L^{2}([0,T];V_{{\varepsilon}})
.

In the natural way, by components, we define the weak and strong convergence in
E_{\varepsilon}=V_{\varepsilon}\times H_{\varepsilon}
as well as the weak convergence in
L^{2}([0,T];E_{\varepsilon})
. Also the space
E_{0}=V\times H_{0}
and the projection
P
onto
E_{0}
are defined in a natural way, the latter being given by
P\vec{\boldsymbol{v}}=(v_{1},Pv_{2})^{\top}
. In an obvious way we also define operators
\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}
and
\mathbb{A}
.
The following theorem is a basic tool for proving weak or strong convergence of solutions to
\varepsilon
-parametrised evolution problems, understood in the sense of Definition
A.31
. The theorem can be found in
[
30
, Theorem 5.2, Theorem 7.1]
. The first part is easily proved by combining the Laplace transform and a compactness result as in Theorem
A.49
.

Theorem A.47
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of non-negative self-adjoint operators in
H_{\varepsilon}
that converge to a non-negative self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}
in some subspace
H_{0}\leq H
in the sense of weak resolvent convergence.

1.

If
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}
, then

(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}},\quad\forall\lambda>1.

2.

If
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}
, then
e^{-t\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{t,E_{{\varepsilon}}}e^{-t\mathbb{A}}P\boldsymbol{f}
for every
T>0
. If
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\in E_{0}
, then
e^{-t\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\to e^{-t\mathbb{A}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}
for every
t\geq 0
.

Remark A.48
.

The pointwise convergence
e^{-t\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{E_{{\varepsilon}}}e^{-t\mathbb{A}}P\boldsymbol{f}
in Theorem
A.47
does not necessarily hold if we only assume that
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightarrow{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\in E,
see
[
30
, p. 2267]
.

A version of the following theorem can be found in
[
30
, Theorem 5.2, Theorem 5.3]
.

Theorem A.49
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of non-negative self-adjoint operators in
H_{\varepsilon}
that converge to a non-negative self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}
in some subspace
H_{0}\leq H
in the sense of weak resolvent convergence. Let
T>0
and
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of weak solutions of the evolution problems (
148
) where
{\mathcal{A}}
is replaced by
{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}},
with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in V_{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon}
and right-hand sides
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{\varepsilon})
such that

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H,\quad\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H).

(166)

Then one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];V),\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{0}),

(167)

where
\boldsymbol{u}
is the weak solution of the evolution problem (
148
) for the limit operator
\mathcal{A}
, with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V
,
P\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0}
and the right-hand side
P\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{0})
.

Proof.

We write the problem (
148
) in the form (
156
). As a consequence of (
166
), (
158
), and Lemma
A.45
, there exist
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l}=(\boldsymbol{u}_{l},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{l})\in L^{2}([0,T];E_{0})
such that the convergence (
167
) holds.
Due to above mentioned bounds and weak convergence, we have

\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}})(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l})(\lambda),\qquad\lambda>1,

(168)

where
\mathcal{L}
denotes the Laplace transform (where extend
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}
and
\boldsymbol{f}
by zero on
(T,\infty)
).
On the one hand, the Laplace transform
\mathcal{L}
of the solution of the

{\varepsilon}
-parametrised equation is then given by

\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}})(\lambda)=(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}})(\lambda)+(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}}_{0},\qquad\lambda>1.

(169)

On the other hand, the Laplace transform of the solution of the limit problem is given by

\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}})(\lambda)=(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\mathcal{L}(P\vec{\boldsymbol{f}})(\lambda)+(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}P\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{0},\qquad\lambda>1.

(170)

Using (
153
), we infer that for every sequence
(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}})_{{\varepsilon}>0}\subset E_{{\varepsilon}}
such that
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}\in E
, and every
\lambda\neq 0
we have (see (
163
))

(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathbb{A}+\lambda\mathbb{I})^{-1}P\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}.

Using (
168
), (
169
), (
170
), and the fact that for
\lambda>1
one has
\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}^{{\varepsilon}})(\lambda)\xrightharpoonup{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}})(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{L}(P\vec{\boldsymbol{f}})(\lambda)=P\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{f}})(\lambda),
we infer that for every
\lambda>1
one has
\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}_{l})=\mathcal{L}(\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}),
and the claim follows.
∎

We proceed to the strong convergence analogue of Theorem
A.49
, see
[
30
, Theorem 7.2]
.

Theorem A.50
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})
be a sequence of non-negative self-adjoint operators in
H_{\varepsilon}
that converges to a non-negative self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}
in some subspace
H_{0}\leq H
in the sense of strong resolvent convergence. Let
T>0
and
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of weak solutions of the evolution problems (
148
) where
{\mathcal{A}}
is replaced by
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon},
with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in V_{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon}
and right-hand sides
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{\varepsilon})
such that

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0},\\
&\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\in H_{0}\quad\mbox{a.e. $t\in[0,T],$}\quad\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(s)\bigr\|^{2}_{H_{\varepsilon}}ds\to\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|\boldsymbol{f}(s)\bigr\|^{2}_{H}ds.\end{split}

(171)

Then for every
t\in[0,T]
one has

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in V,\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in H_{0},

(172)

where
\boldsymbol{u}
is the weak solution of the evolution problem of (
148
) for the operator
\mathcal{A}
, with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0}
and the right-hand side
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{0})
.

Proof.

Again we use (
156
), formula
(
157
) and Theorem
A.47
. The proof follows by using Lebesgue theorem on Dominated convergence and the fact that as a consequence of Lemma
A.46
we have
\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(s)\|^{2}_{H_{\varepsilon}}ds\to\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{f}(s)\|^{2}_{H}ds
, for every
t\leq T
.
∎

Theorem
A.50
can be generalized as follows.

Theorem A.51
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
be a sequence of non-negative self-adjoint operators in
H_{\varepsilon}
that converge to a non-negative self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{A}
in some subspace
H_{0}\leq H
in the sense of strong resolvent convergence. Let
T>0
and
(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of weak solutions of the evolution problems (
148
) where
{\mathcal{A}}
is replaced by
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon},
with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in V_{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\in H_{\varepsilon}
and right-hand sides
\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}([0,T];V_{\varepsilon}^{*})
,
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}([0,T];V_{\varepsilon}^{*})
such that the sequences
(\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
,
(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon})_{{\varepsilon}>0}
are bounded in
L^{2}([0,T];\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V^{*}_{{\varepsilon}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,V_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V),

where
\boldsymbol{f},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(O,T;V^{*})
.
Then one has

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];V),\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,H_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{2}([0,T];H_{0}),

where
\boldsymbol{u}
is the weak solution of the evolution problem (
148
) for the operator
\mathcal{A}
, with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V
,
P\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0}
and the right-hand side
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*})
.
Furthermore, if we assume that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0},

(173)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(0)\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}(0)\in V,

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}(t)\in V,\quad\mbox{ for a.e. $t\in[0,T]$},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|\left(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\partial_{s}\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}(s)\bigr\|_{V_{{\varepsilon}}}^{2}\,ds\to\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\|\left(\mathcal{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\partial_{s}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\bigr\|_{V}^{2}\,ds,

where
\boldsymbol{f},\ \partial_{t}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*}),

then we have

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in V,\quad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\xrightarrow{H_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\in H_{0},\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\forall t\in[0,T],\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

where
\boldsymbol{u}
is the weak solution of the evolution problem (
148
) for the operator
\mathcal{A}
, with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in V
,
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in H_{0}
and the right hand side
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}([0,T];V^{*})
.

Proof.

The argument follows the proofs of Theorem
A.49
and Theorem
A.50
, by using the formula (
159
) instead of (
157
) (see also (
160
)).

To prove the first part, notice that the Laplace transform of the solution
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}}^{{\varepsilon}}
, respectively
\vec{\boldsymbol{u}},
is given by formula (
169
), respectively (
170
). (Note that in (
170
) we replace
P\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}
with
\vec{\boldsymbol{f}}
.) This is established by a density argument, using the fact that
L^{2}([0,T];H_{{\varepsilon}})
is dense in
L^{2}([0,T];V^{*}_{{\varepsilon}})
, respectively that
L^{2}([0,T];H)
is dense in
L^{2}([0,T];V^{*})
.

To prove the second part, use the second part of Theorem
A.47
and notice that (
173
) implies that for all
t\in[0,T]
one has

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}(t)\xrightarrow{V_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{f}(t),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{(s-t)\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}}(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1}\bigl(0,\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}(s)\bigr)^{\top}ds\xrightarrow{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{(s-t)\mathbb{A}}(\mathbb{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1}\bigl(0,\boldsymbol{f}(s)\bigr)^{\top}ds,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{(s-t)\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}}(\mathbb{A}_{{\varepsilon}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1}\bigl(0,\partial_{s}\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}(s)\bigr)^{\top}ds\xrightarrow{E_{{\varepsilon}}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{(s-t)\mathbb{A}}(\mathbb{A}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{-1}\bigl(0,\partial_{s}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\bigr)^{\top}ds,

as a consequence of the dominated convergence theorem and Lemma
A.46
.
∎

Remark A.52
.

It is easy to see that in Theorem
A.49
, Theorem
A.50
, and Theorem
A.51
the sequences
(\|\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{V_{{\varepsilon}}})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
(\|\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{{\varepsilon}}\|_{H_{{\varepsilon}}})_{\varepsilon>0}
are bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T)
.

Remark A.53
.

The claims of Theorem
A.49
, Theorem
A.50
and Theorem
A.51
can be strengthened slightly. Namely, it suffices to require that
\boldsymbol{f}^{{\varepsilon}}\xrightharpoonup{t,1,H_{{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{1}\bigl([0,T];H\bigr)
in (
166
) to obtain the convergence

\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,\infty,V_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];V\bigr),\qquad\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{t,\infty,H_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];H_{0}\bigr).

Similarly, it suffices to require
\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{f}^{\varepsilon}\|_{H_{{\varepsilon}}}\,ds\to\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{f}(s)\|\,ds
in (
171
) to obtain (
172
). The statement of Theorem
A.51
can also be strengthened accordingly.

A.5
Additional claims

In the membrane space, under no additional assumptions on the symmetries of the set
Y_{0}
, the function
\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)
(see (
26
) as well as the parts A of Sections
3.2.2
,
3.2.3
) is a symmetric matrix that is not necessarily diagonal. We will first prove two lemmata and then a proposition concerning the problem (
46
), which involves the function
\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)
defined in Section
3.2
.

Lemma A.54
.

There exists
C>0
such that for every
\lambda>0
we have:

\left(\bigl(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\bigr)^{\prime}(\lambda)\xi,\xi\right)>C|\xi|^{2},\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\xi\neq 0.

Proof.

We will give the proof for
\delta\in(0,\infty),
and the other cases can be treated analogously. Notice that for the function
\lambda\mapsto{(\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda)}^{-1}{\lambda^{2}}
one has

\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda}\right)^{\prime}=-1+\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{n}^{2}}{(\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda)^{2}}.

It follows that

\begin{split}\bigl(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\bigr)^{\prime}(\lambda)&=\boldsymbol{I}_{2\times 2}\langle\rho\rangle+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{n}^{2}}{(\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda)^{2}}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle\cdot\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle^{\top}-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle\cdot\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle^{\top}\\[3.99994pt]
&=\boldsymbol{I}_{2\times 2}\langle\rho_{1}\rangle+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{n}^{2}}{(\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda)^{2}}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle\cdot\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle^{\top}.\end{split}

Here
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n},

n\in{\mathbb{N}},
are those eigenfunctions of the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{00,\delta}
associated with the eigenvalues
\tilde{\eta}_{n},

n\in{\mathbb{N}},
that satisfy

\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigl\rangle\neq 0,

and we have used the identity

\boldsymbol{I}_{2\times 2}\langle\rho_{0}\rangle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle\cdot\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle^{\top},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,2.

The proof of the required estimate is concluded by noting that for
\xi\neq 0
one has

\left(\bigl(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}\bigr)^{\prime}(\lambda)\xi,\xi\right)=\langle\rho_{1}\rangle|\xi|^{2}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{n}^{2}}{(\tilde{\eta}_{n}-\lambda)^{2}}\left(\bigl\langle\rho_{0}\overline{(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}_{n})_{*}}\bigr\rangle,\xi\right)^{2}>\langle\rho_{1}\rangle|\xi|^{2}.

∎

Lemma A.55
.

Let
\lambda_{0}>0
be such that there exists a nontrivial solution
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the problem (
46
). Then there exists
\eta>0
such that for each
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\eta)
the problem (
46
) has only the trivial solution.

Proof.

The problem (
46
) can be reformulated as follows:

\begin{split}\left((\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right)=\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),\end{split}

where
(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}
is a self-adjoint positive square root of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb},
which has compact inverse. Thus, the above problem can be rewritten as

\left((\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},(\mathcal{A}^{\rm memb}_{\delta})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right)=\left((\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}},(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\varphi}\right),\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}).

By substituting
\boldsymbol{v}=(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{a}}
, we have reduced the problem (
46
) to the following equivalent problem: find
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
that is an eigenfunction for
(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}
with eigenvalue
\mu^{\lambda}=1
, i.e.,

(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}.

The operator
(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}
is compact and its positive eigenvalues, in decreasing order, are characterised by the variational principle

\mu_{k}^{\lambda}=\max_{V<L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}),\ \dim V=k}\ \ \min_{x\in V,\ ||x||=1}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\rm memb}^{-1/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{A}_{\rm memb}^{-1/2}x,x\right),\qquad k=1,2,\dots.

Denote by
k_{1}
the index of the eigenvalue
1=\mu_{k_{1}}^{\lambda_{0}},
which can clearly be done due to the assumption on
\lambda_{0}
. Next, denote by
k_{2}
the index of the next smaller eigenvalue
\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda_{0}}<1
.
Furthermore, notice that for
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
one has

\begin{split}(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}&=(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda_{0})(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}\\[3.50006pt]
&+(\lambda-\lambda_{0})(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}(\tilde{\beta}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}^{\rm memb})^{-1/2}+O(|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{2}),\end{split}

where
\|O(|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{2}\|\leq C|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{2}
for some
C>0
.
For this reason, by virtue of Lemma
A.54
, one has
\mu_{k_{1}}^{\lambda}>1
, …,
\mu_{k_{2}-1}^{\lambda}>1
,
\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda}>\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda_{0}}
whenever
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\eta)
, for some
\eta>0
. Due to the continuity of
\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda}
with respect to
\lambda
, we can redefine
\eta>0
so that for every
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\eta)
we have
\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda_{0}}<\mu_{k_{2}}^{\lambda}<1
. Thus, for
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\eta)
, unity is not the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{A}_{\rm memb}^{-1/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{\beta}^{\rm memb}(\lambda)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{A}_{\rm memb}^{-1/2}
.
∎

Proposition A.56
.

The set of all
\lambda>0
for which the problem (
46
) has a nontrivial solution
\mathfrak{a}\in H^{1}_{\gamma_{D}}(\omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
is at most countable.

Proof.

Using the preceding lemma, we can define the following family of disjoint intervals:

\mathcal{F}=\{[\lambda,\lambda+\eta_{\lambda})\subset\mathbb{R},\quad\mbox{ $\lambda>0$ is such that the problem \eqref{generalizedeigenvaluemembrane} has a nontrivial solution}\},

where
\eta_{\lambda}
is provided by Lemma
A.55
.
Such a family can be at most countable, from which the claim follows.
∎

Finally, we prove a lemma whose variants we used on several occasions within Section
4.3
. We begin by introducing some notation. We define the following norm and seminorm on
H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}):

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{s,h,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\|\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\|\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})},

where we have for every
\boldsymbol{u}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}
a decomposition
\boldsymbol{u}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
is employed, with both
\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}
and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}
depending on
\boldsymbol{u}
in a linear manner. We also assume that
\mathcal{A}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator whose domain is a subset of
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

c_{1}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{s,h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}\leq(\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})\leq c_{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{s,h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}\quad\forall{\boldsymbol{u}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}).

(174)

Lemma A.57
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is as above and let
\lambda\notin\sigma(\mathcal{A})
. Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{u}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
satisfies

\bigl(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{u},\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\bigr)-\lambda(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\Omega}\left(\boldsymbol{f}_{1}:\sym\nabla_{h}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}+{\varepsilon_{h}}\boldsymbol{f}_{2}:\sym\nabla_{h}\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}+\boldsymbol{f}_{3}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)\,dx\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\xi}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}+\mathring{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

where
\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
and
\boldsymbol{f}_{3}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Then one has

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{h,{\varepsilon_{h}}}\leq\frac{C(\lambda)}{\dist\bigl(\lambda,\sigma(\mathcal{A})\bigr)}\left(\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\right),

for some
C(\lambda)
that is bounded on bounded intervals.

Proof.

By virtue of the Riesz representation theorem and (
174
), we know that there exists
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
C>0,
which depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
only, such that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{f}_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}+\|\boldsymbol{f}_{3}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})}\right),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi})-\lambda(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\xi})=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\bigl(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\bigr)\quad\ \forall\boldsymbol{\xi}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{\rm D}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}).

(175)

We can now use the spectral theorem (see, e.g.,
[
32
]
): there exists a measurable space
(M,\mu)
with a finite measure
\mu
and a unitary operator

\mathcal{U}:L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\to L^{2}(M)

and a non-negative real-valued function
a,
which is an element of
L^{p}(M)
,
p\in[1,\infty)
,
such that

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if
a(\cdot)\mathcal{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)\in L^{2}(M)
;

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\mathcal{U}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{U}^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)=a(\cdot)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot).

Notice that the second claim implies

•

\mathcal{U}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\mathcal{U}^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)=\sqrt{a(\cdot)}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{\psi}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot).

•

\sigma(\mathcal{A})={\rm EssRan}\,a:=\bigl\{r:\forall\varepsilon>0,\ \mu\{m\in M:r-\varepsilon\leq a(m)\leq r+\varepsilon\}>0\bigr\}.

Furthermore, (
175
) implies

\bigl(a(\cdot)-\lambda\bigr)\mathcal{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)=\mathcal{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)\bigl(\sqrt{a(\cdot)}+1\bigr),

from which, by virtue of

\lambda\notin{\rm EssRan}\,a,

one has

\bigl(\sqrt{a(\cdot)}+1\bigr)\mathcal{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)=\mathcal{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\cdot)\frac{\bigl(\sqrt{a(\cdot)}+1\bigr)^{2}}{\bigl(a(\cdot)-\lambda\bigr)}.

Therefore, there exists
C(\lambda)>0,
which is uniformly bounded on compact intervals of
\lambda,
such that

\bigl\|(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\boldsymbol{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bigr\|_{L_{2}}\leq\frac{C(\lambda)}{\dist\bigl(\lambda,\sigma(\mathcal{A})\bigr)}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{L^{2}},

(176)

from which the claim follows immediately.
∎

Remark A.58
.

Throughout the paper, we also use some variants of the above lemma, see the discussions around (
94
), (
99
), (
107
), (
109
). They generically apply to setups that can be put the form (
175
), and they result in estimates of the type (
176
). The key ingredient for their validity is the fact that the right-hand side of the equation is in the dual of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{I}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
with respect to the graph norm.
